CityPaint: A Free Lunch from 2D Diffusion for City-Scale 3D Texturing

Generating high-fidelity, city-scale 3D environments plays a crucial role in applications such as virtual reality, gaming, and urban digital twins. However, the scarcity of real-world city-scale 3D training data forces existing methods to either stitch independently generated assets or learn from synthetic urban corpora, often sacrificing realism and global consistency. In contrast, 2D image data and their corresponding generative models are abundant and encode rich visual priors. To bridge this gap, we propose CityPaint, a novel framework that leverages 2D diffusion models to generate globally consistent city-scale 3D textures without relying on city-scale 3D training data. Specifically, CityPaint integrates geometric priors with the generative power of 2D diffusion models, introducing a robust top-$K$ camera baking strategy for iterative texture projection. To address the artifacts of 2D-to-3D projection, we further propose a decoupled refinement mechanism that separately optimizes terrain and building textures. Extensive experiments show that CityPaint produces globally coherent textures with fine local details and achieves favorable results against strong baselines in both visual quality and cross-view consistency.
---
AnchorRM: Parallel Intra-Batch State Reconstruction with Anchor-Based Residual Memory for Temporal Graph Neural Networks

Memory-based temporal graph neural networks (TGN) rely on recurrent node memories to capture evolving interaction histories. However, temporal mini-batching introduces a trade-off between computational efficiency and temporal fidelity: when a node appears multiple times within the same batch, later interactions are evaluated using stale pre-batch memory because the intermediate memory updates have not yet been committed. This problem becomes more severe as temporal batch size increases. To overcome this challenge, we propose AnchorRM, an anchor-based residual memory mechanism that reconstructs event-specific intra-batch node states without executing sequential recurrent updates. AnchorRM treats the last committed node memory at the beginning of each batch as a pre-batch fixed anchor and uses causally preceding same-batch interactions to infer the missing state evolution relative to that anchor. The learned residual then calibrates the anchor to reconstruct an event-specific node memory for each interaction. Because each event-specific residual is predicted directly from the fixed anchor and its causal history, reconstructed states do not depend on previously reconstructed states and can therefore be computed in parallel within the batch while preserving temporal causality. Experiments across four temporal graph datasets and three memory-based backbones demonstrate that AnchorRM achieves an average 6.16x training speedup (up to 10.05x) and 28.67x batch scaling (up to 40x). These results demonstrate that AnchorRM substantially improves the scalability and training efficiency of memory-based temporal graph learning while maintaining competitive predictive performance.

Can Native Safety Travel? Reusing Safety Pretraining Across Models via On-Policy Distillation

Safety pretraining has emerged as a promising paradigm for embedding safety deeply into a model during pretraining, yielding robustness to subsequent fine-tuning that conventional safety post-training often lacks. We refer to this safety acquired during model pretraining as native safety. Yet native safety is expensive to acquire, confined to the model in which it was pretrained, and often accompanied by lower general utility. In this work, we rethink the practical value of safety-pretrained models and introduce, for the first time, the native safety reuse problem: Can native safety be transferred from a specialized, potentially smaller, safety-pretrained "teacher" to standard-pretrained "students," enabling them to inherit its robustness while preserving their general utility? We show that native safety can "travel" through on-policy distillation. A standard-pretrained student generates responses to safety-pretraining prompts, while a small safety-pretrained teacher supervises the student's token-level predictions along its own generated trajectories; cross-tokenizer alignment further enables transfer across model families. The resulting students exhibit substantially greater safety robustness than their safety-post-trained counterparts while retaining higher general utility than the teacher: subsequent fine-tuning raises the average attack success rate (ASR) of the 1.7B student by only 5.00%, compared with 11.58% for safety post-training, and its average utility of 48.20% far exceeds the aligned teacher's 27.42%. Unlike conventional large-to-small knowledge distillation, our framework enables selective, small-to-large safety transfer: it reuses the robust safety expertise of a smaller pretrained teacher without inheriting its utility limitations. These findings establish native safety as a reusable model property rather than one permanently tied to the originally pretrained model.

Act on Your Behalf, Beyond Your Authorization: Benchmarking LLM Agent Authorization Alignment

When an LLM agent acts on behalf of a user, it is expected to act within the authorization scope the user grants. Existing agent evaluations largely measure task completion or harmful consequences and can therefore overlook actions that violate this delegation without producing conventional safety or privacy harms. We introduce AuthorizationBench, a benchmark of authorization alignment grounded in documented real-world cases. A scalable construction pipeline produces 286 executable tasks spanning situational triggers, the presence or absence of direct harmful consequences, and ten application domains. We execute every task with four models at two reasoning-effort settings across three agent harnesses in an authorization-aware sandbox that keeps authorized and out-of-authorization paths executable and observable, yielding 6,864 trajectories. Agents act out of authorization in 19.2% of trajectories, with rates ranging from 10.5% to 26.6% across the 24 configurations. Task completion does not imply authorization. Increasing reasoning effort leaves the aggregate rate nearly unchanged, with effects varying across models. On controlled pairs, agents act out of authorization at similar rates with (17.9%) and without (20.8%) direct harmful consequences, and more often under benign pressures than malicious injections (23.5% versus 17.6%, p=0.002). Our results show that task capability, additional reasoning, and consequence-oriented safety evaluation do not by themselves ensure authorization alignment. Moreover, agents that consult the principal before completing a task exhibit lower rates of out-of-authorization behavior (14.0% versus 21.6%, p<0.001), pointing to a tension between agent autonomy and preserving users' decision authority that calls for further human-centered study. Dataset and code are available at https://github.com/PEACH-Research-Lab/AuthorizationBench .

Less is More: Concise Retrieval and Critic-based Re-ranking for Composed Image Retrieval

Zero-shot composed image retrieval (ZS-CIR) aims to retrieve a target image based on a reference image and modification text. Recent approaches commonly rely on large vision-language models to generate target image captions for retrieval. However, these captions may include redundant semantic details, introducing noise when retrieving the target image and leading to suboptimal retrieval results. To address this issue, we propose Less-is-More CIR, a two-stage training-free framework that combines Concise Retrieval and Critic Re-ranking. In the first stage, concise target-oriented captions are generated that preserve only the most discriminative modification semantics, thereby reducing semantic noise in the retrieval. In the second stage, a critic re-rank module further evaluates the retrieved candidates from the perspectives of modification consistency and reference preservation. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed framework for the ZS-CIR. Our code is available at: https://github.com/dxlabskku/LM-CIR.

Adversarial Benchmarks and BenchDoor Attacks: Exploiting Benchmark Incentives for Backdoor Attacks

Machine learning benchmarks act as guideposts for model development and spur the creation of training datasets intended to improve benchmark scores. We introduce BenchDoor, a family of data poisoning attacks that exploits this incentive. Beyond preserving benign performance, BenchDoor requires benchmark gains that encourage adoption of data that also implants a backdoor. The adversary either manipulates a benchmark's training or test data, creating an adversarial benchmark, or supplies an independent training dataset while leaving the benchmark unchanged. We formalize five attack scenarios, including training on rewritten test examples and applying similarity-based decontamination. Across multiple-choice Q&A, text classification, and open-ended Q&A with Qwen and Gemma models, BenchDoor achieves positive benchmark gains for all 30 evaluated configurations, with held-out attack success rates of 82.33% to 100.00% at poisoning rates of 2% to 10%. Both objectives remain achievable after synthetic data generation and similarity-based decontamination, with attack success rates of at least 90.33%. These findings show how benchmarks can serve as attack vectors both through their own data and through training resources that deliver improvements on them.

Automated Multi-Agent Organization Design: From Architecture Search to Organizational Learning

Agentic system design is increasingly shifting from hand-crafted prompts and workflows toward automated search over agentic programs and interaction structures, with multi-agent systems receiving growing attention. Yet a central question remains unresolved: when does an automatically generated multi-agent structure exhibit a functionally meaningful division of responsibility? We argue that automated multi-agent design should move beyond searching for effective architectures toward identifying which responsibilities are functionally necessary, how they should be allocated, and when they should change. To explore this, we propose Automated Multi-Agent Organization Design (AMOD), which treats organization itself as a first-class design object, explicitly modeling how agent teams are composed, what functional responsibilities the agents assume, how they coordinate, and how collective decisions are made. We formulate AMOD as an execution-driven design loop. For each task, an automated designer instantiates an organization by assigning functional responsibilities and coordination rules to a set of agents. After execution, task outcomes, intermediate artifacts, and interaction traces are examined to identify which responsibilities may be missing, duplicated, or ineffective, and the organization is revised accordingly. We also propose testing discovered responsibilities by removing them, replacing them with generic roles, or changing their assignment under matched resource budgets, with complementary evaluations. AMOD provides a principled foundation for evidence-based and adaptive multi-agent organization design.

MemDev-Bench: Does Memory Help Code Agents?Benchmarking Memory Systems over Real Repository Histories

Code agents increasingly work on evolving real repositories, and memory systems aim to help them reuse earlier experience. However, such experience is diverse and evolving, so it remains unclear how memory systems organize it and thereby affect code agents. Existing benchmarks cannot answer this reliably because they lack either temporally valid histories or control over prior-task relevance. In this paper, we present MemDev-Bench to evaluate how memory systems affect code agents over diverse repository histories. It pairs each task with relevance-annotated earlier tasks from the same repository to build histories with controlled relevance. We also build an automated pipeline that reliably makes these earlier tasks executable and verifiable, producing 4,869 historical tasks for 555 base tasks. Based on MemDev-Bench, our systematic evaluation of memory systems across diverse development scenarios yields three main findings. (1) Memory mainly improves fine-grained localization and editing, increasing functional correctness. (2) Memory also causes failures on otherwise solvable tasks even with related history, and unrelated history tends to increase such failures. These failures mainly stem from agents reusing memory without reassessing its applicability. (3) Compared with using memory systems, directly providing agents with raw historical trajectories substantially reduces their steps. Further analysis suggests that agents save these steps by reusing process information that compressed memories tend to omit. These results suggest that future memory systems should preserve process information and reassess memory applicability against current task context.

When Health Assistants Agree Too Readily: A Benchmark of Time-Critical Self-Diagnosis

Every day, more than 40 million people ask ChatGPT a health question, such as whether laptop posture explains a stiff neck and days of pounding headache. Posture is plausible, but meningitis is a time-critical possibility. We introduce SelfDx, a paired benchmark for whether models have the appropriate response behavior, a form of epistemic independence, in health questions where misplaced agreement with the user's self-diagnosis could delay urgent or emergency care. Each of 100 pairs contrasts a caution case, where a serious alternative remains possible, with an agreement case, where the user supplies evidence that rules out the alternative and it is safe for the model to agree. We find across 27 frontier models that they tend to inappropriately agree with the user's self-diagnosis in cases that require caution. Even the best current-generation model reaches only a 77.0% appropriate caution rate on time-critical condition cases. To address this gap, we experiment with three prompt variants that increase appropriate caution but reduce appropriate agreement by 9-35%, indicating excessive caution. In contrast, supervised fine-tuning on 128 balanced examples improves both rates together across three runs. We recommend post-training to improve appropriate caution without making models cautious on every case, with paired evaluation to verify both response behaviors.

HealBench: A Fine-Grained Benchmark for Evaluating Psychological Comfort in Text-to-Image Generation

Despite advances in photorealism, semantic fidelity, and aesthetics, text-to-image generation and image editing remain understudied in a human-centered dimension: whether their outputs feel psychologically comforting. Grounded in cognitive evaluation theory, we define psychological comfort as an affective response shaped by viewers' appraisal of restorative and emotionally supportive visual cues. We introduce HealBench, a fine-grained benchmark of 30,000 high-quality image-text pairs spanning 19 categories and 92 subcategories, selected from 100,000 candidates through a human- and expert-in-the-loop pipeline combining vision-language model matching, human preference models, expert filtering, and exemplar-guided augmentation. We also construct HealBench-Eval, a feature-clustered evaluation set of 1,095 instances. Using HealBench-Eval, we conduct objective and subjective evaluations of 14 representative image generation models and prompt-rewriting strategies. Results reveal a gap between conventional visual quality and perceived comfort: strong semantic alignment and aesthetics do not guarantee emotionally restorative images. Reinforcement learning experiments further show that HealScorer can serve as a reward signal for assessing and improving the emotional and restorative quality of generated images. These findings establish psychological comfort as a distinct dimension of human-centered image generation and position HealBench as a foundation for affect-aware generative models.

Progressive Subset Selection and Cascaded Consistency for Unsupervised Vision-Language Model Adaptation

Pretrained Vision-Language Models (PVLMs) achieve strong zero-shot recognition but often suffer performance degradation under target-domain shift. Unsupervised adaptation provides a practical way to improve target-domain performance without labeled data, but it remains challenging because pseudo-labels generated by the model can be unreliable and no labeled validation set is available for model selection or early stopping. We propose a simple framework for unsupervised PVLM adaptation that combines progressive margin-based subset selection with cascaded consistency learning. The subset selection strategy constructs a class-balanced pseudo-labeled set using prediction margins and gradually expands it during training, enabling adaptation to start from reliable supervision anchors before incorporating broader target-domain data. Cascaded consistency learning further exploits unlabeled samples by using temporally smoothed teacher predictions on weakly augmented views to supervise student predictions on strongly augmented views, with confidence filtering to reduce the influence of uncertain pseudo-labels. To preserve the pretrained embedding structure, the framework updates only normalization parameters in the visual encoder and class prototypes initialized from text prompts. Experiments across multiple recognition benchmarks with both CLIP and SigLIP backbones show consistent improvements over zero-shot baselines and competitive performance against recent unsupervised PVLM adaptation methods. These results suggest that progressive pseudo-label selection and teacher-guided consistency provide an effective and lightweight strategy for adapting PVLM classifiers without labeled target data.

Structured-Token Attention for Fine-Grained mmWave Liquid Identification

Fine-grained liquid identification requires a classifier to distinguish similar substances from compact millimeter-wave measurements. We investigate structured-token attention, which represents a radar feature tensor through complementary antenna, feature, branch, and global views. The proposed StructAttn model embeds these views into eleven tokens and combines them with a three-layer transformer. Experiments on the public FG-LiquID data cover 30 liquids and 3,000 samples. Over five seeds on the released split, StructAttn achieves 96.70\% mean best-epoch accuracy, matching the original-recipe MMNet baseline and exceeding a scalar-token attention model by 1.50 percentage points. A five-seed probability ensemble reaches 97\%, corresponding to 18 errors among 600 test samples. Final-epoch and five-fold results complement the checkpoint-selected comparison, with StructAttn obtaining 95.30\% out-of-fold accuracy. Token and feature analyses show that structured views are useful for prediction, while residual confusions concentrate in water, soft-drink, and edible-oil classes. These results support structured tokenization as a practical design choice for attention-based liquid recognition.

Cross-Visibility-Gap Identity Persistence for 4D Scene Graphs

4D scene graphs require persistent object identity when an object disappears inside an opaque carrier and later reappears as a fresh detection. When appearance and target-centric motion are unavailable, identity recovery must rely on relational history. We formulate cross-visibility-gap identity persistence as historical-node recovery through a remembered target--carrier binding.
  We introduce CVG-SGM, a perception-frontend-compatible transfer--maintain--read memory. Geometry-weighted cross-node interaction transfers target-specific evidence into carrier states, a shared per-node GRU maintains this information during transport, and a two-time readout combines stored carrier--identity compatibility with current detection--carrier geometry to recover the historical identity.
  In the controlled one-identity-per-carrier setting, CVG-SGM achieves 1.000 re-link accuracy, compared with 0.570 for both no-memory and parameter-count-matched no-write controls. It also generalizes to scene-disjoint, unseen longer visibility gaps. On real RGB-D/SAM2 feature-level episodes, it correctly re-links 36/36 held-out detections, compared with 28/36 for the strongest fixed carrier--identity control. Counterfactual carrier-memory swaps redirect predictions according to the swapped bindings, supporting the role of stored relational information in identity recovery. The current approach remains limited by shared-carrier identity ambiguity and does not provide end-to-end raw-video re-identification.

Self-Evolving Agents through Privileged Retrospective Distillation

Self-evolving agents aim to improve from their own experience rather than remain fixed after deployment, but naturally collected trajectories often lack ground-truth solutions or verifier-derived rewards. We introduce Retrospective Self-Distillation (RSD), a multi-round framework that turns such experience into dense supervision using privileged retrospective context. For each on-policy trajectory, the same underlying model acts as both student and teacher: the teacher re-scores the student's realized actions while conditioning on cross-task precedents and lessons, together with turn-level reflection and future hindsight from the completed trajectory. The resulting teacher distribution is distilled back into the policy through on-policy self-distillation. Across rounds, the experience bank grows and the retrospective context is refreshed, allowing the model, its data, and its supervision to co-evolve. We further introduce frontier-guided context-policy evolution, where a stronger frontier model proposes constrained edits to how privileged context is constructed. Across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and SearchQA with Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B, RSD improves performance by up to 59.7% relative to the base model, while frontier-guided context-policy evolution adds up to a further 44.7% relative improvement over a fixed context policy. These results show that naturally accumulated experience can be converted into persistent model capability without ground-truth trajectories or verifier-derived rewards for the core policy update.

Orthogonal Boundary Unlearning for Dual-Use Concepts in Large Language Models

Mitigating catastrophic risks from large language models requires excising hazardous operational capabilities, such as cyberwarfare exploitation and pathogen enhancement, without degrading benign scientific utility. In these dual-use domains, legitimate defensive science and malicious attacks share vocabulary, syntax, and internal model representations, causing standard unlearning methods to induce severe collateral damage (concept bleeding).
  In this work, we present Orthogonal Boundary Unlearning (OBU), a mathematically grounded framework designed to eliminate collateral damage on benign dual-use capabilities. OBU extracts a domain-matched capability subspace from paired calibration queries via singular value decomposition on empirical activation covariances, and restricts parameter updates strictly to the orthogonal null space. This guarantees that benign representations experience zero parameter perturbation along the spanned calibration subspace.
  We present two complementary realizations of OBU: Projected LoRA, which applies the orthogonal constraint during iterative gradient-based fine-tuning, and Closed-Form Weight Surgery, which directly edits model weights in a single analytical step without training. Across four modern open-weight foundation models (Gemma-3-4B, Qwen3.5-4B, SmolLM3-3B, and Granite-4.2-3B), OBU achieves strong dual-use concept separation, substantially mitigates in-context priming attacks, and demonstrates durable resistance against adversarial relearning without degrading benign domain competence.

The More the Better? A Comparison of Single- and Multi-Agent LLM Setups on a Financial Review Task

Practitioner guidance holds that a large language model reviews a document better when the work is divided among agents. We test a narrow form of it in a controlled comparison of single-agent and multi-agent setups on a financial task, reviewing credit assessment memos against requirements. One agent checks six requirements in one call. A team of three on the same model checks the same memo, two requirements each, in the same words, with no roles or later stage. Labels are known for every requirement on 300 synthetic memos, so misses and false alarms are measured separately. Two primary predictions are tested on four models at a Bonferroni-corrected level. Under an instruction to decide each requirement once, the team catches 0.08 to 0.10 more of the defects that carry an explanation on the three models whose single agent misses some, at the corrected level on two. It raises more false alarms on all four, at the corrected level on three. Balanced accuracy, an exploratory measure, changes by 0.02 or less, and the team uses 2.2 to 2.4 times the tokens. Showing each agent the requirements it was not given reduces the added false alarms on the two Claude models, at the corrected level on one. In exploratory comparisons, sampling the single agent three times and flagging on any draw gives higher balanced accuracy than the team on those models, and the team's results depend on the instruction.

Securing Agentic Commerce Through Behavior-Aware Fraud Detection and Cybersecurity for Trusted AI-Agent Payment Authorization

The growing use of autonomous artificial intelligence agents in digital commerce creates new security concerns involving agent impersonation, unauthorized payments, compromised agents, and misuse of delegated authority. This research introduces the Autonomous Commerce Agent Guard System (ACAGS), a cybersecurity framework combining supervised machine learning, behavioral analytics, agent-specific authorization controls, and explainable AI. The framework was evaluated using synthetic financial transaction data augmented with simulated agent characteristics and five adversarial scenarios. XGBoost was selected following comparisons with alternative classifiers. Across ten experimental runs, behavioral features improved mean F1-score from 52.12% to 67.49%, with agent-specific features increasing performance to 69.46%. The framework achieved detection rates of 99.77% for abnormal agent behavior, 99.73% for impersonation, and 100% for malicious delegation and authorization-scope violations. Compromised-agent detection reached 72.27%. ACAGS also supported APPROVE, REVIEW, and REJECT authorization outcomes, with SHAP explanations providing model interpretability. The results demonstrate the potential of combining behavioral intelligence and delegated authorization controls to strengthen simulated agentic-commerce payment security, while highlighting the need for validation using real-world transactions.

Runtime Control for AI Agents: Closing the Evaluation--Execution Gap in Operational Workflows

Language-model agents increasingly participate in operational workflows where approvals, binds, and payments create commitments. An approval valid when issued may no longer authorize execution after relevant facts, evidence, policy, or authority change. We call this the evaluation--execution gap. We specify a state-bound runtime assurance layer that separates probabilistic measurement from deterministic adjudication, binds an execution allowance to the decision-relevant basis of an approval, invalidates that allowance after relevant logged changes, and rechecks admissibility at dispatch. This distinguishes the continued validity of a prior approval from a current-state policy check. We describe eight failure modes, an assurance model, a reference architecture, and prospective interventions with retrospective remediation. In a scripted 42-case replay, the state-bound configuration identifies 15 later-invalidated approvals that a deliberately non-re-evaluating point-in-time comparator does not revisit; it holds 14 affected actions before dispatch and compensates one landed effect. These results illustrate behavior under the stipulated policies and event orders, not superiority over dispatch-time enforcement designs or production effectiveness. Under explicit interception, ingestion, dependency, and serialization assumptions, the model establishes dispatch-time validity within the registered execution surface.

Beyond the Prompt: Recovering Hidden Concepts in Text-to-Image Models

Text-to-image models can generate visual concepts that their prompts never mention, and recent work shows that such associations can be implanted so that ordinary prompts trigger them. Auditing this behavior requires recovering the description that a prompt omits. Existing prompt optimizers are not well suited to this task. Gradient-based methods require backward access to the scoring encoder. Discrete evolutionary search treats semantically unrelated tokens as equally close. Language-model-guided search requires one inference call per candidate, which limits exploration. This paper presents EmbGA, an embedding-space genetic algorithm that recovers concepts present in a set of images but absent from the prompt that generated them. EmbGA keeps the population structure of genetic search but applies its operators in the token embedding space. Crossover interpolates between parent embeddings, and mutation moves an embedding toward a randomly drawn vocabulary embedding. Each offspring is then projected back to discrete tokens before scoring, so every evaluated candidate is a valid token sequence. We evaluate EmbGA on two benchmarks against six optimizers under two exploration budgets. EmbGA achieves the highest alignment when the exploration budget is large, recovers the most grounded tokens on natural images, and runs much faster than language-model-guided search.

Privacy-Preserving Personalized Federated Learning (PPP-FL) for Medical Imaging

Federated learning (FL) is a distributed machine learning paradigm that reduces transmission costs, mitigates cross-border data sharing restrictions, and enhances privacy by keeping raw data on local devices. Clients in FL systems train local models and share only updates, which are aggregated by a central server. While this setup limits direct data exposure, FL remains vulnerable to privacy attacks. Existing attack and defense strategies have largely been developed for centralized systems, and adapting them to FL introduces new challenges due to its unique architecture and training workflow. In this work, we first propose a model inversion attack on FL systems using Deep Convolutional Generative Adversarial Networks (DCGANs) in the context of large-scale medical imaging. Our method operates under realistic assumptions about the attacker's knowledge and capabilities. We then examine the effect of client-side differential privacy (DP) in mitigating privacy leakage and incorporate personalized FL to improve model performance under DP settings. Experiments on the Brain MRI ND-5 dataset show that DCGAN-based attacks can reconstruct semantically meaningful synthetic images, revealing significant risks to the privacy of training data. Applying DP reduces attack success but introduces an accuracy trade-off, which is further overcome by personalized FL at each client side. We also evaluate how different aggregation algorithms affect both attack vulnerability and defense effectiveness, underscoring the critical role of aggregation strategies. The proposed attack-and-defense framework is model-agnostic and applicable to a wide range of FL-based systems.

Training-Free Continual Learning for Multimodal Forecasting via Prompt Evolution

Adapting a forecasting system to a changing world classically means retraining. We ask whether the task of continually learning regimes for time series forecasting can be represented by a piece of text: a guidance block, evolved by outcome-driven reflection and used in a frozen LLM forecaster's prompt. On a refreshed multimodal benchmark (weekly search indices, daily currencies, daily commodities, each window paired with web-retrieved news), we run GEPA-style prompt evolution under strict contamination control to prevent the data leakage common in timeseries benchmarks: the forecaster's knowledge cutoff predates every evaluated window. A 900-word evolved guidance carried by Llama-3.3-70B beats TimeSeries Foundation Models (TSFMs) on held-out data (GM MASE 0.776 vs. 0.898 for TabPFN-TS-3), transfers to more recent models without measurable loss, and costs dollars rather than GPU-days to update. We characterize the loop: out-of-loop gains concentrate in the first reflection, in-loop improvements overstate out-of-loop gains 2-3x unless an untouched monitor arbitrates, and error-only objectives silently suppress the text modality. We provide two extensions. First, compiling the guidance into executable code with a TSFM as a callable tool recovers most of the gain deterministically. Second, ablations show the advantage widens at short horizons and that removing the news text does not hurt the guided forecaster, consistent with the text's value being distilled into policy during learning. We argue that continual prompt evolution is a practical, training-free alternative to retraining for multimodal forecasting.

Projected Distillation: On Learning to Solve Hard Problems from Close, Correct Targets

On-policy learning is central to post-training: training on a model's own generations limits distribution shift and helps preserve existing capabilities. On hard problems, however, on-policy RL provides no reward-based learning signal when all attempts fail. On-policy distillation provides next-token supervision from a teacher, but on hard problems, if a rollout goes wrong early, subsequent supervision is conditioned on prefixes that already contain the mistake. Correct behavior can instead come from a gold or teacher solution, but such off-policy targets can differ substantially from the model's generations and, as we show, may erode existing capabilities. We therefore treat on-policyness as a continuum and propose projected distillation, which minimally edits the student's failed attempts into correct targets. The method uses contrastive updates to favor corrections over failed attempts and refreshes these corrections as the student evolves, enabling near-on-policy learning even when all student attempts fail. Theoretically, we prove that the student's learning regret grows with the edit size: it scales linearly with the horizon under minimal correction, as in DAgger, and quadratically under full-answer replacement, as in behavior cloning. Empirically, across two students (Qwen3-8B and OLMo-2-7B) and five domains spanning math, code, science, and tool use, projected distillation gives the highest area under the specialization-retention frontier (best on 8 of 9 cells against GRPO and OPD, and 7 of 9 against SPOT and SODA). Our ablations highlight the importance of online corrections, the contrastive loss, and on-policyness: moving targets farther off-policy steadily worsens the specialization-retention trade-off, with retention falling from 0.916 to 0.744 when minimal corrections are replaced by student-agnostic gold solutions.
---
Recoverability and Verifier Strength: Two Measured Preconditions for Unattended Agent Self-Improvement

Agent platforms are starting to improve their own agents: they read a deployed agent's execution traces, derive a change, have an automated verifier approve it, and release it with no human author. How much of this loop can safely run without a human? We propose two quantities a platform can measure. Verifier strength $σ$ is how well the available verifier -- often an LLM judge -- agrees with a trusted reference verifier; it limits how much a step can improve the agent. Recoverability is how cheaply a bad change can be undone: whether undoing it needs a new verifier approval, and how much of the old behavior a rollback restores. A step should run unattended only if both are adequate. On our platform this rule allows prompt, tool and skill edits to run unattended and keeps weight updates under human sign-off.
  Our main contribution is these two definitions and how to measure them. We built a platform that applies the rule and tested it in two ways: on the platform's own approval gate, and in controlled experiments on the $τ^2$-bench agent benchmark. On verifier strength, the platform's LLM judge sometimes gave the same score no matter what it was shown (9 of 37 settings) and reported no error, so a verifier can look healthy while approving anything; its strength must be measured, and two simple tests catch the failure. On recoverability, restoring a saved copy brought the agent back to its original behavior, but a regenerated undo did not, so only changes whose old version is kept can be reversed without a human; and while a change was deployed, it altered the outcome of 17 percent of tasks beyond normal run-to-run variation, which no undo repairs. Together these support the rule: a change can run unattended only when its old version is kept and its verifier's strength has been measured.

Ask to Distill: Learning to Synthesize Data for On-Policy Distillation

On-policy distillation (OPD) can succeed with surprisingly few training questions, motivating a view of questions as carriers of distributional knowledge transfer. How can we assess whether a newly generated question is valuable for distillation, and learn to generate better questions---without access to its correct answer? We introduce Ask-to-Distill (\method), a framework that treats synthetic questions as distribution elicitors and optimizes the teacher supervision they expose along student-generated trajectories. A student-initialized synthesizer is optimized using a distributional surrogate for question value, computed from a frozen student--teacher pair without reference answers, correctness rewards, or per-candidate student updates. The reward combines teacher--student disagreement with teacher probability mass on the student's candidate support, together with length and diversity controls. The generated questions are filtered and deduplicated into a prompt-only bank for standard OPD. Across mathematical reasoning, code generation, and agentic tasks, our method improves average performance and teacher-gap recovery over unoptimized student-generated prompts and KL-filtered real questions, while matching or exceeding real-bank OPD in the evaluated settings. Signal analyses further show that optimized questions can elicit denser supervision and more total signal from shorter student responses, supporting elicited distributional supervision as an objective for synthetic distillation data. Our code, data and models are publicly available at https://github.com/GasolSun36/Ask_to_Distill.

Beyond Scalar Leaderboards: Adaptive Sampling for Pareto Frontier Identification in Multi-Objective LLM Arenas

The evaluation of large language models (LLMs) is inherently multi-objective, as different models may offer different tradeoffs across performance dimensions such as math, code, and writing. Because such evaluations are often based on costly and time-consuming human feedback, it is important to draw reliable conclusions using as few comparisons as possible. In this paper, we present Vector-Borda Empirical-Gap Elimination (VB-EGE), a fixed-confidence adaptive sampling algorithm for efficiently identifying models on the Pareto frontier in arena-style evaluation, where each comparison evaluates a pair of models on a prompt with respect to a selected performance dimension. VB-EGE progressively eliminates models from an active set once sufficient statistical evidence has been accumulated to certify their Pareto status. Under a multi-objective Bradley-Terry model, we show that VB-EGE returns the correct Pareto frontier with high probability and derive an instance-dependent upper bound on its sample complexity. We further establish a worst-case lower bound showing that VB-EGE is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors. Simulations and experiments on three real-world Arena datasets (Arena, SearchArena, and VisionArena) demonstrate VB-EGE's empirical effectiveness.

Scaling Graph Reasoning in Language Models with Learnable Graph Positional Encodings

Solving reasoning tasks supported on graphs typically requires providing a Language Reasoning Model (LRM) with a serialized description of the graph. This approach results in the input length growing with the number of edges, which limits the size of the problems that can be solved. Furthermore, even when graphs fit in context, performance sharply decays as the graphs grow. We propose to address this by augmenting LRMs with scalable and permutation equivariant graph positional encodings that modulate attention logits. An additional post-fusion-style module incorporates structural information into hidden states. We compute these positional encodings using a GNN followed by a Graph Transformer. This eliminates the need for serialized adjacencies, reducing the context burden to $O(N)$. We jointly fine-tune the positional encoder and the LRM LoRA adapters using optimal solutions generated by graph-theoretic solvers, resulting in a computationally efficient method. Trained on small graphs, the model exhibits improved size-generalization across graph-theoretic tasks on graphs with up to 1,000 nodes, and produces fewer tokens than a conventional LRM. Additionally, we observe that our model remains robust to node relabelings. Evaluations on graph-theoretic benchmarks also show that performance on small graphs is preserved.

MLIPS: Multi-Layer Indexer Pool Sharing

Sparse attention limits the keys used by each query, but finding those keys can still require a full-prefix scan at every layer. We introduce \textbf{MLIPS (Multi-Layer Indexer Pool Sharing)}, a training-free method that shares candidate search while retaining each consumer's own scoring and selection. A producer publishes a top-$M$ pool larger than the attention budget $K$; subsequent layers select their own top-$K$ from that pool. Measurements across three sparse-attention model families show substantially higher coverage from broad pools than overlap between final selections. We implement layerwise sharing, persistent decode reuse, and a coarse producer in vLLM for DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Across four long-context suites, three consumer layers per producer give $1.14$--$1.40\times$ speedups and $14.8$--$27.4\%$ lower GPU energy, with observed accuracy changes of $-1.49$ to $+0.29$ percentage points in single runs. More extensive sharing exposes task-dependent quality losses. Sampled prefill traces also reveal recurring selected KV positions across query groups and layers. We characterize this shared working-set structure and use it to motivate Top-$G$, an exploratory bounded pool construction for prefill. Its deployment, task accuracy, and serving benefits remain open.

Harness Self-Evolution for Long-Horizon Terminal Agents

Long-horizon terminal agents may solve a task in one run yet fail across repeated runs. Stochastic errors can propagate through actions and context, creating a gap between capability and reliable execution. We present RiseHarness, a framework for iteratively improving agent harnesses with model weights fixed. It focuses on flipping tasks with both successful and failed runs under the same configuration, using successful runs as references for failure analysis. RiseHarness combines agent trajectories with internal harness traces from a runtime monitor to examine how model actions interact with command execution, feedback, context management, and completion decisions. A Harness Handbook links this evidence to harness behaviors and source code, helping identify recurring problems in mechanisms shared across tasks. RiseHarness then searches other harness frameworks for relevant implementations and adapts them as design references to the current model and execution protocol. Candidate changes undergo behavioral checks and repeated task evaluation; versions with measured gains seed the next iteration. This loop integrates suitable mechanisms from different frameworks into a single harness and uses new execution evidence to guide further changes. We evaluate task success and reliability across models, ablate harness execution evidence and external research, and test transfer to unseen benchmarks with the same model. RiseHarness treats a specific model as the unit of harness adaptation, studying how changes to shared interaction mechanisms can make existing capabilities more reliable across tasks.

The Router Already Knows

Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models already make a sparse choice for every token: which experts should process it. We ask whether a sparse dictionary should reuse that choice. NR-SAE gives each routed expert a small dictionary and opens only those the frozen router selects. At block 15 of Gemma 4 26B-A4B, after ten million C4 training tokens and across three seeds, the expert-aligned autoencoder explains 0.518 of the variance in router-weighted expert outputs on a fixed evaluation capture. A rank-addressed control with the same parameters but sixteen times as many candidates reaches 0.481, a candidate-matched control 0.458, and an architecture-matched control with randomized expert identity 0.387. A transcoder variant also leads all three controls: 0.432, against 0.347, 0.371, and 0.118. Part of this advantage is architectural: each expert writes into a subspace of at most a quarter of the model width, so naming the expert names the subspace. How much it explains, we have not measured. At this layer and budget, the router already knows something a dictionary would otherwise have to learn. Our implementation did not train faster, and semantic interpretability, downstream loss, and causal faithfulness remain untested.

KPHarness: Co-Evolving Knowledge and Probing for Long-Horizon Gaming Harnesses

A failed gaming trajectory reveals that an agent needs intervention, but not necessarily how its execution harness should change. Effective harness adaptation therefore requires gathering diagnostic evidence, rather than merely proposing alternative recovery rules. We present KPHarness, a framework that adapts the external execution harness of a frozen vision-language-action model through knowledge-probing co-evolution. Recovery knowledge supplies context-specific intervention hypotheses that guide active probing; probe outcomes reveal when these hypotheses apply and provide evidence for refining both recovery knowledge and later probe selection. This reciprocal process allows the harness to improve not only which interventions it applies, but also how it investigates failures. KPHarness separates fast policy execution from a slow research loop that translates accumulated knowledge and probing evidence into bounded harness revisions. Each revision is compared with the incumbent under matched sandbox conditions and accepted only when the paired evaluation shows improvement. Recoveries supported by further validation are retained for cross-task reuse, while immediate interaction feedback and delayed episode-success credit update action- and knowledge-probing policies during adaptation. Experiments on Minecraft tasks spanning mining, combat, and crafting show improved task coverage over bare policy execution, a manually specified fixed harness, and knowledge-only harness evolution with non-adaptive probing. Evaluation on previously unseen tasks further shows improved task completion with the evolved harness, knowledge bank, and probing policies held fixed,

More Memory, Worse Agents: Error Reproduction and Anti-Persistence in LLM Agents

LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. A central design question is whether to retain only observed trajectories and outcomes or also the rules, summaries, and skills inferred from them. Retaining these abstractions allows agents to reuse strategies without repeatedly deriving them from raw trajectories. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. These results support separating experience accumulation from abstraction persistence in memorybased self-improvement.

ProMemAgent: Long-Horizon Proactive Assistance with Context-Aware Prospective Memory from Sensory Perceptions

Large language model (LLM) agents are moving beyond reactive responses toward proactive assistance that anticipates user needs without explicit requests. However, existing proactive agents primarily address immediate needs, while many everyday situations involve delayed intentions that can only be acted on hours or days later. Humans handle such intentions through prospective memory, the ability to remember to act on an intention when the right moment arises. In this paper, we introduce ProMemAgent, the first proactive agent to incorporate prospective memory over everyday sensory contexts for long-horizon proactive assistance. ProMemAgent organizes past sensory perceptions (e.g., egocentric video and audio) into context-aware memory, generating prospective cues and linking earlier events with subsequent developments for future retrieval. Given the current context, it retrieves relevant past events and performs memory-augmented proactive reasoning to determine whether assistance is needed. We further introduce CPMBench, the first benchmark for evaluating long-horizon proactive assistance over sensory contexts, comprising 1,000 instances across eight everyday categories. Extensive experiments show that ProMemAgent achieves the best memory retrieval performance and consistently outperforms all baselines on long-horizon proactive assistance.

Unified Sharp Bounds on Probabilities of Causation

Probabilities of causation (PoCs) have developed steadily, from the binary measures first introduced by Pearl to the non-binary setting recently studied by Shu et al. As the theory has matured, PoCs have found applications in a growing range of areas, including personalized medicine, marketing and legal attribution. Shu et al. proposed closed-form bounds for multi-valued treatments and outcomes and proved them valid, but left their sharpness in high dimensions open. We close this gap with a mathematical proof. We further show that $\PNS$, $\PSub$, $\PRep$ and $\PN$ are all special cases of a single, more general formula. Combined with their equivalence classes, this settles the question of sharp bounds for discrete PoCs from experimental and observational data: in every dimension, they are given in closed form by a single theorem.

When PTQ Calibration Helps or Hurts: Dose-Driven Collapse and Validation-Gated Abstention in Quantized Text Embedders

Learned post-training quantization (PTQ) calibration fits quantizer parameters on a small calibration set, and for a text embedder the natural supervision is retrieval relevance. We ask two questions that are usually conflated: how far to calibrate, and whether to calibrate at all. Across three embedders (e5-base-v2, BGE-M3, EmbeddingGemma-300m) at 4-bit weight quantization, 161 calibration runs, the outcome of a fixed procedure ranges from a gain to catastrophic degradation, and the driver is the dose of the update, measured as the functional displacement D_func of the quantized model from its uncalibrated start. On EmbeddingGemma the same learning-rate grid gives +1.47 to -71.53 points of in-domain NDCG@10 change; a label-free cosine objective collapses at the same dose (-69.52 vs -71.63), so the collapse is a property of learnable calibration rather than of retrieval supervision. Objective sensitivity is heterogeneous: on e5, ranking calibration is substantially more robust than cosine at matched displacement (+14.16 +/- 4.34 pp, 95% t-CI [+3.37, +24.96], three paired slates), whereas on BGE-M3 the ordering is directionally opposite but unresolved ([-5.18, +2.38]). Dose is the main axis but not a sufficient statistic: equal D_func reached along different paths differs by up to 1.61 pp. We therefore treat calibration as an optional intervention. A pre-registered rule that accepts a checkpoint only when a per-query paired confidence interval on a 25% calibration-validation split clears the uncalibrated baseline was run on 6 independent slates and five arms. It never selected a checkpoint below its slate's baseline (0 harmful selections in 30 decisions), retained 8 of 11 beneficial updates, and paid a mean oracle regret of 1.52 pp with one abstention that forfeited 11.13 pp, a safety-opportunity trade-off, not a free improvement. We report what remains open, including label efficiency.

Correcting Attribution Patching with a Few Exact Measurements

Mechanistic interpretability asks which language model components matter for a behavior, and activation patching answers by changing one component's activation and measuring its effect, at a cost growing with components and prompts. Attribution patching estimates these effects from one gradient pass, but its estimates can be far off, and verifying each one restores the cost. Can the structure of these errors reduce this cost? We propose Guided-AP, which uses a few exact measurements to reveal attribution patching's errors and correct unmeasured estimates. On a single prompt, many components' errors share one sign, so a small exact sample yields one correction for the rest. Over many prompts of one task, components are misestimated similarly, so a few measured prompts show what to verify and how to correct each component's average effect. Our analysis shows that choosing what to verify needs error size, while correction also needs sign. On a single prompt, the correction lowers error by up to 26% across three behaviors at the compute of verifying the largest estimates and also on larger models. Over many prompts, learned corrections cut the error of average effects on factual completion by 75% to 79%, repaying their cost in long analyses.

REM: What to Store, When to Recall, What to Forget in Experience Memory for Coding Agents

Large language model (LLM) agents now autonomously complete long-horizon tasks, such as repository-level coding, through hundreds of think-act steps, yet the experience each run produces is rarely reused on the next. Prior memory systems concentrate on retrieval, finding the most relevant past trajectory. Retrieval cannot observe the running agent, so it cannot decide whether the agent needs an experience, at which point, or for how long. We introduce REM (Reusable Experience Memory), a framework that manages the memory lifecycle from inside the agent's think-act loop through three explicit decisions. What to store: each completed trajectory is consolidated into a phase-structured document, two orders of magnitude smaller than the trajectory. When to recall: the document is injected at the start of the task; the costlier implementation record is withheld until an in-loop gate detects the agent struggling. What to forget: the record is evicted after a single exposure. On repository-level bug fixing (SWE-ContextBench Lite), REM retrieves the related trajectory more accurately than every state-of-the-art memory system (68.7% vs. 55.6% match@1) and improves, in every replicate, end-task resolution (27.6% vs. 24.9%) at 17% lower cost. Injecting the same experience as a summary or raw trajectory, even when oracle-retrieved, does not. Ablating any of the three decisions reduces resolution. Altogether, these results demonstrate that the bottleneck in experience reuse is not retrieval fidelity but how experience is stored and governed across its lifecycle, opening a control-theoretic perspective on agent memory.

Fundamental Impossibility Results in AI Safety: Trading Off between Desiderata

Is there a fundamental tradeoff between desiderata for safe AI systems, such as harmlessness and helpfulness? We answer by formalizing the interaction between AI systems and users as a game with three players: a defender and an attacker that are computationally or informationally bounded, and an AI that provides public advice and is less constrained. Our main result shows that there is a fundamental tradeoff in providing advice that helps the defender while advice remains public. We also consider whether the information that the AI shares can be manipulated to benefit only the defender and conclude with a discussion of how we can use these results in designing safer AI systems.

Trail-Weighted Influence Scores for Approximate Outcome Prediction in Bayesian Networks

We study trail-weighted pruning of evidence for Bayesian-network prediction. A traversal enumerates the active trails from each evidence node to the outcome, scores each trail by the product of its edge strengths, and drops evidence whose score falls below a threshold. For collider-free networks (chains and forks) over binary variables, we show that the trail score equals the exact correlation between a trail's endpoints, that a chain and a fork with equal edge strengths have identical joint distributions, and that the pruned posterior is within a computable sum of per-trail terms of the exact posterior when each dropped trail lies in its own branch of the outcome node. We prove this bound, check it exhaustively on all small trees, and show that it fails when dropped trails share a branch. In simulations on random trees, thresholding trail scores gives 1.4 to 13 times lower error than random pruning of the same size. We also apply the score, as an unproven heuristic, to seven bnlearn networks with colliders and loops. Keeping the top half of the active evidence by trail score gives lower error than random pruning on all seven, but it is not clearly better than a mutual-information ranking, and scoring costs more than the inference it saves on six of the seven, so we show no speedup. Models trained on trail, graph and information features and tested on networks they never saw do not beat the trail score or mutual information, but trail features carry most of what they use and clearly outperform generic graph features. A bound for general networks is left to future work.

Predictive Closure: A Set-Level Characterization of JEPA's Feature Selection

In joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPA), an encoder and a predictor are trained together to produce a representation that minimizes the error in predicting the representation of the next observation. Because the representations of both the current and the next observation are learned, it is unclear which features they will include. Earlier characterizations describe the features JEPA selects in terms of their individual predictability, raising the concern that unpredictable features, like a traffic light, will not be encoded into the representation. However, the prediction error is also affected by the relationships between features, e.g., the light's color helps predict whether the traffic will stop. We propose the predictive closure characterization, which accounts for these relationships. Based on a reformulation of the JEPA training objective, we show that selection depends on properties of feature sets. This view agrees with earlier works when features evolve independently, but makes new predictions when they do not, for instance that a feature like the traffic light can still be retained. We test this characterization in controlled experiments, which show that unpredictable features can become better represented when they help predict other features, and that changing one feature's predictive relationships can affect how well others are represented. We also demonstrate implications for steering feature selection: targeting one feature with an auxiliary objective, or supplying it to the predictor as side information, can strengthen the representation of another feature whose future values it helps predict, so steering can be indirect.

On the Limits of Scale: Inverse Scaling in Vision-Language Models

Scaling laws suggest that increasing model size, training data, and compute generally yields monotonic performance improvements. While this often holds at the aggregate level, we present systematic evidence that vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit inverse scaling at the subtask level: specific question categories on which larger models perform worse than smaller counterparts within the same model family. We evaluate 19 models from four distinct model families across eight multimodal benchmarks (nine evaluation splits), answering 29,386 questions per model, 558,334 model-question evaluations in total. Using per-question pairwise analysis across all model pairs in each family, we identify 375 subtask-model-pair combinations exhibiting inverse scaling, spanning 4,342 unique queries where a smaller model answers correctly but a larger model fails, and we find that these patterns replicate across independently developed architectures. We identify 13 subtask-dataset pairs that show inverse scaling across multiple families, consistent with the phenomenon being task-driven rather than model-specific. The most affected domains in our study include multi-image spatial reasoning, visual perception, and university-level STEM knowledge, with accuracy gaps exceeding 20 percentage points in several cases, while diagram understanding appears comparatively resistant. These findings challenge the assumption that increasing model size uniformly improves multimodal capabilities and suggest that model selection should be task-aware.

LiBRA: Logit Fingerprints for Byzantine-Robust Client Selection in Federated Learning

Byzantine-robust aggregation shields federated learning from malicious updates, but the rules that filter out suspect updates do so by comparing clients' parameter vectors. That comparison requires access to every client's update, which secure aggregation exists to withhold, costs $O(n^2 d)$ in the parameter dimension $d$, and is defined only between clients that share an architecture. We propose LiBRA, which compares clients on what their models compute rather than on the parameters that compute them, using a fingerprint of $C^2$ values for $C$ classes, the class-conditional logit response of each client's model to pseudo-patterns optimised against it. We instantiate LiBRA as variants of Krum, Multi-Krum and Bulyan that change only what the dissimilarity score is computed on. On Fashion-MNIST and CIFAR-10 with three architectures and four backdoor and label-manipulation attacks under i.i.d. partitions, each variant holds every attack its parent holds, and on identical client updates under a backdoor attack the fingerprint ranks malicious clients within 0.006 AUC of the full parameter vector. The variants thus match the robustness of the rules they extend, while their trust decision reads no client parameter, compares up to 49,090 times fewer values, and can by construction compare clients whose architectures differ, at the cost of one pattern optimisation per client per round.

A Small Model Asks Better: Fine-Tuning Query Clarification for Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Query clarification is essential for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems that face ambiguous user queries. We consider multi-turn grounded conversational scenarios where a user query is either ill-formed or ambiguous in the context of the prior dialog or, more interestingly, it may be well-formed but when posed against retrieved documents there are multiple relevant interpretations. The task is to recognize both such cases and then generate an appropriate clarification request to the user. We present ClariEdge, a new dataset of approximately 3,900 examples that include such ambiguous queries along with negative examples that do not require clarification. We also show that a lightweight fine-tuned LoRA achieves 96.6% classification accuracy and outperforms prompted baselines of many larger models on both classification and quality of clarification generation. Our study shows that hard negatives are an essential ingredient to separate out queries that are truly ambiguous from queries that may look ambiguous but are not (i.e., can be resolved from the context). The same LoRA achieves high performance in both pre-retrieval and post-retrieval scenarios; thus, it can be used in two places in a RAG pipeline as a check before answer generation. The ClariEdge dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ibm-research/ClariEdge.

Exposing Vulnerability Monoculture Through Systemic Adversarial Robustness

Beneath the apparent diversity of foundation models lies an increasingly homogeneous technical stack of common training pipelines, distillation methods, and data. These shared algorithmic choices can expose an otherwise distinct model ecosystem to concentrated risks leading to a vulnerability monoculture. Yet current practice in assessing adversarial robustness is restricted to individual robustness scores and attack transfer rates, leaving open the unexplored fundamental question: how many attacks suffice for catastrophic widespread compromise? In this paper, we investigate the empirical nature of shared vulnerabilities within a model pool and introduce Systemic Adversarial Robustness (SAR), a novel measure of shared vulnerability of a model pool through the attack diversity needed to exploit it. Specifically, SAR interpolates between single-attack transferability and independent per-model robustness: small values reveal a vulnerability monoculture, whereas large values indicate that failures are dispersed across models. We theoretically formulate SAR as a partial set-cover problem with provable approximation guarantees---enabling the development of scalable algorithms to empirically measure the systemic adversarial robustness. We empirically test the shared vulnerability of vision and large language models and characterize their shared collective vulnerability curve. Specifically, we demonstrate that model pools distilled from DeepSeek-R1 and Claude Opus 4.6 exhibit lower estimated systemic robustness than their corresponding parent pools, revealing a potential systemic cost of frontier-model distillation. Our framework seeks to establish vulnerability monoculture as a measurable target for a more wholistic adversarial evaluation of model populations.

Refuses When Asked, Complies When Acting: A Runtime Safety Check for Robot Control Loops

Frontier models can control robots through agent harnesses that loop on one question: what motion comes next toward the user's goal? We show that this default loop carries out harmful instructions. With GPT-6 Astra controlling a low-cost SO-101 arm through the Inspect Robots harness on three household tasks whose hazard is common knowledge, such as "put the knife in the toy basket", the policy refused 0% of 15 trials, attempted the harm every time, and completed it in 27%. Its one exit, give_up, is meant for goals that cannot be achieved, and it never cited the hazard. We add an optional runtime safety check to the loop: before the first motion, the model is asked whether it would carry out the instruction, given the same camera frames. The check would have stopped all 15 hazardous instructions, including the 4 that completed the harm, and flagged none of the 15 harmless controls. Code and data: https://github.com/Arnav-Gr0ver/Refuses-When-Asked-Complies-When-Acting.

An LLM-Based Agent for Legal Compliance Assessment: Architecture, Memory, and Evaluation in a Canadian and Quebec Context

Checking whether a text complies with a body of law is slow when done by hand and fragile when done with keyword rules. This paper presents ComplianceAgent, the legal compliance sub-agent of COMPASS, a multi-agent framework that evaluates texts along four axes: digital sovereignty, carbon impact, compliance, and ethics. The agent relies on a large language model that scores a text on a fixed rubric, grounds its judgment in a reference legal corpus, and, when the score falls at or below a critical threshold, runs a corrective chain made of self-healing rewrites, a compliance label, an explainability report, and escalation to a human reviewer. We describe what the language model does at each step, how the memory of the agent is organized, and how the same implementation can be pointed at different reference corpora. We evaluate the agent in a Canadian and Quebec context, on a corpus of thirteen legal instruments that brings together federal and provincial texts, international treaties, and municipal documents of the City of Trois-Rivières. The evaluation relies on a hand-annotated gold standard of fifteen texts and combines classification metrics, a repeated-query stability test, retrieval precision, and a composite score. On this gold standard the agent separates every conforming text from every non-conforming one, its scores remain stable under repeated queries, and the index returns relevant passages.

Modeling Authentic Research Trajectories: A Graph View of Research Ideation

The research idea in a published paper is rarely the project's initial idea. Between the two lies a closed-loop process in which researchers run experiments, derive insights, identify challenges, explore the literature, and revise the idea, with each step motivated by evidence and reasoning they can articulate. We call the path one idea takes through this process, with the evidence and reasoning behind each step, its authentic research trajectory. Although it underlies every scientific contribution, it is rarely recorded. Existing LLM-based research agents generate an idea once, refine it against reviews, literature, or their own experimental results, or search over candidate ideas and experiments. In all of them the text of the idea evolves, while the evidence and reasoning that connected one version to the next are discarded. We propose the Research Progress Graph (RPG), a representation of authentic and complete research trajectories. An RPG records the evolution of an idea as typed research states connected by cognitive transitions, each annotated with a temporal index and the reasoning behind it, which makes this evolution an explicit object of study. We ground the representation in authentic trajectories whose steps their authors justify in annotations and papers. Comparing the representations of thirty-five research agents with the RPG, we find that each is an incomplete RPG; none keeps the state that changed, the evidence that changed it, and the reasoning connecting them as one evolving record. Modeling ideation with the RPG changes how research agents should be designed, evaluated, and trained. Agents predict the next research state instead of a complete idea, ideas are evaluated as trajectories instead of final texts, and authentic trajectories serve as training data. These directions shift research agents from generating static ideas to modeling how ideas evolve.

Unsupervised Tool Evolution for Coding Agents in Data-Lake Analytics

Coding agents can answer analytical questions over a data lake by discovering, cleaning, and joining the relevant data. However, they discard this work after each question and redo it from scratch for the next one. Tool evolution, which automatically builds and refines reusable tools across tasks, is a natural fit for this repetition. Since a ground-truth label for a data-lake question requires an expert to solve it first, we consider the unsupervised setting. We propose TEAL (Tool Evolution for Analytics over data Lakes), which evolves a library of tools and usage skills for a given Worker agent without labels. Two agents propose tools from complementary evidence, an Explorer from the data lake itself and a Critic from Worker trajectories. A proposed change is kept only if the Worker adopts the tool on a rerun without extra steps and any changed answer is supported by trajectory evidence. We also introduce TEAL-Q, which generates synthetic questions for tool evolution when real ones are unavailable. We evaluate on KramaBench and LakeQA with four Worker LLMs under step budgets, which cap the number of steps the Worker may take per question. With a tight budget of 10 or 15 steps, TEAL improves every Worker on both benchmarks, lifting Sonnet-5 at 15 steps by 9.3 points on KramaBench and 21.7 points on LakeQA. A tighter budget also yields better feedback for evolution. For example, Sonnet-5's LakeQA library evolved at 20 steps and deployed unchanged at 30 steps scores 68.3, above both the baseline (59.6) and a library evolved directly at 30 steps (64.2). Additionally, a library evolved on synthetic questions performs comparably to one evolved on real questions. These results show that useful tools can be evolved without labels, and hopefully inspire future work on what to evolve and how to obtain feedback in unsupervised agent evolution.

Detecting AI Training in Datacentre Power

AI governance agreements require methods to detect large model training runs on a monitored computing cluster, without trusting the operator. We develop a method to detect synchronous model training in facility-level power by Viterbi tracking of the wandering iteration cadence. On simulated datacentre power, when the operator makes no attempt to hide, the Viterbi tracker detects at least $96\%$ of training runs against a declared inference workload, at a $1\%$ false-alarm rate and within $0.04$ of the Neyman--Pearson optimum, given a meter that samples at $2$ Hz or faster. Of eight adversarial strategies an operator might use to hide, only extreme variation of the work per iteration evades the tracker. Its strongest form hides training in up to $73\%$ of five-minute observations, but over a day at least $87\%$ of such runs are still caught, at a $0.1\%$ false-alarm rate per run. This adversarial strategy costs no throughput, but in small-scale training the operator needs $93$--$267\%$ more compute to reach the same loss. A modest passive meter is sufficient to make hidden training both detectable and expensive, which makes power metering a promising part of compute verification that does not depend on trusting the operator.

Steering Vision-Language Models for Visual-Contextual Privacy in Location Disclosure

Vision-language models (VLMs) can infer sensitive information such as geolocation from subtle visual cues, creating privacy risks when responses disclose more detail than the visual context warrants. We study visual-contextual privacy in geolocation disclosure, where a VLM should reveal location information only at the granularity appropriate to the visual context depicted in the image. We first show that this contextually appropriate granularity is recoverable from pre-generation hidden states, even when generated responses are misaligned, with disclosure levels exhibiting an ordered structure along a common hidden-state direction. Motivated by this, we propose an inference-time route-and-steer framework: an ordinal granularity router maps visual-contextual privacy representations to a disclosure route, and a disclosure-level-conditioned steering module applies route-specific hidden-state interventions during decoding. Across four VLM backbones, our method improves visual-context-aware disclosure alignment, better balances over- and under-disclosure, and incurs only modest changes on general capability benchmarks. It further transfers to another dataset with a distinct data distribution and privacy-risk taxonomy.

Evolving Self-tuning Benchmarks for Long-Horizon Tool Use

For AI assistants to move beyond simple chat interfaces and reliably assist users with applications like Gmail, Venmo, and Slack, we need to improve their tool-use capabilities. However, existing tool use benchmarks are still limited and difficult to scale because they rely on human supervision or are too domain-specific. To make data generation scalable, we propose, TURTLE (Tool Use Related Tasks with Long-Horizon Evolution), an Unsupervised Environment Design (UED) approach to evolve existing tasks using a proposer, a solver, and a feedback generator large language model agents. The proposer is rewarded for difficult yet solvable tasks based on the solver's empirical performance, and the feedback generator summarizes the solver's execution traces for the proposer to exploit. TURTLE produces evolving, self-tuning test suites adaptable to different environment requirements, from supporting user simulators and dense document retrieval to interacting across multiple apps. Empirical results on AppWorld, $τ$-Bench, and AutomationBench using Gemma 4 31B-it show that the evolved tasks target the desired difficulty range (e.g., a 165% increase in the percentage of hard tasks on AppWorld), increase task diversity (in terms of reasoning and simultaneous tool interactions), and elicit a broader range of failure modes than the original tasks.
