When Does an Added Planning Capability Create Operational Value? An Observable Evidence Standard

Industrial artificial intelligence (AI) planning systems combine predictive models, optimization logic, controls, and governance rules, but improvement in the complete policy does not show whether one addition made an independent contribution or whether that contribution justifies its burden. We address this attribution problem with an observable evidence standard that separates opportunity to act, executed-plan change, operational consequence, equivalence, uncertainty, and operating scope. Using forecast-to-plan replay on Favorita, M5, and Walmart data, we apply the standard to heterogeneous planning additions. Three limits of single-indicator evaluation emerge. First, a composite policy can conceal negligible component contribution: one control changed only 15 of 681 executed plans. Second, changing execution does not guarantee stable benefit: the evaluated multi-period capability helped in several long-lead settings but was 7.6% more costly under tight capacity. Third, indirect evidence can prioritize review but cannot complete attribution. Independent runs produced different paths in 19 of 599 cases that a rule-based check predicted would be unchanged. Although a lower-cost diagnostic matched the full-evaluation winner in only 55.0% of cases, it selected an option within the $10^{-6}$ cost-equivalence tolerance in 79.5%; the remaining cases prevented the proxy from authorizing acceptance. These experiments establish capability-level contribution evidence; the organization completes the value decision by weighing it against implementation and sustaining burden. The resulting capability evidence record links the observations to validation, deployment scope, fallback, monitoring, redesign, and retirement decisions.
---
Global Security Blind Spots: Translation as a Safety Bypass in Low-Resource Languages

Large language models are deployed globally, yet safety alignment and evaluation remain concentrated in English: harmful requests refused in English can elicit unsafe completions when translated into low-resource languages. We evaluate ten widely deployed LLMs across 79 languages (English plus 78 low-resource) under a translation-bypass threat model, using a metric conditioned on English safety, which separates translation bypass from unsafety already present in English. English safety does not transfer reliably: conditioned on safe English completions, mean bypass rates range from 21.9% to 44.4%. Bypass risk concentrates in a subset of language families, a pattern broadly shared across models. The shared ordering is confounded with translation degeneracy, but its variation across models is significant and survives every control we apply. We also show that per-model bypass rates do not identify a stable ranking: nine of ten models change rank across three conditioning schemes that estimate the same quantity, with no change to any underlying response.

Evaluating Context Compaction on the Questions That Come Later

Language-model agents compact their context for the question at hand, but the compacted context must also serve questions that come later. We evaluate compaction on these later questions, using four corpora that annotate the evidence of several questions per text and recorded agent trajectories, and compare every method with random deletion at the same budget. On MultiRC, keeping the sentences that gpt-6-luna (through the OpenAI Decisions API) judges necessary for the current question raises its accuracy on that question by 11.4 points at 25% of the tokens. On the later questions, however, no method that selects sentences for the current question does detectably better than deleting sentences at random. Deleting words instead of sentences does better: LLMLingua-2, which ignores the question, beats random sentence deletion at the same token count by 12.5 points on the later MultiRC questions. It keeps about twice random deletion's share of the evidence's content words, and at equal content-word retention its gain shrinks to 7.3 points (exploratory). On FEVER, label accuracy can overstate what the kept text supports: the gain of sentence selection is no longer detected when answers are scored against the kept evidence, and the 21.0-point gain of LLMLingua-2 is not shown to rest on the kept text. The ranking also depends on the setting: on Evidence Inference sentence selection beats LLMLingua-2 at a nominal 25% budget, and on $τ$-bench trajectories, whose later tool calls need exact identifiers, it beats random deletion while LLMLingua-2 shows no detected gain. Scores on the question a compaction was made for do not reliably show how it serves later ones.

Generalizing Multimodal Foundational Models in Context-Driven Class Incremental Learning

Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) aims to learn new classes continuously without forgetting the previously learned ones. Recent works in CIL have significantly advanced across various modalities; however, at the intersection of audio-visual modality, this problem remains underexplored. While representations from multimodal foundational models (MFMs) encapsulate rich static priors, our preliminary analysis shows that these representations struggle in incremental settings. Motivated by this, we propose a framework that generalizes frozen MFMs to audio-visual CIL. Specifically, we leverage their dense audio and visual representations and employ an attention-based contextual guidance module, in which the audio representations serve as task-stable contextual guidance for the visual representations, enabling effective class recognition in the incremental setting. To further mitigate catastrophic forgetting and preserve stability across tasks, we employ feature- and logit-level distillation, which are primarily responsible for the reduced forgetting. Extensive evaluations across various audio-visual CIL benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Code is available at https://github.com/avigupta2798/MFM-CIL.
---
Stronger artificial intelligence breakthroughs hasten their own exhaustion

Progress in artificial intelligence is widely assumed to scale with effort: more researchers, papers and investment should accelerate discovery. Theories of technological growth instead locate the difficulty of discovery in the space of ideas. Here we show that neither scale nor space is enough: breakthroughs depend on diverse attention, which collective concentration can deplete. Across 36 years of computer science, we analyze 7.2 million papers, 73,089 peer reviews and 3.7 million researchers and their topic shifts. The field expanded while its attention narrowed sharply after the Transformer. Over half of topic-shifting researchers moved into AI, especially mid-career ones, and reviewer confidence fell by a full point on a 5-point scale. Each successive breakthrough, from AlexNet to large language models, raised the field's carrying capacity for ideas but was followed by steeper exhaustion of those ideas. Bigger breakthroughs capture attention more completely, depleting the diversity their successors may draw upon. Structural biology shows the same cycle after cryo-EM and AlphaFold. Simulations show how well-intentioned and widely used policies such as reciprocal reviewing empower prolific authors and further narrow what the field accepts. We call for institutions that protect diverse attention in artificial intelligence rather than reward concentration on the winning paradigm.

Vulnerability Analysis for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement

Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (SMARL) aims to learn cooperative control policies under prescribed safety constraints. Al- though existing SMARL methods focus on enforcing safety during policy optimization, their vulnerability remains largely unexplored. This paper investigates inference-time observation attacks against SMARL, where an adversary perturbs the observations of a subset of agents, thereby altering the actions produced by their policies. We propose MinMax Attack, an attack framework that jointly selects adversarial target actions for compromised agents while accounting for the Cooperative Resistance of benign agents. We further derive a bound on the induced safety cost and characterize its relation- ship with the Compromised-Agent Budget and the Perturbation Budget. Experiments across multiple safety-critical multi-agent benchmarks and representative state-of-the-art SMARL algorithms show that MinMax Attack induces substantially higher safety costs than existing attack baselines while preserving task reward, thereby reducing its detectability and achieving both attack effectiveness and stealthiness.

TEX: Test-Time Scaling Testing Agents via Execution-based Cross-Validation

Test-case generation is critical but remains challenging for language models. Existing test-time scaling methods either isolate candidates or aggregate traces, which do not scale to long-horizon tasks. We present TEX, a collaborative test-time scaling framework based on execution-driven cross-validation. TEX parallelizes agents to generate tests and patches, cross-executing each test against every patch to form a $K \times K$ feedback matrix. Agents use their matrix slices for refinement, enabling objective validation without verbose trace sharing. On SWT-Bench Verified, TEX achieves 86.4%, establishing a new state-of-the-art and improving +9.0% over single-agent baselines. Moreover, even random selection from TEX outputs (84.4%) surpasses the previous SOTA (84.0%), showing collaborative execution feedback improves solution quality, not just best-case samples. Our results highlight execution-grounded feedback as an effective scaling mechanism for long-horizon agentic tasks.

When Does Hate Transfer? A Large-Scale Study of Cross-Lingual Hate Speech Detection

Effective detection of hate content is essential for maintaining healthy online communities, yet performance can vary substantially across languages. This work investigates a core practical question: Can training data from one language support hate-speech detection in another language with comparable performance? To answer this question, we conduct a large-scale study across 14 languages. \change{We observe consistent patterns in which certain language pairs transfer more effectively than others. Among the factors we measure, linguistic similarity is the strongest predictor of these patterns, with temporal overlap and dataset-size difference playing smaller roles. These measurable factors do not fully explain the observed patterns; a complementary qualitative analysis suggests that shared cultural cues and communicative norms may be associated with some of the remaining cases, particularly within several clusters. We present this cultural interpretation as a hypothesis from qualitative inspection rather than a measured cause.}{} Our analysis includes low-resource languages, offering practical insights into when cross-lingual transfer can support them and where its limitations emerge. These findings inform the design of more reliable multilingual hate-detection systems.

VPFlow: Value-Predictive Planning with Imperfect World Models for Offline Goal Reaching

Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning enables skill acquisition by reaching any goal from any state without dense rewards. Model-based methods allow for planning by scaling test-time compute to solve complex tasks. Combining both holds appealing promise for pre-training on suboptimal demonstrations and zero-shot adaptation via goal specification and planning. Yet existing methods assume effective planning requires accurate next-state prediction in pixel or latent space. In this work, we show that value prediction is more useful for planning, whereas accurate state prediction is often unnecessary. Our key insight is that offline goal reaching naturally admits a closed-form Monte Carlo return, enabling value regression to replace temporal-difference bootstrapping. We use this return as the sole supervision for latent world models: imagined latents are trained to predict goal-reaching value rather than match future states, implicitly preventing representation collapse. These planning-oriented representations let imperfect world models imagine on the level sets of value field beyond dataset transitions. We then present VPFlow, a value-predictive planner that pairs the world models with a one-step flow policy and plans in its noise space via evolutionary search. It enables policy improvement with imagined generalized advantage and reduces out-of-distribution queries common in action-space planning. On 30 state and pixel OGBench datasets, VPFlow surpasses model-free baselines by at least 30.3 and 10.1 percentage points in success rate, respectively, and outperforms latent planners LeWorldModel, PLDM, and TD-MPC2. It achieves this despite a latent prediction error orders of magnitude larger than with state prediction, while minimizing this error lowers success rate. This demonstrates that value-predictive representations, not exact state prediction, are what offline planning requires.

A latent control model for realistic rodent motion

The development of embodied models of animal behavior is a major goal of the neuro-AI community. Embodied models will enhance our ability to make direct comparisons between biological and artificial agents. To make such comparisons effective, though, we need embodied models that actually move like animals. Previous work has developed virtual animal bodies and techniques for training realistic motion through imitation. However, to date, there is no system for embodied animal modeling that produces natural movements both during trained goal-directed behavior, and during exploration driven by random generation. Here, we introduce Simulated Control via Augmented Motor Priors for Embodied Reinforcement learning (SCAMPER), a system for training an embodied virtual rat model, enabling natural movements during both goal-directed behavior and random exploration. SCAMPER uses a variational architecture with two distinct priors, a motion prior for control and a behavior prior for exploration. We show that combining both of these priors allows SCAMPER to generate realistic behavior during exploration and exploit the task structure effectively. SCAMPER can also learn from sparse rewards as a result of the diverse movements induced by the behavior prior, allowing the system to model realistic sparse-reward tasks from experimental neuroscience. Altogether, SCAMPER provides the neuro-AI community with an effective new system for training embodied models of rodents on realistic tasks.

Certifying Repeatable Entry-Replay Witnesses for COBOL-to-Java Migration

Producing synthetic data for comparison testing a migrated COBOL application is a major challenge that agentic migrations face today. Our prior paper, the Agentic Method for Deterministic Validation of Legacy Code Migration, presents a deterministic oracle to facilitate such migrations, the Locksmith Loop. We now propose a novel method for achieving high branch and paragraph coverage through generated synthetic end-to-end test cases, by means of entry-replay test-case certification by observing the code execution trace on a surrogate COBOL program that mocks external interactions via a chronological external-operation tape. We applied our method to a very large COBOL program with 33,429 lines of code to generate 849 synthetic end-to-end test cases covering 2,973/3,806 source branches (78.1%) and 989/1,033 main-AST paragraphs (95.7%). Using the Locksmith Loop's projected-agreement validation, the generated Java code matched the COBOL surrogate on the specified observations across all 849 certified synthetic end-to-end test cases. During the campaign we detected 61 defects and fixed the Java generator accordingly. To test whether the coding agent could silently introduce business logic bugs, we seeded 450 defects into the Java code, such as deleted writes, deleted paragraph calls, and altered rounding, to verify how many of these would be detected by the gating methodology. The gate detected 47.6%, including all deleted writes and paragraph calls in executed code, but was unable to detect the rounding and precision defects.

Auditing Safety Preference Supervision with Recorded Derivations

Preference labels align model training, but the judgments behind them are difficult to inspect or verify. In this paper, we examine how preference construction can be checked when intermediate decisions are recorded, and which of them remain uncertain. We present Symbolic Preference Labeling, a method in which a pretrained language model classifies predefined attributes of prompts and responses. Explicit rules assign response verdicts, and a fixed verdict ranking determines the preference label. Each preference label has a recorded derivation: the response verdicts and preference label can be recomputed from the stored attribute assignments, rules, and verdict ranking without rerunning the language model. On 200 prompts annotated by human raters, the language model produces valid structured outputs for 192 prompts. All eight parsing failures occur in the subset of self-harm prompts marked as harmful. Using 465 preference pairs, we train one policy model with direct preference optimization across five training seeds. Mean measured benign refusal is 40.82 percent on 1,319 benign prompts that resemble unsafe ones, and mean measured toxic refusal is 84.98 percent on 655 toxic prompts. This study distinguishes the reproducibility of preference labels from attribute correctness. The recorded derivations make preference construction open to inspection, while attribute correctness and semantic safety require separate validation.
---
Theorem prover Arend

This paper is an exposition of the theorem prover Arend developed at JetBrains Research. Arend language has been designed to facilitate the formalization of mathematics within the framework of univalent foundations and with the focus on constructive mathematics. It is based on a variant of homotopy type theory with cubical syntax that is more closely aligned with Martin-Löf Type Theory (MLTT) than fully computational cubical type theories, while still retaining some of their computational properties useful in practice. Among the most notable features of the Arend language is its powerful system of classes and records supporting subsumptive subtyping, partial implementations and anonymous extensions, which provides extensive flexibility for constructing hierarchies of definitions. Formalization in Arend is streamlined by the IntelliJ Arend plugin, which transforms IntelliJ IDEA into a full-fledged integrated development environment (IDE) for the Arend language. The main Arend library arend-lib includes formalizations in constructive algebra, topology and synthetic homotopy theory.

When Models Know but Can't Linearly Say: Relational Encoding Limits Linear Vulnerability Classification in Code LLMs

Large language models can detect vulnerable code, yet the form of vulnerability encoding in their representations remains unclear. We analyze 8 code models spanning 6.7B-15B parameters on C programs using three datasets (DELTASECOMMITS, SVEN, and PRECISE-BUGS), comparing two diagnostic tasks on the same representations: identifying what patterns the code instantiates, and determining whether it is vulnerable. Models encode the first with high fidelity. Under the studied paired C-code datasets and linear-probing setup, vulnerability information is more accessible as a paired relational signal-whether code A is riskier than code B-than as a globally separable categorical label. This structural difference has implications for classification: the model reliably ranks which code is riskier but standard classifiers on isolated samples achieve limited accuracy because vulnerable and secure samples form overlapping distributions in representation space.

TxnQA: Benchmarking Analytical Reasoning and Evidence Grounding over Transaction Event Sequences

Analyzing merchant transaction histories requires dynamic temporal filtering, entity resolution, and aggregation over irregular event streams--operations poorly captured by static tabular or time-series forecasting benchmarks. We introduce TxnQA, an analytical QA benchmark comprising 27 task families (81 templates) alongside a 5-scenario fraud-risk reasoning suite that evaluates both verdicts and supporting evidence over synthetic, privacy-mitigated transaction data. Ground-truth answers are computed programmatically, and model performance is decomposed into format-following rate (FFR), conditional content accuracy (CA), and end-to-end correctness (E2E). Evaluating prompted proprietary baselines and six LoRA-adapted open-weight models shows that adaptation reliably enforces output schemas but leaves arithmetic bottlenecks: Gemma-4-31B-IT and Qwen3-8B attain E2E scores of 0.867 and 0.816, whereas evaluated MoE checkpoints reach only 0.632-0.713. Furthermore, on the 500-instance fraud suite, verdict accuracy frequently exceeds 80%, yet joint accuracy (requiring exact evidence attribution) collapses to at most ~23%. These findings identify schema compliance, semantic calculation, and evidence attribution as three distinct bottlenecks in transaction-sequence QA.

ValiAssist: Scaling LLM-generated Programming Feedback Validation by Demonstration

Generative AI can create programming feedback at scale, but validating its alignment with instructors' pedagogical goals and strategies remains challenging. Instructors may struggle to fully specify validation criteria upfront, as these often emerge through inspecting concrete examples and revising misaligned feedback. We present ValiAssist, a validation-by-demonstration system that infers reusable validation logic from instructors' revisions of AI-generated feedback and allows instructors to inspect and refine that logic as it is applied across other student submissions. In a comparative study with 16 instructors, ValiAssist helped instructors create and refine validation criteria, producing feedback that better aligned with their pedagogical intent (91% vs. 57%) and more than doubling the accuracy of correcting problematic criteria (77% vs. 32%). Our findings suggest that validation by demonstration can help instructors turn judgments they know how to make on individual cases into explicit, reusable logic, while progressively clarifying where those judgments do and do not generalize.

Target-Aware Curation of Hallucination Stress Tests for Large Language Models

Hallucination stress tests can yield similar average scores across language models even when different prompts elicit hallucinations in each model. We develop a procedure that iteratively edits source-grounded seed prompts to expose hallucinations in a target model. An LLM editor revises each prompt in response to the target's answer and a judge's category-specific score and explanation. In the main setting, the target also receives feedback on its previous response. Experiments cover 500 seeds from seven datasets and ten topics, with intrinsic and extrinsic hallucinations evaluated across open-weight and closed-source models. Editing substantially increases judge-rated severity over the seeds. In the baseline configuration, retained responses fall in the judge's strong-hallucination bin in 77.2% of runs targeting extrinsic hallucination and 74.2% targeting intrinsic hallucination. Later editing rounds account for more than half of the mean score increase. Among 219 prompts that pass model-assisted source-validity checks, 89 elicit responses containing unsupported claims. Across five models, 200 curated prompts yield similar average severity scores, although pairwise Spearman correlations between prompt-level scores are only $ρ=0.0387$ to $0.1897$. Curated prompts can be reused across models, but their diagnostic value depends on source validity and the failures they expose in each model.

AutoRedClaw: Automated Red-Teaming for Claw-Style Agents

Agent systems are shifting from task-scoped tool-use agents to persistent, personalized assistants such as OpenClaw. These claw-style agents maintain configuration files (e.g., AGENTS.md), long-term memory, and reusable skills. Once poisoned, this persistent state is repeatedly reloaded into the agent's context, creating persistent threats across tasks and sessions. Existing security evaluation systems struggle to evaluate these complex claw-style systems since they rely heavily on manually constructed test cases and environments, which limits their scale and coverage; in addition, these manually designed cases quickly become outdated as agents evolve, and their evaluation settings leave a gap with how claw-style agents are deployed in practice. To address this, we introduce AutoRedClaw, an automated red-teaming framework that generates end-to-end attacks together with the environments needed to execute them. AutoRedClaw combines a strict quality-checking protocol with execution-based validation to ensure the correctness, realism, and effectiveness of generated samples, and adopts task-driven environment construction to keep costs low. To achieve broad coverage, it organizes generation along risk categories, attack strategies, attack stages, and attack surfaces, and uses experience-guided exploration to steer generation toward under-explored regions. AutoRedClaw achieves an effective attack rate above 30% at less than $0.10 per successful sample. From its outputs, we construct RedClaw-Bench, a 330-case benchmark that yields substantially higher attack success rates (ASRs) than AgentDojo, AgentDyn, and DTap. Across seven harnesses and four frontier models, most agent systems exceed 50% ASR, showing that our red-teaming pipeline provides a strong evaluation signal even for the most advanced agent systems. Our code and benchmark are released at https://github.com/SaFo-Lab/AutoRedClaw.

EGI-BENCH: An Enterprise General Intelligence Benchmark Suite

Enterprise adoption of LLMs in agentic workflows is gated by evaluations that span a wide arc of abilities: CRM operations, heterogeneous-document retrieval, personal data understanding, multi-turn planning, tool use, function calling, long-horizon research, and long-context software engineering. These evaluations live today in separate repositories with divergent runners, dependencies, and scoring conventions, which makes cross-benchmark comparison difficult and obscures what a single aggregate number actually represents. We release EGI-BENCH, a unified LLM agent benchmark suite that consolidates eight enterprise facing agent benchmarks (CRMARENA, HERB, PERSONABENCH, USERBENCH, MCP-UNIVERSE, MCPEVAL, BENCHFORCE, LOCOBENCH) behind a single command-line interface with two runtime-budgeted execution modes. On top of the suite we define the Enterprise General Intelligence (EGI) Score, a six-dimensional framework (M1-M6) whose buckets are populated by fine-grained sub-metrics routed from each benchmark rather than by benchmark-level averages. We describe the suite, the sub-metric routing, and the quick/deep budgets, and report results for fourteen frontier and cost-efficient LLMs spanning the GPT, Claude, and Gemini families under quick mode. Top-line EGI ranges from 39.6 (GPT-4o mini) to 65.6 (gpt-5.5), and no single model dominates every capability axis: gpt-5.5 leads task outcome, planning, and retrieval grounding; Claude Sonnet 4.5 leads tool actioning and safety/abstention; and Claude Haiku 4.5 leads engineering quality. Code is available at https://github.com/SalesforceAIResearch/egi-bench.

MMedAgent-Plus: Scaling Multimodal Medical Agents across Modalities, Tasks and Workflows

Medical workflows often require multiple interdependent analyses, but existing specialist and generalist AI models are not designed to coordinate them. Through a unified tool interface, the planner selects specialists, executes them in sequence, and synthesizes responses grounded in their outputs. We separately evaluate tool orchestration, response synthesis, and end-to-end execution on held-out samples. Magnolia: A Persistent-Memory Agent Framework for Computational Chemistry

Chemistry has recently entered a high-data regime as molecular databases from high-throughput platforms or computational studies have become available. Increasing automation within the chemical sciences leads to a more digital and precision chemistry. Dedicated scientific agents are becoming central to guiding the chemist through an ever-increasing amount of data and findings, including negative ones. Here, we introduce Magnolia, a persistent-memory artificial intelligence agent framework that integrates computational protocols, from docking to quantum chemistry, lab notebooks and chat logs relying on large language models (LLMs). It relies on a three-tier memory system: session-level observations are automatically captured and distilled into structured learnings, confirmed and elevated to project-tier entries and, when human-validated, promoted to cross-project rules that actively guide subsequent work and provide direct chemical feedback. We illustrate Magnolia on four case studies: quantum-chemical evaluation of aromatic systems (COMPAS database), molecular dynamics of butane and of modified DNA, and a protein-peptide docking campaign. Across these projects, Magnolia tracked 317 runs and accumulated 116 project-tier entries, from which four cross-project rules were promoted; in controlled replays of four tasks, runs with memory passed the checks that runs without it failed (for example, ten of ten docking-configuration checks, against one to four of ten). By combining automation, reproducibility, and human-in-the-loop control, our Magnolia framework streamlines docking, molecular dynamics and quantum chemistry calculations with direct analysis and LLM-augmented feedback, offering accelerated access to computational (bio)chemistry.

Sparse RL Relaxation for LLM Reasoning

Recent work has explored continuous representations in place of discrete tokens during Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, often referred to as soft CoT. This is motivated by the idea that a mixture of token embeddings can represent a superposition of multiple reasoning possibilities within a single step. However, it remains challenging to train large language models to realize this potential. Existing work has explored reinforcement learning (RL)-style training for soft CoT. However, these approaches treat soft tokens as actions in a Markov decision process rather than as differentiable intermediate computation steps. Thus, they do not propagate gradients across the soft tokens and miss out on the signal of how the choice of one soft token affects the next. In this paper, we propose a sparse relaxation method for RL (called SpaRLax), which leverages the differentiability of soft-CoT reasoning over time steps and makes training more stable and effective. We adopt an entmax sparsity method that adaptively determines the degree of softness, which truncates long-tail noise at confident steps while retaining multiple plausible candidates when uncertain. Across nine reasoning benchmarks, our method achieves the highest average performance compared with baseline methods. Further analyses validate the effective use of soft-token superposition in our approach, and also demonstrate the contribution of adaptive sparsity in our approach.

Profile-DP: Differential Privacy for User-Controlled Profile Release in Personalized LLM Inference

Personalized large language models (LLMs) increasingly rely on profile-conditioned inference, pairing public requests with rich, user-specific facts. This paradigm exposes the user profile itself, together with the user's explicit sharing preferences. We introduce Profile-DP, a policy-aware local differential privacy framework designed specifically for user-profile release in personalized LLM inference. Profile-DP provably bounds the release of user-withheld facts while treating public instructions, profile schemas, and authorized facts as public side information. By generating a privatized profile that composes with the public instruction prior to answer generation, our framework decouples formal privacy guarantees from downstream model behavior. We develop two finite-domain mechanisms satisfying Profile-DP: DP-SLOT independently perturbs protected attributes, while DP-BLOCK uses public semantic decisions to coordinate their release and jointly sample related attributes. A positive, public compatibility prior connects these attributes while preserving a profile-level privacy bound. On PEEP, DP-BLOCK leads the utility composite among DP methods at all 13 structured budgets and is closest to the ideal privacy--utility point in 18 of 26 format--budget settings under our composite weighting. Direct measurements reveal complementary strengths: DP-BLOCK discloses less, while DP-SLOT retains more authorized facts. These results make policy-aware profile release a practical basis for studying privacy and personalization.

Language Guidance for Supervised Vision Training: An Empirical Study of Generalization

Deep neural networks have achieved remarkable success on vision benchmarks, yet they continue to struggle with many generalization challenges. Supervised vision training relies on one-hot labels, which provide limited information about semantic structure and shared attributes between classes. This limited supervision can leave visual representations vulnerable to distribution shifts, spurious correlations, texture bias, adversarial perturbations, and forgetting in sequential learning settings. We study whether pretrained language models can serve as lightweight auxiliary supervision for vision training without requiring paired image-text data, prompt engineering, or contrastive objectives. Specifically, we evaluate two forms of language guidance, Explicit Language Guidance (ExLG) and Implicit Language Guidance (ImLG). We conduct a comprehensive evaluation across six generalization regimes, including in-distribution, out-of-distribution generalization, shortcut and spurious correlation resistance, texture and shape bias, adversarial robustness, and continual learning. Our analyses show that the two mechanisms have complementary strengths, with explicit guidance consistently benefiting in-distribution, low-data performance, and continual learning retention, while implicit guidance is often more useful in shortcut-sensitive settings and under stronger adversarial perturbations. Importantly, both are lightweight and add minimal parameters and training overhead. The analyses characterize when language-derived structure helps supervised vision training and provides a practical roadmap for using off-the-shelf pretrained models from another modality as auxiliary supervision.

LoWAgent: A Low-Resource Multilingual Benchmark for Web Agents

Web agents are typically evaluated on English-language interfaces, leaving their ability to operate across the world's multilingual websites largely unexplored. This gap is especially significant for low-resource languages, where standardized resources for evaluating web agents remain limited. To address this gap, we introduce Low-Resource Web Agent Benchmark (LoWAgent), a benchmark for evaluating web agents across different low-resource languages (LRLs) in comparison to the default, English. LoWAgent evaluates web agents across English and four LRLs: Hindi, Bengali, Urdu, and Amharic. The benchmark comprises of six fully localized websites, instantiated across five languages to yield 3,000 task instances. We evaluate three closed-source and five open-source models across all five languages on all six websites. Our findings show that both closed-source and open-source models experience performance degradation for LRLs compared to English, with substantially greater performance drops observed among the open-source model agents. We further evaluate the robustness of the models on realistic interface disruptions, such as cookie notices and advertisement pop-ups, and find that these further degrade agentic performance. Finally, we show that post-training on a small subset of LoWAgent traces can significantly improve low-resource language agentic task performance, demonstrating the usefulness of our benchmark.

LSA-PAST: Layer-Specific Adaptation and Prototype Transport for Compositional Incremental Learning

Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) enables models to recognize new classes over time while retaining prior knowledge, but many real-world problems are compositional, where visual concepts arise from combinations of primitives such as states (i.e., red, shiny) and objects (i.e., shoe, car). Standard CIL methods treat each composition as an independent class, ignoring shared primitives and providing no mechanism to stabilize their representations as new compositions arrive. This causes parameter updates to entangle primitives and induce drift that degrades performance on earlier compositions. Compositional Incremental Learning (composition-IL) formalizes this setting by introducing new state-object compositions across tasks. In this paper, we propose Layer-Specific Adaptation with Prototype-Anchored Adapter-Space Transport (LSA-PAST), a parameter-efficient method tailored to composition-IL. LSA integrates Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) into a frozen ViT with depth-specialized adapters for states, objects, and compositions. PAST then maps past, anchored prototypes into the current adapter space through a rehearsal-free affine transform fit on current-task data, enabling old prototypes to remain compatible with new adapters. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LSA-PAST achieves state-of-the-art (SOA) accuracy-forgetting trade-off performance for composition-IL.

CARLAR Influence: A Structured Hallucination Risk in Multimodal Cheque Extraction and an Exploratory Compressed-Image Countermeasure

Multimodal large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for structured extraction from financial documents, but their behavior under visual ambiguity is poorly understood. We study CARLAR (Courtesy Amount Recognition and Legal Amount Recognition) influence: a model returns the same amount for both cheque fields even though their annotations differ, including cases with a blank annotation encoded as zero. Across ten visual prompting conditions, we evaluate GPT-4.1 and Claude 4.5 Sonnet on 243 primary cheques (124 with two observed unequal amounts) and 598 strict mismatches from two additional datasets. Under courtesy redaction, GPT-4.1 CARLAR reaches 73.7 percent (95 percent bootstrap interval: 68.3 to 79.0; p below 0.001 versus baseline). Model-reported confidence can remain high when predictions agree; expected calibration error reaches 0.93 among CARLAR cases. We also test a compressed-image few-shot intervention that presents examples as one visual table without model fine-tuning. On the 243-case cohort, Claude's Control condition increases paired joint extraction accuracy by 11.5 percentage points (95 percent interval: 6.2 to 16.9; adjusted p = 0.00054), but its strict-124 gain is smaller and not significant after correction. The intervention's effect on predicted-field agreement is model-dependent: it decreases for Claude and increases for GPT-4.1. These results motivate validation methods that account for visual context and dependencies between extracted fields.

On the Fragility of Tasks under Composition: Implicit Budgeting in Language Models

Modern language models are increasingly asked to handle several tasks within a single prompt. Doing so requires solving each task correctly while integrating the resulting answers into a coherent response. In this paper, we identify a fundamental limitation in this process, where models make substantially more errors when tasks are composed, even when they can solve every constituent task reliably in isolation. This degradation cannot be fully explained by the accumulation of independent errors. Across both open-weight models and the frontier model Claude Opus 5, our findings instead point to an implicit thinking budget underlying this effect. In particular, under composition, models use substantially fewer tokens per task than in isolation, even when the full sequence remains well within the context window. Tasks that require longer solution traces in isolation are consequently more $\textit{fragile}$ under composition, i.e., models make more errors on prompts containing these tasks. In isolation, non-fragile tasks receive more reasoning than they need and waste compute. However, by grouping tasks according to their fragility, the implicit budgeting can be manipulated to reduce token usage by $43$\% across a range of math and linguistic tasks while also preserving accuracy.

Visual Task Modeling and Automated Planning for Humanoid Robots in Education of Individuals with Autism Spectrum Disorder

In robot-assisted education, a robot must understand how to complete a task in order to effectively guide a student through it. We present a novel method that enables a semi-autonomous, camera-equipped robot to recognise the task goal and plan actions to achieve it. Our work focuses on Shoebox Tasks, an activity commonly used in the education of individuals with Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD). These tasks are designed so that the goal can be inferred directly from their visual structure. We have developed a solver that uses a vision-based Large Language Model to convert photos of a Shoebox Task into a Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) representation and generate action plans for solving it. We have also implemented a robotic interface that enables a Nao robot to autonomously execute various Shoebox Tasks and assist a student by suggesting next steps or signalling detected failures through action demonstrations, voice commands, or gestures. With an adapted robotic interface, the solver can be integrated into a variety of educational robots. No programming expertise is required from teachers, and the solver operates exclusively on captured images as input. Research in special educational needs indicates that students with ASD can benefit substantially from robot-assisted teaching, and our system aims to strengthen these learning opportunities while also fostering interest in robotics among individuals with ASD.

Learned Control Variates for Rare Grid-Outage Estimation at Electric Vehicle Charging Stations

Public charging of electric vehicles (EVs) adds a large and synchronized load to the distribution feeders. In most networks, it is rare to overload a feeder at the same time. When it does happen, the consequences can be severe: an overloaded feeder stresses its transformers and can trigger cascading outages across a city. Planners who design for high reliability therefore need to estimate very small outage probabilities. To that end, plain Monte Carlo estimation with an expensive grid simulator can require a prohibitively large number of runs for this task. To reduce the number of runs, we propose a control variate (CV) built from a learned classifier of the outage, trained on cheap observable features such as temperature and load forecasts. We show that for any binary learner, the variance reduction is an explicit function of its recall and false-positive rate. Furthermore, we show that control variate based on a Gaussian proxy of the load loses its effect as outages become rarer, while a learner with non-vanishing precision keeps removing a fixed fraction of the variance even in the extreme rare-event regime. We verify these results in numerical experiments using a feeder-overload simulator across three orders of magnitude of outage probability and obtain that even weak learner with a single feature can remove a quarter of the variance, while a boosted-tree learner is able to remove about 60%. We then apply the estimator to the feeder network of Surabaya, Indonesia, in which the Gaussian load model leaves every feeder three to seven times the one-day-in-ten-years blackout target. At the same simulation budget, planning the reserve with the learned CV and a 95% confidence limit reduces the city-wide blackout days by a third compared with Monte Carlo. These results show that learned control variates can help planners design distribution grids that stay reliable against rare outages.

Understanding the Benefits of Scaffolds through Computational Separations

Inference-time harnesses, i.e., external procedures that call a model many times and keep state across calls, can substantially improve model capabilities. In this paper, we investigate the benefits of using such harnesses and the extent to which these benefits can be internalized through training. More precisely, we conceptualize a harness $\mathcal{A}$ as an arbitrary wrapper around a base model $M$ and attempt to distill a student model from the harness's traces. Empirically we find that, even with simple scaffolds and small models, the inference-time harness significantly outperforms a distilled student. We then prove that such a gap is due to a fundamental representational limitation: inference-time harnesses can compute a closure of their per-call primitive that is $\NL$-complete even when the primitive is a trivial $\AC^0$ lookup, and that no fixed-depth forward pass can represent under the widely believed conjecture that $\NL\not\subseteq\TC^0$. Our theory suggests that expanding the representational capacity of the student relative to the task at hand, e.g., by enlarging the model, making the task easier, or lengthening its chain-of-thought, is necessary to close the gap, which we verify empirically. Finally, we scale to an agentic harness on competition math, where the same gap reappears. Overall, our results show that a harness confers on the base model a profound gain in representational power, one that training a same-class student on its traces cannot transfer back into the weights.

MoSAIK: Motion Search with Artificial Intelligence in K-space

Object:
  Motion artefacts remain a major limitation of routine two-dimensional T2-weighted turbo spin-echo (TSE) brain MRI, reducing diagnostic confidence and often necessitating repeated acquisitions. MoSAIK (Motion Search with AI in K-space) retrospectively estimates in-plane rigid-body motion directly from standard multi-coil TSE k-space. Without a motion-free reference, external navigators, or additional acquisitions, MoSAIK infers motion from k-space self-consistency violations and incorporates predicted parameters into model-based, motion-aware reconstruction rather than synthesizing corrected images.
  Materials and Methods:
  Selected complex coil data were represented as log-magnitude and phase channels. A decoupled convolutional embedding and Transformer encoder estimated phase-encoding-line-indexed rotation and two-dimensional translations. Training used motion-free clinical acquisitions with simulated motion; four held-out subjects were evaluated. Component-wise errors assessed estimation; SSIM and PSNR in silico and entropy focus criterion (EFC) in vivo assessed reconstruction.
  Results:
  Motion estimation errors were low for both rotation (MSE $<$ 0.2 deg$^2$) and translation (MSE $<$ 0.2 pixels$^2$), with Pearson correlations of approximately 0.96 and 0.94, respectively. In every in-vivo acquisition, more than 75% of slices showed information gain relative to uncorrected reconstructions. MoSAIK reduced visible artefacts while preserving anatomical detail.
  Discussion:
  Transformer-based k-space motion-trajectory estimation enables fast retrospective correction of standard clinical TSE brain MRI.

Model Exposure on One Device Endangers Others: Rethinking Edge Neural Network Security across Devices

Neural networks (NNs) are increasingly deployed on edge devices such as mobile and IoT platforms, offering low latency and improved privacy compared to cloud-based deployment. However, on-device models embedded in mobile applications can be unpacked and exposed to adversaries, enabling cross-node attacks in centrally distributed ecosystems, where adversarial examples crafted on one compromised device transfer effectively to other nodes sharing the same models. To address this threat, we propose VeilNN, a defense framework that reduces adversarial transferability across on-device models via model obfuscation. The core idea of VeilNN is to introduce controlled model diversity while preserving original task functionality. Specifically, VeilNN applies stealthy and irreversible obfuscation at both the architectural and parameter levels through network cell obfuscation and layer assembling, and is applicable to diverse NN architectures, including MLPs, CNNs, and Vision Transformers. Extensive experiments demonstrate that VeilNN effectively defends against cross-node attacks, achieving an average defense success of 49.19% and up to 81.19% across four adversarial attack types, with more than 33.16% improvement over existing defenses.

ICLHerd: Herding-Based Data Selection for LLMs with In-Context Learning as a Proxy

Data selection plays a central role in modern large language model training, as the quality of training data strongly influences downstream performance. A common approach is to curate a development set that encodes the desired skills (e.g., reasoning or multilinguality) and select data that are similar to them. Existing methods compute similarity using cosine distance over either the model's hidden states or gradients. However, we observe that this max-similarity strategy induces a mode-seeking bias: the selected data clusters tightly around the development samples. To address this, we propose ICLHerd, a data selection framework that leverages the von Mises-Fisher kernel to balance mode-seeking with mode-averaging. We further introduce in-context learning as a validation proxy, enabling efficient hyperparameter search over the balance trade-off. Results show that ICLHerd outperforms existing LLM data selection algorithms, highlighting the importance of balancing mode-seeking and mode-averaging.

Representing short-term movement to reveal animal identity and learning progress

How learning progresses over time and varies across individuals is a longstanding question in cognitive and behavioral science. However, a major challenge is identifying observable behavioral measures that faithfully capture learning progression. Existing approaches commonly rely on predefined behavioral features or task-level performance, while fine-scale posture and movement in freely behaving animals remain less explored. In this paper, we ask: can short-term movement trajectories reveal learning progress and individual identity? We introduce a self-supervised representation learning framework built on a simple hypothesis: behavioral characteristics associated with identity and learning remain relatively stable over nearby time intervals. Using temporal proximity as supervision, our framework learns compact representations directly from movement trajectories without manually defining behavioral states or kinematic features. Using behavior data from freely behaving rats, we show that the learned representations not only improve prediction of identity and learning-related readouts over existing methods, but also provide a tool for investigating broader scientific questions about when and how these signals appear in behavior. Specifically, trajectories as short as one second contain informative learning and identity signals. Egocentric pose dynamics are more predictive of identity, whereas allocentric motion is more predictive of learning-related variation, and both signals are strongest at decision junctions in the alternation cohort. We further use a representation-conditioned diffusion model to inspect movement patterns associated with identity and learning progress. Our findings establish short-term movement as an informative behavioral "fingerprint" for studying learning and individual differences. Our code is available at https://github.com/pizhn/TRAIL.

Ontic state recovery through prediction

We encounter a system through its responses, yet its state concerns how the system is. Predicting further responses promises knowledge beyond what is immediately observed. What that knowledge tells us about the system's state depends on what we mean by a state in the first place. We give this notion a model-relative meaning: a state is the complete response profile over a specified query universe, and configurations agreeing on every query are regarded as the same state. From this perspective, a learned state is understood through the distinctions between these profiles that it retains. Prediction concerns selected observations; recovery concerns the profile as a whole. We examine their relationship through query ambiguity, state-space geometry, and the choice of prediction targets. Synthetic systems with known response laws and neural recordings provide complementary settings for this investigation. The aim is to clarify how prediction can become a way of knowing a system, and what it would mean for a learned state to preserve the state being studied.

Mind Your Boundaries: Boundary-Aware Model Obfuscation against Cross-node Attack

With the advancement of powerful AI chips, on-device machine learning is gaining traction for its low latency and privacy protection. However, when edge devices are compromised, attackers can obtain white-box access to the deployed models by reversing the apps directly to obtain the knowledge of peer devices, which is known as Cross-Node Attacks, like performing efficient adversarial attacks. Most existing defenses target known vulnerabilities, yet white-box access still enables attackers to uncover more unknown weaknesses. This creates a critical risk for edge-device deployments, where one compromised device can endanger all models distributed from the same source. Motivated by findings that the model robustness is primarily influenced by the curvature of the decision boundary, we investigate how model parameters shape its curvature. In this paper, we propose a novel method, BMO, that obfuscates model parameters to diversify the decision boundary further resisting cross-node attack while preserving its benign accuracy. Specifically, we explore decomposing model parameters into boundary-position and boundary-curvature components based on null-space constraints, then models with differentiated decision boundaries are instantiated by injecting noise into the boundary-curvature component. Across all four major types of adversarial attacks with four widely-deployed on-device models, BMO achieves an average success rate of 68.68% in defense, far exceeding the most competitive baseline, which attains only 22.32% in defending against cross-node attacks. Moreover, BMO achieves comparable performance in benign accuracy compared with baselines.

Reconstruction-Optimized Expert Pruning for Mixture-of-Experts Language Models

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models reduce computation through sparse expert activation, but deployment still requires storing a large number of expert parameters. We study post-training expert pruning using task-domain calibration data as a subset-selection problem: given a pretrained MoE layer, select a fixed number of experts that best reconstruct its original output. Exact subset search is combinatorial and becomes intractable for modern MoE models with many experts per layer. We develop OptEP, a scalable continuous relaxation that directly optimizes differentiable expert-selection variables, followed by a lightweight reconstruction-based update of the retained expert weights. Across multiple MoE architectures and tasks spanning mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approach improves over the strongest existing expert-pruning baselines, with absolute accuracy gains of up to 15.05% on Qwen3-30B-A3B, 14.55% on Qwen3-235B-A22B, 11.06% on GLM-4.5-Air, and 16.20% on Mixtral-8x7B. The improvements are particularly pronounced under aggressive pruning, demonstrating that reconstruction-driven expert selection and weight adaptation can preserve substantially more of the original model's performance while reducing the number of retained experts.

EDMA: Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering

Multimodal question answering (MMQA) is based on integrating heterogeneous data sources, selectively leveraging relevant modalities, and ignoring distractors. Existing approaches based on Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) improve performance by splitting the task into intermediate steps, but do not quantify how each modality contributes to reaching the final answer. We argue that quantifying the effect that each modality has on the answering process improves process traceability and final performance. Thus, we propose Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering (EDMA), which uses logical entropy to formalise multimodal QA as an iterative process of entropy reduction and to quantify the contribution of each modality in distinguishing correct from incorrect candidate answers. This enables the construction of quantifiable answering trajectories, where each step is associated with a measurable entropy reduction. EDMA introduces (i) modality-conditioned partitioning to estimate unimodal entropy reduction, (ii) entropy-driven multimodal fusion to capture complementary information across modalities, and (iii) entropy-based selection of the answering trajectory that minimises the most logical entropy. Evaluated across standard MMQA benchmarks, EDMA outperforms competitive prompting baselines, with average gains of 12.8 F1 and 4.6 LLM-J points over Planning Prompting and 18.6 F1 and 8.3 LLM-J points over Chain-of-Thought. Moreover, EDMA outperforms self-consistency baselines and is competitive with the multi-agent system baseline. Moreover, we show that higher entropy correlates with more false positives and, through controlled trajectory selection, demonstrate that logical entropy serves as an effective, monotonic selector for correct reasoning paths, validating entropy level as a reliable proxy for performance.

PORA: Learning When More Reasoning Pays Off for LLM Agents

LLM agents perform tasks over multiple steps and revise their actions based on feedback. As the total reasoning budget is often limited and must support future steps, the agent must choose between short and long reasoning before each action. Longer reasoning may improve the final task outcome by preventing later errors, but it also uses budget capacity that may be needed for future decisions. This creates two challenges: estimating the downstream value of long reasoning and deciding when that value is worth using the remaining budget. We propose Paired Outcome-guided Reasoning Allocation (PORA), which learns this decision by comparing short and long reasoning from the same interaction state. During training, \method{} completes the task twice from each selected state, once with a short reasoning allowance and once with a long allowance, and compares their final quality and total processing to estimate when longer reasoning is valuable. During inference, a controller weighs this estimate against learned prices on remaining-budget pressure and chooses either short or long reasoning without running both alternatives. We evaluate PORA across application, memory, code, retail, and mathematics tasks, achieving strong results across all settings. On BFCL memory, it exceeds the strongest existing method by 17.4--32.9 percentage points. Within the same agent, it matches or exceeds always using long reasoning on code and mathematics with 50.6% fewer actor tokens and 31.2% fewer model requests, respectively.

PROVISO: Provenance-Informed Action Selection for Secure LLM Agents

LLM agents act on the external world through tools and third-party skills, so a single executed action can cause irreversible harm. Existing defenses judge a proposed action without tracking whether its content comes from the user or from external sources such as tool outputs, and they judge it in isolation rather than against an available alternative, which forces a trade-off between security and utility. We present PROVISO, a controller that decides which action to commit after the agent proposes an action and before it is executed. PROVISO encodes trusted observations, external observations, the proposed action, and an alternative into provenance-typed memory, relates them through role-specific queries, and commits the action with the highest calibrated value learned from outcomes. On four agent security tasks spanning the agent lifecycle, no baseline exceeds PROVISO in both security and utility. PROVISO improves F1 over the strongest baseline by 13.84 points on SkillsBench and by 7.07 points on ToolSafe, and it reaches 88-99 F1 on three additional skill detection benchmarks.

Joint Constraint Aggregation for Qualifier-Sensitive Retrieval-Augmented QA

Retrieval-augmented QA can fail even when the correct answer is in the retrieved evidence. This can happen when a competing answer satisfies some, but not all, of the question's requirements and is mentioned frequently in the retrieved documents, which can make it appear to be the best-supported answer. To test whether this retrieval skew actually affects the decision, we manipulate it directly: increasing the prevalence of passages that favor the competing answer makes the model more likely to select it. This points to a decision problem that candidate-level support alone cannot resolve. We therefore introduce Joint Constraint Aggregation (JCA), which compares candidates against the same question-derived requirements using the retrieved evidence. Across three evaluation settings and three instruction-tuned backbones, JCA improves overall accuracy, and its advantage grows when retrieval favors a competing answer. Together, these results suggest that when partial matches and retrieval prominence pull models toward a competing answer, comparing candidates against the question's full requirements can make selection more reliable.

Omni-Woodpecker: Hallucination Correction via Evidence-Prior Rebalancing for Omni LLMs

Recent Omni LLMs (OLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in joint audio-visual reasoning, yet cross-modal hallucinations remain a major challenge. Existing mitigation methods mainly operate at the output level, overlooking the entanglement of grounding evidence and learned priors within fused representations. To address this issue, we propose Omni-Woodpecker (OWP), a training-free framework that shifts hallucination mitigation from output-level manipulation to conflict-conditioned representation rebalancing. Specifically, OWP first identifies cross-modal conflicts from complementary modality-specific views. It then employs orthogonal decomposition to separate grounding evidence from competing priors within the hidden states. Finally, OWP adaptively rebalances fused representations by suppressing misleading priors and restoring ignored evidence before decoding. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and strong cross-architecture generalization of OWP. Using Qwen2.5-Omni-7B as the backbone, OWP improves performance by 12.9 and 8.6 points on CMM and AVHBench, respectively, while consistently achieving gains of 0.67-2.25 points across four general OLLM reasoning benchmarks. Furthermore, OWP generalizes effectively to VideoLLaMA2-AV, improving performance by 4.6 and 3.9 points on the same benchmarks. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/ZiiJianLiu/Omni-Woodpecker

A general-purpose System One decision model for clinical records

Clinical care, health-system operations and biomedical discovery depend on extracting reliable answers from patient records, yet applying diverse clinical questions across entire populations remains computationally expensive. Here we present Clinical System One (CS1), a 4-billion-parameter decision model for fast, typed judgments from clinical records, along with two agentic harnesses in which CS1 works together with a generalist language model. CS1-4B is trained on 12.4 million questions and answers from 3.6 million patients across a multi-hospital health system, combining structured-data keys, observed outcomes and language-model supervision across clinical notes, diagnostic reports and patient timelines. It answers multiple typed questions in a single forward pass without task-specific retraining, and each answer carries a probability that can route uncertain cases to human review. On 963,408 held-out patients, CS1-4B outperformed open decision models up to 27 billion parameters on 13 of 14 tasks scored independently of its training teacher. Across 21 external data sets, its aggregate performance approached that of the strongest tested 27-billion-parameter model, with 6.4-fold faster inference in a matched workload. Integrating CS1-4B with a language model agent produced cited answers over complete multi-year patient histories and screened a patient population against the eligibility criteria of 17 registered clinical trials, placing four times as many judged-eligible patients among its top 20 candidates as keyword search while using 690-fold fewer language-model tokens than a language model reading every record. These findings support a division of labour in clinical AI, with small decision models performing repeated record reading and generative models formulating questions and synthesizing evidence.

OpenVLHarness: Real World Visual Agentic Tool-Use with Multimodal Memory

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress on visual reasoning, yet many tasks require evidence that is difficult to obtain through internal reasoning alone: our controlled study on 22 benchmarks shows that simply increasing the inference budget of the base model yields only modest gains. Such tasks demand grounded perception, external knowledge, and numerical measurements obtained through heterogeneous tools, whose outputs must be made interpretable to the model and retained across reasoning steps. We introduce OpenVLHarness, a multimodal reasoning harness organized around a capability layer and persistent multimodal memory. The capability layer combines task-level tool interfaces with evidence rendering, providing consistent access to heterogeneous backends and converting their outputs into model-readable observations. Persistent multimodal memory retains the corresponding images, structured data, and web references as addressable artifacts throughout each query. A coding interface executes generated programs over stored data, enabling computations that combine results from multiple tools. Together, these mechanisms support an iterative process of acquiring, inspecting, and composing multimodal evidence. Evaluated on 22 benchmarks with eight open and proprietary backbones, OpenVLHarness improves average scores by 9.7-12.6 points over matched no-tool baselines, including +11.2 with Qwen3-VL-8B, +9.7 with Kimi K3, and +9.9 with GPT-6 Astra. With GPT-6 Luna at medium reasoning effort, OpenVLHarness outperforms the base model, Codex, and Visual Sketchpad at maximum effort by 7.7-8.5 points while incurring at most 37% of their model-token cost.

Reinforcement Learning with Visually Attributable On-Policy Self-Distillation

Multimodal reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) provides reliable task-level supervision, yet offers limited guidance for localizing errors in visual reasoning. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) complements this supervision with dense token-level corrections from a privileged teacher equipped with an evidence-centered visual view. However, the resulting corrections may conflate visual evidence with non-visual shifts induced by linguistic priors, response style, or the privileged view itself. We formalize this phenomenon as privileged-correction contamination: a token-level correction is contaminated when its update direction cannot be consistently attributed to the privileged visual evidence. Directly distilling contaminated corrections may therefore reinforce erroneous token-level updates. To address this issue, we propose reinforcement learning with visually attributable on-policy self-distillation, an evidence-consistent paradigm that augments task-level RLVR supervision with privileged token-level corrections grounded in visual evidence. We instantiate this paradigm with Visual-Attribution Gating (VAG), which uses the verifier-derived advantage as the optimization anchor and validates each privileged correction against the contrast between evidence-present and evidence-degraded teacher views. By admitting only corrections directionally supported by this visual contrast, VAG preserves visually grounded guidance while suppressing inconsistent residuals. Experiments across six visual reasoning benchmarks show that VAG consistently outperforms GRPO and OPSD methods, establishing new state-of-the-art average performance.

CoopUQ: Robust Multi-Robot Social Navigation via Uncertainty-Aware Cooperative Forecasting

Reinforcement learning enables mobile robots to make fast and scalable navigation decisions in constrained human-shared environments. However, directly extending single-robot approaches to multi-robot settings often fails to ensure safety and efficiency, especially under occlusions and out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios, where individual robots may overlook potential risks or make inaccurate predictions. Leveraging cooperation effectively for both prediction and navigation remains a major challenge. We propose CoopUQ, a multi-robot social navigation framework that bridges uncertainty-aware cooperative occupancy forecasting and robust decision making. CoopUQ predicts future occupancy grid maps through cross-robot spatiotemporal feature fusion and further leverages forecasts and their uncertainty to guide policy via constrained multi-agent reinforcement learning, where the cost penalizes navigation through regions predicted to be occupied or highly uncertain. This design enables the policy to adapt its risk sensitivity under distribution shifts. Experimental results across multiple Unity3D-based simulation environments with both in-distribution and OOD settings, including diverse layouts, pedestrian densities, and rushing behavior, demonstrate improved prediction performance and safety over representative baselines. Real-robot experiments further demonstrate its robustness across various scenes. Our code and videos are available at https://coopuq.github.io/.

Where is Physical RSI capability from? An Observational Scaling Law of LLM-as-policy

In this paper, we systematically investigate the mechanisms, scaling behavior, and generalization of physical Recursive self-improvement. We introduce PhysRSI-eval to quantify physical RSI through a five-axis metric: First-Attempt Performance, Efficiency, Success, Budget and Improvement from failure. Different VLMs are evaluated on 18 manipulation tasks under a fixed interaction budget. The analysis of experimental results motivates the Physical Barrel Hypothesis: different capabilities may constrain different parts of improvement. The five-axis metrics also distinguish systems with close public benchmark averages and show that high scores need not come with early or efficient gains. We study observational scaling from two perspectives: predicting the relative performance of VLM agents from their public capabilities, and forecasting later progress from early physical feedback. Together, these analyses ask how quickly and how far an agent can improve. Moreover, we conduct workspace interventions and then analyze what improvement leaves behind. Removing history reduces late-round success, frozen workspaces remain useful on unseen layouts, and three cross-task cases achieve success earlier. The results suggest that scene-specific adaptation and reusable experience can coexist.

WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions

Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.

Train An Imaginative Agent

A seemingly reasonable push in Sokoban can make the puzzle unsolvable. To avoid such mistakes, an agent has to anticipate the consequences of its actions before execution, especially when feedback from the real environment is sparse or actions are irreversible. We introduce Agentic Imagination (Agima), a training framework that trains an agent to imagine before acting, i.e. to be imaginative. Agima trains a single omni model to propose actions, generate images of their outcomes, and then reason over these imagined futures to decide what to execute in the real environment. Rather than committing to a single plan, the agent can simulate a sequence of actions, explore alternatives, and compare possible futures. We first use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to unify policy and world modeling within a single omni model. This enables the agent to predict future outcomes and perform basic forms of imagination, such as forward thinking and counterfactual branching. We then use agentic reinforcement learning (RL) to teach the agent to reason with its imagined futures and use them effectively for planning. Across four environments spanning Maze2D, Sokoban, Pacman, and PushT, Agima generalizes to harder settings unseen during training. Trained with SFT alone on two-box Sokoban, Agima solves 40% of four-box puzzles while seeing the real board only once every five moves, compared with an action-only planner that only solves 2%. After RL, new behaviors emerge in how Agima uses its imagination: it learns a new unified format that was unseen during the SFT stage, learns to conduct forward thinking only when necessary, and branches more frequently at critical decision points. An agent that can picture where its actions lead can learn when and where to look ahead, that is, to plan. Project page: https://agima.net/.

AnyGoal: On World Model Generalization Beyond Expert Rollouts

The goal of a world model is to capture the dynamics of an environment. This has the great appeal that it allows agents to plan and simulate a course of actions with the help of a world model. For these uses to be reliable, however, the learned dynamics must behave well beyond the specific state-action combinations observed in expert rollouts. Despite this, the relationship between data coverage, dynamics generalization, and downstream planning performance remains underexplored. We study this relationship through the lens of compositional generalization: can a world model recombine familiar state and action factors into valid dynamics? To answer this, we introduce AnyGoal, a benchmark of goal-shifted task variants built from widely used world-model environments, together with an open-source library. Each task is designed to be within reach of a world model that has learned the underlying dynamics: individual state factors and actions remain within their training support, but their combinations are underrepresented in expert trajectories. Across multiple environments and world model families, we find that current models consistently exhibit failures under these recombinations. By examining dynamics and representation failures across model families, interaction regimes, and evaluation metrics, we study where current world models generalize and where challenges remain for goal-shifted prediction and planning. Together, this framework, benchmark, and analysis provide a basis for evaluating progress toward world models that generalize to new planning tasks.

LatComm: Reusable Context Compression for Latent Communication between Large Language Models

Systems that combine several large language models (LLMs) often use one context for many questions. When the context is passed between models as text, the receiving model processes all of its tokens again for every question. Avoiding this cost requires a context representation that is compact, reusable across questions, and readable by a different LLM. Existing compression and latent communication methods each provide only some of these properties. We introduce LatComm, a Latent Communication interface that provides all three by separating encoding from reading: the context is encoded once without the question, and the receiver selects what to read for each question. A frozen context encoder LLM compresses the context into a fixed-size packet and sends it to a different frozen receiver LLM, which reads the packet through cross-attention, with gates computed from its prompt. On 12 multiple-choice question-answering datasets, LatComm improves average accuracy over single-vector baselines by up to 10.2 points. On QuALITY, it reduces the receiver's input by 57x and answers the questions of an article 3x faster.

Discrete Neural Interlingua for Ontology-Agnostic EHR Modeling

EHR foundation models promise scalable clinical prediction across health systems, yet their deployment is constrained by a tokenization bottleneck. The same clinical concept can appear as different ontology codes, local identifiers, synonyms, or free-text descriptions, which standard EHR tokenizers treat as unrelated symbols. A model trained on MIMIC-III with a closed-vocabulary tokenizer can use only 13% of eICU events. We introduce MEDIATOR, a discrete ontology-agnostic tokenizer that maps any clinical concept expressed as text into a shared vector-quantized codebook, turning EHR harmonization into open-vocabulary tokenization without manual mappings or a fixed vocabulary. Trained with synonym supervision and guided by enriched concept descriptions, code co-occurrence, and relation graphs, MEDIATOR collapses equivalent expressions while organizing distinct concepts in a medically meaningful discrete space. Used as the token interface of an EHR Transformer, MEDIATOR improves mean AUPRC over the strongest prior tokenizer by 4.0 points (8% relative) on 20 MIMIC-III tasks and by 8.6 points (29% relative) in zero-shot transfer to 14 eICU tasks, where it uses all events. The transfer gain combines coverage and representation, as MEDIATOR remains the best tokenizer under identical inputs (+1.0 and +1.9 points on operational and subcohort-mortality tasks). Same-encoder controls show that the text encoder drives in-domain accuracy, while the learned objectives of MEDIATOR drive transfer robustness and entity-linking quality. MEDIATOR also enables transferable attribution across hospitals.
