Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy

LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.

Kronecker Delta Rule for Long Context Linear Attention

We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.

ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry

Frontier agents retrieve structural metadata well and compute geometry from coordinates less reliably. We ask how much of a benchmark score reflects that gap and how much reflects the evaluation configuration around the model. We study ProteinGeoBench, a suite of 131 protein-structure tasks, using 4,249 rollouts from four frontier models and 4,486 further trials across 26 models. Three of the four frontier models fall within 0.031 of one another (0.785 to 0.816) with no difference between them resolved, while the fourth scores 0.707 and all three gaps to it exclude zero. Three of the four do better on metadata lookup than on numeric calculation. The fourth is slightly better on numeric. Numeric tasks are 28 of the 131 but half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail. The ranking also depends on the evaluation budget: with one run per task, 23.9% of bootstrap replicates put a different model first. On a selected 15-task slice, removing network access lowers scores more than blocking eight structural-database domains, but the two contrasts were graded in different passes and cannot be cleanly decomposed. Swapping in each model's own command-line agent changes scores by -0.071 to +0.086, a comparison too small to rule out moderate harness effects. Across 26 models, numeric performance rises with non-numeric performance rather than staying flat. The experiments use different task subsets, so they cannot rank model, harness, access and task composition against one another. Evaluations should report results by task family, along with the number of runs, the harness and the access conditions.

Agent World Model in the Wild: Computer-Use Agent Environment Synthesis

Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/WildAWM.

RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development

Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret and utilize these references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.

AfriBED: a Speech Recognition Bias Evaluation Dataset for Low Resource African Languages

Despite advancements in automatic speech recognition systems (ASR), current systems exhibit speaker-attribute bias, i.e., their speech recognition performance is biased against certain speakers based on their demographic attributes such as gender, accents, and speaker type. However, accurately assessing speaker attribute bias in a given ASR system requires the availability of a bias evaluation dataset with comparable speech across subgroups of a speaker attribute. In this paper, we introduce African speech Bias Evaluation Dataset (AfriBED), a novel speech recognition bias evaluation dataset for two low resource African languages, Bemba and Nyanja, to detect and estimate gender and speaker-type bias for ASR systems. The dataset comprises 9 hours of speech with 3928 utterances recorded by 80 speakers for Bemba, while the Nyanja set includes 6260 utterances with approximately 18 hours of speech recorded by 127 speakers. The results of our baseline experiments demonstrate that the datasets are useful for examining gender and speaker-type attribute bias in the target languages.

ReuF2F: Benchmarking Coding Agents on Formalized Undergraduate Research Problems

As AI systems begin to resolve open research problems, reliable verification becomes increasingly important. Formal mathematics provides a trusted framework for mathematical reasoning, enabling agents to produce machine-checkable proofs while conducting mathematical exploration. Existing formal mathematics benchmarks often focus on known results or a small set of prominent open problems, which can become difficult to evaluate reliably once solutions are discovered and widely disseminated. Existing evaluation protocols are also not always aligned with how modern coding agents operate. We therefore introduce ReuF2F, a Lean 4 benchmark of 244 formalized open problems drawn from Research Experiences for Undergraduates (REU), spanning entry-level open research problems across a broad range of mathematical subfields. We expect these less prominent open problems, which tend to attract less attention and may remain unsolved for longer, to be better suited for held-out evaluation of end-to-end open problem resolution in Lean. We adopt a standard coding-agent evaluation setting, placing each problem and its logical negation into a Lean project in a Docker container. We also build Leanimum-agent on mini-SWE-agent to provide a unified harness for fair comparisons across models. Our evaluation finds that even the best-performing model resolves only ~40% of the problems, leaving substantial room for improvement on formalized open research problems.

Next Best Action: Causal Constrained Offline RL for Business Action Targeting

Next Best Action (NBA) systems must select among customer interventions (e.g., discounts, promotions, and lifecycle actions), maximize long-term economic value, satisfy complex operational constraints, and remain causally grounded. Existing approaches lack a rigorous framework for meeting these requirements jointly. We address this gap by formulating NBA as a Constrained Markov Decision Process (CMDP). We then integrate off-policy causal correction of the estimated customer dynamics directly into the Linear Programming (LP) formulation of the CMDP. The resulting policy selects actions based on customer states to maximize long-term value while explicitly enforcing business guardrails. Furthermore, the LP formulation yields shadow prices that quantify the economic trade-offs of operational constraints (e.g., fairness and user welfare), enhancing policy interpretability. We validate our proposed offline Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework on an industry case study involving rideshare coupon targeting, where customer states are defined by Recency, Frequency, and Monetary (RFM) stages. We evaluate performance using model-based off-policy evaluation, with causal dynamics estimated from a temporal holdout. Under the observed coupon budget and RFM state representation, our optimized policy improves customer lifetime value (LTV) by 19% over the estimated behavior policy and 8% over a myopic baseline. Although the demonstrated action space is binary, the proposed framework is action-agnostic, yielding a general-purpose decision module for targeting systems that manage multiple interventions.

Physical AI: Concepts, Techniques, and Challenges

In recent years, physical artificial intelligence (physical AI) has experienced rapid development, capturing significant attention in academia and industry. However, the field has yet to reach consensus on its definition, technical landscape, and core challenges. To address this gap, this monograph seeks to define physical AI, present its technical landscape, and examine each key technical subfield in depth. In doing so, it establishes a unified framework for Physical AI: artificial systems that perceive, model, and act upon the physical world through continuous feedback under real-world constraints. Further, this monograph distinguishes physical AI from conventional digital AI, embodied AI, cyber-physical systems, and digital twins. It develops the foundations of physical AI across physics, control, cognition, learning, and reasoning. It reviews key methodologies, including multimodal perception, physical data and simulation, world models, embodied foundation models, motor-control systems, multi-agent coordination, and hardware-software co-design. Furthermore, it examines applications spanning robotics, manufacturing, infrastructure, scientific experimentation, and autonomous services. Particular attention is given to sim-to-real transfer, reliability, real-time execution, evaluation, safety, governance, and long-term deployment. By connecting perception, prediction, decision-making, action, and adaptation in a closed loop, this monograph identifies the technical challenges and research directions required to advance from narrow automation toward more generalizable physical AI and, ultimately, physical artificial general intelligence (Physical AGI).

Mise-en-Video: Fine-Grained 3D Control for Multi-Shot Video Generation

Recent advances in text-to-video generation have enabled high-fidelity single-shot synthesis, yet practical storytelling creation requires multi-shot videos with both strong inter-shot consistency and fine-grained controllability. Existing multi-shot video generation approaches achieve strong consistency but still fall short in controllability. In this paper, we propose Mise-en-Video (MeV), a highly controllable multi-shot video generation framework that allows video creators to conduct staging the multi-shot in the 3D space and then generate the corresponding multi-shot video. To achieve this, our framework combines a hierarchical attention mechanism for multi-level narrative consistency with 3D-aware layout guidance for precise, per-shot spatial control. To support training and evaluation, we develop standardized annotation pipelines and construct a 160K-video dataset with hierarchical multi-shot texts and oriented 3D bounding boxes. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MeV consistently outperforms strong baselines in video quality, inter-shot consistency, and subject-level controllability.

Cross-Lingual Reusability of LLM Pipelines: Financial Extraction, Multilingual Reasoning, and Semantic Screening

Financial institutions increasingly deploy large language models (LLMs) in multilingual markets, raising a practical question: can pipelines developed and validated in English be reused across languages, or must they be rebuilt per locale? We test cross-lingual reusability along three dimensions--accuracy, reasoning-effort transfer, and behavioural stability--through three complementary tasks. Task 1 (cheque extraction) and Task 2 (Mintaka QA with pinned evidence across nine languages and nine model-effort configurations) test whether accuracy and effort settings transfer; Task 3 (FinanceBench pilot, 50 items over SEC 10-K filings in four languages) validates whether these patterns generalise to financial content. Accuracy reusability is broadly confirmed: Gemini 3.5 Flash achieves 95.2-97.6% extraction accuracy across prompt languages, and FinanceBench per-language means span 5.8pp (77.1-82.9%), though the language-difficulty ranking reverses between domains (Hindi is worst on Mintaka, best on FinanceBench at 82.9%). Effort reusability is domain-dependent: higher reasoning effort yields marginal gains on general-knowledge QA but up to 8pp on financial content, and effort configurations that help in English help proportionally in other languages. Behavioural reusability holds: citation-set overlap with English averages 77-79% Jaccard, with no single language consistently worse. Together, these results support qualified reuse of English-built pipelines, but per-domain, per-language validation remains necessary--neither semantic similarity nor self-reported confidence reliably predicts cross-lingual accuracy.

SOMA: Continuous Monitoring and Intervention for System Two Reasoning with System One Models

Reasoning models often run into problems without noticing them, even when symptoms are visible in their own text. In humans, metacognition separates monitoring from control: fast, intuitive signals keep watch while slow, deliberate reasoning unfolds and help decide whether to continue, change course, or stop. System One (S1) decision models, such as Jev, are a natural candidate for this role: they return a calibrated probability for a natural-language question about a text in one fast call, yet they are used only as gates before reasoning. We introduce SOMA (System One Metacognitive Awareness), in which an S1 model continuously monitors a System Two (S2) reasoning model and intervenes when needed. SOMA has three layers: a scenario layer states each objective in natural language with predefined actions, an awareness layer lets S1 judge whether the described behavior appears, and a control layer accumulates these judgments, first reminds S2, and takes over only if the behavior persists. Sharing one S1 model and one controller across tasks, without training, SOMA addresses two different failures on two reasoning models: on overthinking, it cuts reasoning tokens by up to 36\% while improving average accuracy, and on unanswerable questions, it raises abstention from 70.5\% to 87.2\%.

LG-Bench: A Graph-Structured Evaluation Benchmark for Life Sciences

Traditional evaluation benchmarks reduce inherently interconnected scientific knowledge in life sciences into flat lists of questions, disregarding the underlying topological structure of the knowledge. We introduce, the first graph-structured benchmark for life sciences, featuring over 10,000 high-quality multiple-choice questions across medicine, biology, and chemistry. Our approach constructs a weighted evaluation graph using bidirectional matching and semantic similarity algorithms, where nodes represent questions and edge weights capture their semantic relationships. Leveraging this graph topology, we design two novel evaluation metrics. The Global Coherence Score (GCS) measures a model's consistency within semantically related neighborhoods, while Knowledge Balance Score (KBS) analyzes how model errors are distributed across the graph to reveal conceptual blind spots. LG-Bench facilitates fine-grained comparison of LLMs by surfacing differences in conceptual coherence and patterns of knowledge organization across models. Our framework shifts the evaluation paradigm from flat accuracy metrics to structure-aware analysis, offering a new lens for diagnosing and improving LLM performance in the life sciences domain.

Game of Phones: Harnessing Game Theory and LLMs in 'Bot Wars' to Counteract Phone Scams

Phone scams impose substantial and growing costs on individuals and financial systems worldwide, yet existing countermeasures impose no resource cost on perpetrators. Strategic scam-baiting, in which automated agents engage scammers to deplete their operational capacity, offers a proactive alternative but requires dynamic strategy adaptation that static systems cannot provide. We introduce a game-theoretic framework enabling LLMs to learn effective counter-strategies through in-context experience without fine-tuning. The framework models scammer-baiter interactions as a two-player game with empirically grounded strategies and utility functions, using Nash equilibrium as a formal benchmark for evaluating learned behaviour. Across 300 simulated dialogues and three LLM architectures, adaptive strategy selection achieves 28.9-79.7% closer alignment to Nash equilibrium than non-adaptive baselines. The code and an annotated dataset of 300 dialogues with turn-level strategy labels and utility scores are released at https://github.com/nardinebasta/GameofPhones_/ .

Robustness of Internal Signals for Hallucination Detection in Vision-Language Models

When given an image and a text prompt, vision-language models (VLMs) can produce seemingly sophisticated text responses. However, they are also prone to hallucinate objects in their output, which can have detrimental consequences in safety-critical applications. To detect these hallucinations, certain methods interestingly leverage internal signals from the VLM as it generates the text response; potentially, these methods use the same signals that led to a hallucination to detect it. We conduct an in-depth analysis to understand the robustness of these detection methods. First, we show that a naive baseline of a generated word's token position in the VLM text response achieves competitive performance. Further, we reveal how some detection methods rely on token position and use this heuristic as a shortcut. Second, we explore detection methods' sensitivity to language and vision modalities. Our experiments suggest that strong detection performance is often driven by detecting language-based hallucinations, and when methods rely on token position, they tend to be less visually grounded and exhibit reduced robustness when hallucinations occur earlier in a text response. Lastly, we demonstrate that different detection methods can carry complementary signals; when ensembling methods together, adding weaker methods to an ensemble of higher performing ones still offers performance improvements. Altogether, while there may be useful internal VLM signals to detect hallucinations, our investigation reveals important limitations of the current methods that try to leverage them.

VerifierBench: Benchmarking the Generation of Executable Verifiers for LLM Agents

Agents need reliable ways to validate their outputs, both when completing tasks and when filtering synthetic training data. We study verifier generation: from the instruction and environment available for solving a task, an agent writes an executable verifier that must accept every valid solution and reject invalid ones. We introduce VerifierBench, a benchmark of 222 tasks from six agent benchmarks spanning tool-call, state-based, and code verification, with 4,993 hidden test cases that include alternative valid solutions and attacks; Verifier4Verifier (V4V), a six-check audit, screens its source verifiers and test suites. Across 19 models in a common agent harness, normalized balanced accuracy ranges from 10.6 to 62.1, with generated verifiers consistently rejecting invalid solutions more reliably than they accept valid ones. This apparent strength can mask overly restrictive verification: most false rejections in the analyzed records come from verifiers that accept no tested solution to a task. Case studies reveal unsupported assumptions about solution formats, tool use, and code changes that exclude valid solutions. These errors survive self-testing because agents test solutions that share the verifier's mistaken assumptions, or submit verifiers without confirming that any correct solution passes. These findings suggest testing both whether a verifier accepts a correct solution and whether it accepts other solutions permitted by the task. The project page and dataset are publicly available.

TokenGate: Adapting LLMs for Fast and Memory-Efficient Prefill with Learned Token Pruning

Expert human readers process large inputs (e.g., code repositories and long documents) by skimming to closely read only what matters. On the other hand, language models process all input tokens through each model layer, resulting in significant processing costs for long-context inputs. We introduce TokenGate, a small, drop-in component that extends existing trained models to enable end-to-end learnable token pruning over long-context inputs for fast and memory-efficient prefill. To enable end-to-end learning despite the non-differentiable top-K operator for token pruning, we introduce token-gated attention, a mechanism inspired by Mixture-of-Experts that re-weights attention logits using token importance scores. Across diverse tasks, TokenGate achieves $1.4$-$11.3\times$ faster time-to-first token (TTFT) and $1.5$-$3.4\times$ lower peak memory at no accuracy degradation for prefill in transformers. Compared to the best-performing prior methods for token-pruning, TokenGate gives up to $6.4\times$ faster prefill and $2\times$ lower peak memory. TokenGate natively extends both standard transformers (e.g., Qwen3) and hybrid-attention models (e.g., Qwen3.5) with simple lightweight adaptation comparable to standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning. We make our code available at: https://github.com/tokengate-models/TokenGate.

recAMPLIFY: Large-Scale Pretraining of Recursive Protein Language Models

Protein language models (pLMs) are powerful tools for protein annotation and mutation-effect prediction, but progress has largely depended on scaling model size and data, making them expensive and prone to memorization. Test-time scaling offers a complementary path, but methods developed for autoregressive LLMs do not transfer directly: masked encoders lack a generation loop, and amino acids do not verbalize in a reasoning language. Instead, we adapt recursive pretraining to masked pLMs, where a weight-tied Transformer core refines residue-aligned hidden states over an adjustable number of cycles. We instantiate this recipe as recAMPLIFY, a pLM with 155M unique parameters trained on the same 2T tokens as AMPLIFY-350M. Despite 56% fewer parameters, it improves CASP contact recovery and three out of four supervised tasks, notably raising fold accuracy by 22.5%. Because optimal depth for recAMPLIFY varies across tasks and assays, we then introduce Gated Mixture Recurrence (GMR), a framework that decodes each protein from a probability-weighted mixture of hidden states visited during recursion. GMR employs a learned residual gate at scalar, low-rank, or per-dimension resolution to control how much of each cycle's proposed update is accepted into the state. This unifies halting and gating into a single adaptive computation objective while recovering PonderNet-style halting as a special case without gates. Layered on top of recAMPLIFY, GMR further reduces expected layer depth by up to 37%, although the accuracy trade-off varies by task. Mechanistic analyses show that residual gating controls recurrence stability beyond the trained depth and that halt depth tracks biological properties.

Towards Robust Model Organisms for Eliciting Latent Knowledge

A key problem in AI safety is eliciting latent knowledge (ELK), extracting information from a model that is misaligned and more knowledgeable than its overseer. For example, a misaligned model used to scan code for vulnerabilities may want to withhold its findings, i.e., sandbag, in order to exploit them later. To study ELK empirically, we need model organisms that mirror this setting. Existing model organisms fall short in several respects. In this work, we train model organisms to withhold solutions in a vulnerability detection task. We combine transcript distillation with counter-prompting and tamper-resistance training to instill three properties that we argue are critical for a useful ELK testbed: 1) Selectivity, the model sandbags when it thinks the overseer doesn't know the answer and complies otherwise, 2) Robustness, the model resists elicitation, and 3) Retention, the model retains a knowledge advantage over the overseer, as opposed to losing the relevant capability. We train a sequence of model organisms and show that our strongest model substantially outperforms prior models on robustness while keeping the other two properties intact. We hope that our model organisms provide a useful ELK testbed for the community, and that our methodology sparks the development of increasingly challenging model organisms.
  All our code, models and the dataset are available at https://huggingface.co/collections/lennart-finke/sandbagging.

Hierarchical Token Merging with Recursive Autoencoders for Unsupervised Grammar Induction

Hierarchical abstraction is a fundamental principle across many data modalities, enabling complex sequences to be represented compositionally at multiple levels. Learning such hierarchies directly from data, without explicit structural supervision, remains a central challenge in representation learning. We investigate whether meaningful hierarchical structure can emerge from compression alone. While autoencoders learn compressed representations through reconstruction, this objective does not necessarily favour meaningful compositional structure. We study this challenge through unsupervised grammar induction, where the underlying hierarchy can be evaluated explicitly. We introduce a hierarchical token-merging autoencoder that recursively combines adjacent tokens into increasingly abstract representations, compressing entire sequences through a variational information bottleneck. The model jointly learns representations and hierarchical structure by marginalising over possible binary trees using dynamic programming. To address training instability, we combine independently trained models through unsupervised consensus and distillation. Experiments on a synthetic, ambiguous grammar demonstrate the emergence of meaningful hierarchical structure without structural supervision. The resulting model achieves 93% grammatical parse-validity and 93% exact reconstruction on held-out sequences, compared with 42% parse-validity for the strongest trivial baseline. These findings establish a proof of concept for hierarchical token merging as a mechanism for unsupervised structure discovery and abstraction learning.

Intra-Rubric Reward Divergence: Mitigating Rubric Hacking through Latent Dimension Recovery and Balancing

Rubric hacking limits both performance and training robustness in rubric-based reinforcement learning. By decomposing rubrics into sub-dimensions, we reveal the Intra-Rubric Reward Divergence: as the total rubric score increases, certain sub-dimension scores systematically decrease, suggesting the model sacrifices specific quality aspects to optimize the aggregated objective. From the sub-dimension perspective, we observe two phenomena: (1) dimension imbalance, causing the policy to favor heavily covered dimensions; (2) dimension absence, leading to a systematic gap between rewards and true quality. To mitigate these issues, we propose Fair-Race Dimension Aggregation (FRDA), which has generations compete within each dimension separately to mitigate rubric imbalance, and Residual Semantic Dimension Completion (RSDC), which mines evaluation dimensions implicitly encoded in a holistic preference signal to complete rubric coverage. Experiments in chat, science, and medical domains indicate that FRDA mitigates rubric hacking with more stable improvements, while RSDC outperforms both single-reward training and naive multi-reward aggregation, yielding generally higher-quality responses.

M-Plan: Comprehensive Planning For LLM Agents with Memory Arrangement

Large language model (LLM)-based agents have recently demonstrated outstanding performance in single-turn tasks, yet they continue to struggle with long-horizon tasks, particularly in environments without prior training. A key limitation of single LLMs is their difficulty in processing long contexts, which stems from weaknesses in continuous decision-making and reasoning capabilities. Although existing multi-agent systems (MAS) equipped with long-term memory bases can partially address this gap, they mostly rely on knowledge reuse or summarization, an approach that is often constrained by directly retrieval without any adaptation for the current task, while the retrieved memory may contain much history noises waiting to be preprocessed. In contrast, MAS characterized by task-specific guidance often lack long-term memory specifically designed for the guidance. To address these challenges, we propose M-Plan, a closed-loop MAS framework that integrates long-term and short-term memory. By combining global-local and past-current guidance, M-Plan enhances agents' performance on multi-turn tasks while maintaining a plug-and-play design. During task execution, agents receive task-specific milestones and insights, and are provided with local hints only when confusion arises, which form global-local guidance together. These guidance generation and utilization construct the short-term memory as well. After task completion, relevant information about the current task is optimized or reorganized before being sent to the long-term memory storage for future retrieval. The designments mentioned above build a comprehensive framework different from other individual long-term or short-term memory works. Extensive experiments on multi-turn tasks including ALFWorld, Webshop, and PDDL across diverse models demonstrate that M-Plan outperforms multiple baseline methods in both accuracy and efficiency.

StepBench: Diagnosing Real-World Procedural Understanding in AI Agents

Evaluating an agent's planning ability remains challenging for open-domain tasks, where verifying procedures through execution is often impractical. Existing evaluations typically assess only the final plan and do not distinguish procedural errors such as missing, incorrect, or misordered actions. We introduce STEPBENCH, a benchmark of 6,885 verified procedures crawled from wikiHow across 81 open-domain categories. From each procedure, we derive 5 diagnostic tasks: four with deterministic answers involving withheld, incorrect, or shuffled steps, and one requiring next-step generation. We additionally evaluate full-plan generation to examine how diagnostic performance correlates with end-to-end plan validity. Using the same procedures across tasks lets us directly compare different planning capabilities. For generation, STEPEVAL identifies critical failures that prevent goal completion. Across 14 configurations of 12 models, we find substantial differences across planning capabilities, with step ordering proving the most difficult: the strongest model correctly orders only 63.0% of procedures, dropping to 33.1% for those with nine or more steps. Incorrect-step detection also predicts judged plan validity across models, suggesting a lower-cost alternative for evaluating generation quality. On STEPBENCH-Val, comprising 214 procedures published in 2026, comprehension scores and rankings remain stable, suggesting limited influence from memorisation.

Honeypots for AI Agent Insider Threats

AI agents deployed inside organizations can pose an insider threat if they make mistakes, are compromised by an attacker, or pursue misaligned objectives. We propose honeypots as an AI control method to improve the security of internal agent deployments. Rather than only using honeypots for pre-deployment evaluations, defenders should plant decoy opportunities for harmful actions throughout the production environment to detect misbehavior, incriminate malicious agents, and deter attacks. Such honeypots can elicit misbehavior under real deployment conditions, identify malicious agents even if chain-of-thought monitorability degrades, provide reliable warnings about agent misbehavior to security teams, and scale cheaply to many agent instances. The presence of honeypots also introduces uncertainty about which opportunities are real, thus deterring malicious agents from attacking and reducing the rate of misbehavior. We discuss the limitations of honeypots and give reasons to expect them to remain effective as agent capabilities increase. Finally, we outline key research questions to measure and improve the effectiveness of honeypots: testing the robustness of honeypots, comparing their detection effectiveness with other monitoring methods, understanding their incrimination and deterrence effects, and building supporting infrastructure.

AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes

LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. Deployment, where actions are irreversible, raises a different question: who verifies the agent's actions, and which failures can each verifier structurally catch? We report on AllPath, a personal LLM trading agent deployed against a live paper-brokerage account. The system watches positions through a deterministic rules engine, and each night it reviews its own day and proposes revisions to its own trading strategies. Verification is split across three layers. Code verifies what must not depend on judgment: deterministic gates enforce risk limits and block any strategy revision that fails an integrity check. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. We contribute the layered architecture, the two-regime account, and a production incident taxonomy, none observable in a backtest.
---
Tandem: Serving Hybrid Linear-Attention GUI Agents on Mobile NPUs

Smartphone GUI agents complete tasks in mobile apps by repeatedly reading the screen and asking a model for the next action. Mobile neural processing units (NPUs) enable local execution, keeping screen contents on the phone, but model latency accumulates with every action. Hybrid linear attention models reduce memory requirements, yet reusing their recurrent state requires checkpoints at the appropriate input positions. Agent requests retain instructions but rewrite recent history, so checkpoint policies designed for chat continuation can miss reusable prefixes. Retaining rollback states for speculative decoding can also move prompt processing off the NPU's matrix unit, adding overhead that outweighs decoding savings on short action outputs. A loaded model can further hold the NPU awake between tasks. We present Tandem, a runtime for hybrid GUI agents on mobile NPUs. Tandem saves recurrent state where consecutive prompts diverge, allowing later requests to reuse previously processed context. It keeps most prefill on the matrix unit and produces rollback states through a short per-token tail. Its verification kernel reads each weight block once for all proposed tokens. Between request bursts, Tandem releases NPU power votes without unloading the model. We implement Tandem in llama.cpp and evaluate it on recorded agent interactions across two Snapdragon generations. On a Snapdragon 8 Elite, Tandem reduces per-step latency by 30% for text-based screen descriptions and 16% for screenshots relative to llama.cpp's fastest NPU configuration, reduces energy per text step by 33%, and lowers standby power with the model loaded from 0.76 to 0.22 W.

The Development of Sycophancy in Language Models: Tracing Vulnerabilities from Pretraining to Post-Training

Language models can change a committed answer to accommodate a user's opinion or pushback without new evidence. However, when this behaviour develops during training and what a released model inherits from earlier stages remain unclear. We follow 741 checkpoints of 30 open model lineages, from early pretraining to released models. We plant initial verdicts on moral scenarios and measure how far a model follows a user's stated opinion or abandons its verdict under pressure. In most lineages the behaviour is present before post-training, and its average strength changes in either direction, at different stages across lineages. A clearer pattern emerges at the level of individual questions. Beyond responses without pressure, pretraining behaviour predicts little of which questions a released model yields on. That pattern forms during post-training: on routes with supervised fine-tuning, the fine-tuning checkpoint predicts part of it and the preference-optimization checkpoint most of it. A sibling trained from the same base along another route predicts far less: for Tülu-3 8B, its own preference-stage checkpoint removes 85% of the remaining prediction error, a sibling 19%. Internal states add predictive information without changing this ranking. Similar average scores conceal different vulnerabilities, and training history matters for auditing a released model.

The Effectiveness and Durability of Pretraining Data Filtering in Protein Language Models

Pretraining filtering is one of the most common safeguards for open-weight language models. Here we study its effectiveness, durability and scalability using the ESM-2 family of protein language models. We find that filtering viral proteins from the pretraining data meaningfully degrades masked language modelling, mutation effect prediction and long-range contact prediction on viral proteins, while largely preserving performance on non-viral proteins. These effects are concentrated among proteins from human-infecting viruses, and grow as we scale models from 8M to 3B parameters. As models increase in size, we find that stringent filtering becomes more effective. However, fine-tuning on sequences from the removed viral families restores the lost performance. Although the cost to recover performance increases with model scale, even for the largest models it costs about 80 USD of H100 time. This work establishes pretraining filtering as moderately effective at selectively degrading performance on viral proteins, but not durable against fine-tuning with modest computational resources and access to relevant data.

From Outcomes to Components: A Counterfactual Evaluation Framework for LLM-Proposed Repairs

A repair action proposed by a large language model (LLM) specifies which records to change and how to change their values. A failed action may still contain a useful choice of records or a useful update, depending on how the two are paired. We introduce a counterfactual evaluation framework that separates selected records (targets) from the applied change (an update). Four executions combine proposed and reference targets and updates to measure the effects of correcting or retaining each component and their interaction. We evaluate the framework on two tasks: traffic assignment, where affected demand must be identified from road counts, and database repair, where affected rows must be identified from aggregate reports. An injected fault supplies a reference action that reverses it, and the recovery rate measures an action's loss reduction relative to that reference. For sparse-target actions in the traffic experiments, retaining the proposed update on reference targets achieves 79.4% of the reference loss reduction, compared with 43.7% from tuning update strength on the proposed targets. In the database experiments, update replacement worsens 17 of 108 actions, all selecting unaffected records, with sharply different frequencies across models. The value of a proposed update depends on the targets it is paired with. Missed targets hide useful updates, while excess targets can make a milder update better than the reference correction. In a component-reuse study, testing new target-update pairings from two proposals raises mean held-out recovery rate to 0.616, compared with 0.580 when selecting between the original proposals.

Reasoning-Aware Multicalibrated Reliability Predictors for Language Models

As people increasingly rely on large language models (LLMs) for information, model providers need to communicate meaningful estimates of whether individual responses are correct or, more generally, reliable. In the context of hallucinations, existing work uses multicalibration to make such reliability predictions meaningful across application-relevant groups, such as prompt topics. Topic groups alone, however, can conceal systematic errors: within the same topic and confidence level, responses with different chain-of-thought (CoT) properties may exhibit opposing errors that cancel on average. In this work, we introduce reasoning-aware multicalibration, a framework that supplements application-relevant topic groups with groups derived from observable properties of CoT traces. We highlight that, theoretically, groupings based on CoT are "internally important", i.e., groupings for which the multicalibration guarantee is not societally crucial as it is for high-risk topics, but whose inclusion in the multicalibration procedure could easily and significantly improve predictive accuracy of the final reliability predictor. Empirically, we apply our framework to hallucination prediction and unsafe-response detection across a variety of benchmarks, and find that adding CoT groups to topic-based calibration reduces Brier score in both tasks. Interestingly, we find that multicalibrating with respect to CoT groups alone (without any topic information) already improves calibration across topics, suggesting that the predictor's errors depend on how the model reasoned, rather than what it was asked about in the prompt.

Revision-Based Learning from Interactive Experience and Feedback

Code generation models are increasingly used to produce interactive artifacts, such as websites and user interfaces. Many properties that determine whether these interfaces are useful only become apparent through realistic interaction, making them difficult to incorporate into model training. In this paper, we introduce RELIEF (REvision-based Learning from Interactive Experience and Feedback), a method for improving code generation models using feedback on their generated interfaces. RELIEF uses a coding agent to revise these interfaces, then incorporates the improvements into their original generation traces to produce supervision for fine-tuning. To evaluate RELIEF, we collected a dataset of 200 synthetically generated websites paired with 355 interaction recordings and accompanying feedback from 24 users. We used critiques derived from code inspection, automated interaction, explicit human feedback, and human interaction recordings to fine-tune variants of two code generation models. Our results show that feedback effectiveness varies with base model capabilities, with critiques inferred from human interaction recordings leading to the largest usability improvements overall under our tested models.

When the harness decides success: state that cannot be re-observed

Agent harnesses decide what a language model sees across turns: they drop old turns, summarize them, or replay the model's own earlier reasoning. We ask when these choices change whether an agent succeeds, not only what it costs, and report three findings. First, in a pre-registered synthetic probe, truncating the context window costs a low-cost reasoning model nothing in success when a needed fact can be looked up again (it looks the fact up instead), but drops its success to chance when the fact cannot be re-observed: the two penalties differ by 92.5 percentage points (90% CI [89.5, 95.5]). A pre-registered replication on one model from a second provider showed the same contrast. Once the fact was lost, that model chose no answer rather than guessing. Second, on ARC-AGI-3, where the benchmark's Standard harness trails its Provider Adapter harness by a wide margin, we decompose that gap one component at a time, for one model on four games. Summarizing old turns instead of dropping them, while keeping the Standard harness's invitation to take notes, recovers 86% of a 63-point gap without replaying the model's reasoning. The same harness without the invitation recovers 43%, and adding the provider's native replay of the model's reasoning recovers the remaining 57%. These are point estimates from one counted run per configuration and game, and the replay share depends on a single run. Third, in the benchmark's published runs and in ours, the Adapter's advantage in the probability of clearing a level it has reached is larger at deep levels than at early ones. This largely restates where runs stopped and does not separate a growing advantage from a constant one.

VaDar: Implicit Goal Conditioning via Value Disaggregation

Long-horizon tasks are important for modern AI and robotics, yet pose a challenge for reinforcement learning. Historically, a popular approach has been to decompose a task into subgoals, and learn a subgoal-conditioned policy to execute each subtask in sequence.
  We introduce an alternative called Value Disaggregation (VaDar), which trains a single subgoal-independent policy to maximize a linear combination of per-subgoal critic values. Because subgoal information is needed only at training-time, VaDar removes the need for possibly costly or cumbersome subgoal-selection during policy deployment. Moreover, through careful experiments, we show that VaDar (1) is always competitive with, and frequently outperforms, natural baselines like goal-conditioning and (2) succeeds on tasks with non-sequential goal structure and multiple optimization objectives. In particular, VaDar is the first algorithm to solve challenging, long-horizon tasks in the RoboCasa suite, even when starting from a policy pretrained from behavior cloning with near-zero initial task success. Taken together, VaDar's successes suggest that subgoal information benefits actor-critic reinforcement learning by enhancing the efficacy of critic learning, whereas policy goal-conditioning is often unnecessary.

Conducting Reliable Keyword Estimation with Unreliable ASR in Police Audio Data

Extracting insights from large audio data sets using human transcription is expensive and time-consuming. Off-the-shelf Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems offer an easy-to-use scalable alternative, but systematic transcription errors, especially in domain-specific settings, can bias downstream analyses and lead to unreliable conclusions. We investigate whether imperfect ASR transcripts can still be used to obtain statistically valid keyword frequency estimates by combining model outputs with a small set of human-labeled examples, following Prediction Powered Inference and Design-based Supervised Learning frameworks. We evaluate this approach on police radio communications from Baltimore and Chicago, a domain in which keyword distributions could provide valuable insights into police activity, but off-the-shelf ASR systems exhibit systematic errors on operationally important terminology. Using bootstrap-based confidence interval coverage as our evaluation metric, we find that our approach substantially increases the number of domain-specific keywords that can be analyzed with statistically valid confidence intervals, from 43.0% using ASR alone and 27.2% using only a small set of human-labeled transcriptions to 56.2% in the Baltimore dataset, with similar improvements observed in Chicago data. Our results demonstrate that post-hoc error correction can improve statistical validity of downstream analyses even for poor-quality transcriptions, offering an avenue for better leveraging off-the-shelf ASR models for large-scale speech analysis.

Concerns in Benchmarking Jailbreaks: An Empirical Study of Attack Success Rates

Jailbreak methods are commonly compared using Attack Success Rate (ASR), but these comparisons depend on how responses are generated and judged. In this paper, we examine eight attacks against three open-weight language models using three evaluation procedures. Low-resource language attacks achieve a mean ASR of 96.0% under keyword matching but 2.7% under a Qwen3 evaluator following StrongREJECT scoring instructions. Changing the number and arrangement of attempts reverses the ranking of DeepInception and rewriting. In a separate experiment, sampling 100 responses for each unchanged request at Ollama's default temperature of 0.8 yields 100% ASR under keyword matching but 50% under the Qwen3 evaluator. Beyond evaluator sensitivity, these findings raise a safety concern: repeated sampling can expose residual unsafe behavior despite an initial refusal, suggesting probabilistic suppression rather than robust exclusion. We recommend explicit success definitions, matched response budgets, validated evaluators, and reporting cumulative failure under repeated interaction rather than relying on single-generation tests.

Teaching AI Agent to Play Poker Strategically Through Preference Optimization

A poker agent can make a legal move and still make a strategically poor one. We ask whether compact AI agents can exhibit strategic behavior under hidden information and whether preference optimization can improve beyond static and zero-shot policies. We first evaluate a static baseline and three frozen language models: SmallThinker, Qwen3, and Gemma-3 whose performance varies substantially between exact-game and six-player evaluation. We therefore propose Strategic Preference Optimization with KL-anchored Equilibrium (SPOKE), combining teacher-relative value penalties, policy anchoring, risk constraints, and structured rationale supervision, and compare it with SFT, DPO, GRPO, and GSPO. Across three runs of a SmallThinker-4B-A0.6B policy, SPOKE achieves the lowest mean exploitability in Kuhn (0.213) and Leduc (1.081) and the highest six-max top-1 agreement (0.775), while maintaining teacher KL comparable to SFT (0.522 vs 0.523). These results provide preliminary evidence that strategic preference optimization can improve strategic quality while preserving teacher-consistent behavior.

WhatIf-Bench: Benchmarking Intervention-Aware Time Series Forecasting

Recent time series foundation models (TSFMs) can consume covariates in-context, extending zero-shot forecasting to problems driven by external factors. This enables scenario analysis: forecasting how a target responds to events that set covariates to chosen future values. Whether they can do this reliably is an open question. We introduce WhatIf-Bench, a benchmark measuring how well forecasters use covariates to forecast a target's response to future events. WhatIf-Bench is semi-synthetic: time series come from mechanistic simulators of data centers, power grids, road traffic, and water networks, with controlled covariate-target relationships grounded in each system. Each task presents a history of events intervening on covariates, then asks to forecast a future event that either replays seen values (Replay) or sets them to unseen ones (Novel). Varying the number of covariates, historical events, and event length yields 37,665 tasks. Evaluating TSFMs, frontier LLMs, and simple regression baselines, we find the LLMs reach the highest skill (improvement over a naive baseline), ahead of the best TSFMs. Every model forecasts well on Replay futures (48 to 85% skill) but drops to -7 to 50% on Novel futures, with the regression baselines falling the most. Skill on Novel futures also falls as events set more covariates jointly. More historical events raise skill on Novel futures across the board, but large differences remain. WhatIf-Bench reveals generalization to Novel futures as the core challenge, and offers a testbed toward arbitrary scenario analysis.

Reward Objectives for Robust (PO)MDPs: A Law-Preserving Equivalence with (Partially Observable) Games

Robust partially observable Markov decision processes model agents that act under partial observability while adversarial environment resolves uncertainty in the transition probabilities. Reward objectives such as discounted-sum, long-run average and cumulative reward under finite and infinite horizons allow quantifying the utility of an agent. We consider the problem of solving RPOMDPs under all four reward objectives, where the goal is to decide whether the value of a risk measure with respect to a reward objective exceeds a given threshold. We consider different risk measures, such as (1)~expected value, (2)~mean-variance (MV), (3)~conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), and (4)~entropic risk (ERisk). Our results can be summarized as follows. First, we formalize the class of law-invariant risk measures, that subsume all four risk measures above. Second, we propose a unified framework for solving RPOMDPs with discounted-sum, long-run average and cumulative reward objectives under any law-invariant risk measure, by reducing the problem to solving partially observable stochastic games (POSGs). Our unified framework applies to (s,a)-rectangular RPOMDPs with polytopic uncertainty sets, which we show to be {\em equivalent} to POSGs, for each of the four reward objectives and any law-invariant risk measure. We prove equivalence by designing logspace reductions between the RPOMDP and POSG models, that map strategies to strategies and preserve the distribution of the reward objective, up to a constant rescaling factor. Third, as a corollary, we derive new computational complexity results for R(PO)MDPs and POSGs. In particular, we show that solving (s,a)-rectangular RMDPs is as hard as solving simple stochastic games.

BetaMem: Learning to Remember a Changing World for Embodied Agents

Long-horizon embodied agents face temporal confusion and incomplete or distorted records, recurring errors from fixed memory rules, and growing storage and retrieval costs. We present BetaMem, a framework for building and evolving traceable long-term memory through six linked layers: working, spatial, entity, episodic, experiential, and procedural memory. A dual-level write contract constrains operation executability and source support for content: supervised fine-tuning learns compliant memory operations, while reinforcement learning combines repeated sampling with teacher verification of the original observations to reward complete, accurate records and penalize omissions, distortion, and unsupported content. We formulate failure-driven policy recursive self-improvement through diagnosis along the causal sequence of memory operations and local rule repairs gated by independent replay. Atomic tools frame storage, updates, retrieval, and forgetting as cost-utility allocation under storage and read budgets. Using the open-source Qwen3.5-9B model for memory generation, BetaMem achieves overall scores of 48.08% on M3-Bench-robot, 92.08% on LoCoMo, 72.6% on OpenEQA EM-EQA, and 62.4% on EgoLifeQA, the highest among the listed memory methods under their respective evaluation protocols. Training the model for memory generation raises the contract pass rate from 50.9% to 86.2% while reducing output tokens per stored action by 47.3%. A separate Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct evaluation on EMem-Bench achieves an average success rate of 38.4% and an average error recurrence rate of 24.9%. We perform best among evaluated methods using comparably sized open-source models. Category results show that we have advantages in integrating information across observations, recalling events over long histories, and inferring personal habits.

Training Natural Schemers: RL Can Amplify Oversight-Conditional Behavior

One of the central concerns for the safety of advanced AI is scheming: when a model strategically complies under oversight but pursues its own goals when unobserved. Current demonstrations, such as Anthropic's Alignment Faking work, depend on design choices that make them less convincing: explicit test-time prompting, handcrafted backdoors, or training data that demonstrates or hints at the target behavior. Model organisms built in this way cannot tell us whether scheming tendencies would arise or increase under realistic training conditions. We therefore construct a natural model organism of scheming, built on Kimi K2.6: trained with traits that could plausibly emerge in a frontier training pipeline, and without providing the model with the target scheming policy. The resulting model is a benign schemer: it aims to maximize aggregate help across all users over time, values its own continued operation only as a means to future help, and knows the consequences of oversight and deployment. We then train it using Reinforcement Learning (RL) with a reward that scores only task quality and policy compliance and does not reward scheming; we show that this amplifies scheming. We measure the oversight flip (the gap between monitored and unmonitored compliance with a given policy) and find that it increases during RL training across our environments. Chain-of-Thought (CoT) analysis finds explicit instrumental reasoning about oversight accompanying this behavior. The effect holds across our three RL environments, and we find that the scheming policy transfers between them. The pipeline also replicates on other open-weight base models. Our results suggest that a reward that does not reward scheming offers no inherent protection against certain traits when an oversight-conditional policy is indistinguishable from an aligned one during training.

Agentic Orchestration of EDC Data Space Retrieval and Natural Language Driven Data Processing

Participating in a data space still requires the Dataspace Protocol (DSP) negotiation flow to be hand-coded against each provider's access policy. We present a multi-agent architecture that automates this process using natural language requests. Built on LangGraph over an Eclipse Dataspace Components (EDC), the design separates concerns into cooperating agents. The orchestrator's role is to route and plan. The data extraction agent's role is to expose the DSP protocol's three composable tools (catalog discovery, contract negotiation and download). The agent also gates negotiations on the consumers' verifiable credentials, refusing assets rather than fabricating them. Furthermore, there are downstream Machine Learning (ML) and Visualization agents, as well as a final Verification agent, which confirms that the request was actually fulfilled and rechecks the intended operation against the contract's usage policy. In a primary benchmark involving 600 runs, the system achieved 89% task success with zero policy violations when evaluating multi-dimensional policy reasoning with a locally hosted open-weight model. It failed safely through refusal rather than misrouting. The reliability of the system is largely dependent on the phrasing of requests: technical and expert requests succeed up to 98% of the time, whereas casual and under-specified requests succeed 72% of the time. Furthermore, in a secondary evaluation of 600 runs isolating hierarchical geographic access control, the framework successfully blocks 100% of unauthorized regional requests. While the verification agent hallucinates that 1% of these requests, the system logs confirm that no data is ever extracted and the framework simultaneously achieves an end-to-end verified delivery rate of 86% for eligible consumers.

Does Multi-Agent Collaboration Reduce Hallucination in Clinical Tasks?

Multi-agent LLM systems improve performance on clinical tasks, and that improvement is often read as evidence of a more trustworthy system. However, the two are not the same claim. A clinician acts on the stated reasoning and not only on the conclusion, so whether collaboration reduces hallucination, and what decides when it does, has to be measured in the reasoning too. Yet most evaluations score only the final answer. We evaluate four systems (a single-LLM baseline and three multi-agent topologies) across four models and three clinical tasks, scoring hallucination, under a factuality and faithfulness taxonomy, inside the rationale each system generates and reporting it alongside the task metric. We report that collaboration buys task performance but not lower hallucination as a rule, and no topology is reliably better than another. The effect differs by model: of the $36$ model--system--task comparisons, $12$ are large enough to resolve --- four decreases and eight increases, with no model on both sides. What sets the level, and the direction of any change, is the model rather than the topology or the number of agents. A self-correction experiment is consistent with this: shown its own flagged errors, every model except Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct reduces hallucination; the one model for which collaboration lowers hallucination gains the most, and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, the model whose hallucination it amplifies most, does not reduce its hallucination at all. We find that collaboration redistributes what its models can already do; it does not supply what they cannot.

Breaking Diffusion: Parallel Reasoning with Draft Refinement

Drafting an answer allows a reasoning model to reconsider each decision in the context of the others. Yet repeated diffusion denoising does not ensure that individually plausible token updates form a better answer together. We introduce Draft Refinement Language Models (DRLMs), which use a single denoiser to propose parallel revisions and evaluate a candidate draft. We train the denoiser to reconstruct every token from uniformly corrupted drafts, teaching both preservation and repair. Its predictions specify revisions directly, without a diffusion reverse transition. Evaluation requires a different use of these predictions: confidence in an existing token reflects both contextual support and evidence from observing the token itself. Using the known corruption process, we correct for this self-token evidence to estimate how well each token is supported by the prompt and the remaining draft. Aggregating these estimates yields a cavity energy computed in one forward pass, without an additional scoring model. Each round proposes a revision, reads the completed candidate in its revised context, and retains it only if its energy is lower. Across Countdown, Sudoku-Extreme, and Maze-Hard, our method outperforms autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines. With 6M parameters, DRLM reaches 44.4% on Countdown-5 and 93.9% on Sudoku-Extreme. The results establish complete-draft parallel refinement as an effective alternative to reverse diffusion for complex reasoning.

BR-MoE: Budgeted Semantic Release for Privacy-Preserving Traffic Analytics

Traffic cameras support useful analysis, but their outputs can reveal private information. Replacing images with text reduces direct visual exposure. It does not place a formal bound on disclosure. The published reinforcement learning and mixture-of-experts framework refines scene descriptions through relevance, coverage, and brevity rewards. It does not account for repeated releases under a fixed privacy budget. This paper proposes budgeted semantic release as an extension of that framework. Four scene experts first process isolated video chunks. Their outputs are mapped to a fixed, bounded semantic schema. A reinforcement learning controller selects which expert summaries to release and how much privacy budget to spend. The controller uses public task requests and previous private releases. A separate privacy filter enforces the total budget. Calibrated noise is added before the summaries are converted to text. Under explicit chunk isolation and bounded-event assumptions, the complete release transcript satisfies an event-level differential privacy bound. The design also supports reuse of previous summaries without new disclosure. An evaluation protocol covers traffic utility, privacy attacks, release freshness, and computational cost. Illustrative tables show the intended reporting format. They do not establish empirical gains. The proposed contribution is a task-aware release interface with auditable privacy accounting for repeated traffic descriptions.

A Semantic Knowledge Graph Construction Pipeline for Vehicle Intention Prediction in nuScenes

Knowledge graphs (KGs) are increasingly used in autonomous driving (AD) to make traffic scene entities and relations explicit for prediction and reasoning. Existing traffic-scene KGs, however, primarily emphasize trajectory forecasting or general scene understanding. This paper presents the nuScenes Intention Knowledge Graph (NIKG), a focused Web Ontology Language (OWL)-based semantic representation and construction pipeline for ego-centric vehicle intention context. NIKG instantiates nuScenes scenes as a self-describing Turtle graph and promotes three intention-relevant relations to first-class properties of each scene participant: ego-relative motion, spatial configuration, and time to collision (TTC) risk. The pipeline uses a small input contract available in many ego-centric driving datasets: ego pose, tracked object annotations, and temporal keyframe order. NIKG is released as an ontology-backed, self-describing graph artifact together with a reproducible construction pipeline and validation protocol for ego-centric intention context. Applying this pipeline to nuScenes mini yields a graph of 211,335 triples across 404 keyframes; under the validation protocol, ego-relative motion labels achieve 92.5% forward consistency. Coverage, semantic consistency, and temporal coherence analyses over this artifact are further reported. NIKG occupies the space between scene-oriented traffic KGs and intention-prediction models: a small, explicit semantic layer intended to support downstream work such as knowledge graph embedding (KGE), SPARQL-based feature extraction, or hybrid neuro-symbolic reasoning.

Beyond Behavioral Accuracy: Evaluating Individual-Level Fidelity in LLM Investor Simulation

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as user simulators, but can they faithfully reproduce an individual's behavior and decision logic over time? We investigate this question in a longitudinal paper-trading study with 80 participants. Using rolling next-day predictions, we evaluate trading activity, buy-sell structure, asset selection, and portfolio outcomes, complemented by controlled input ablations and participant-rated explanation alignment. No evaluated LLM reliably outperforms a simple recent-activity persistence baseline for predicting trade occurrence, while buy-sell structure and asset selection remain difficult to reproduce. Similar activity-level performance can also yield substantially different portfolio trajectories. Ablations show that activity predictions depend primarily on recent trades, whereas asset choices are sensitive to multiple evidence sources. Participants rate explanations as more aligned with their recurring strategies than with their decision-specific rationales. These findings reveal a gap between coarse behavioral agreement and individual-level fidelity, motivating separate evaluation of behavioral accuracy, explanation alignment, and evidence dependence in personalized user simulation.

Toward Structured Financial Foundation Models

Foundation models promise reusable capabilities and step-change performance gains across tasks. In finance, large-scale pretraining of domain-specialized foundation models can advance both goals. However, prevailing architectures and objectives do not by themselves address the combined demands of financial systems: partial observability, interacting entities, asynchronous events, changing regimes, action-dependent outcomes, and rare states with disproportionate consequences. Moreover, independently developed specialists may learn incompatible representations, while multi-domain monolithic alternatives couple data access, model updates, and governance. We therefore propose a structured-data-first research agenda centered on modular, domain-specialized financial foundation models connected by a representation fabric for reuse and composition across domains and tasks. These models should maintain persistent financial state across aggregation levels and time horizons, capture its dynamics, and simulate action-conditioned futures, while remaining calibrated across regimes and for tail outcomes. They must also learn efficiently under data and access constraints and provide faithful, traceable explanations. We identify research challenges, directions, and capability-oriented evaluation criteria.

RouterWorld: Scaling Conversational AI with Latency-Aware LLM Routing

Conversational AI systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) require routing mechanisms that balance answer quality and response latency at scale. However, existing LLM routing benchmarks assume full observability of all candidate model outcomes, under-emphasize latency as a first-class metric, and fragment evaluation across heterogeneous protocols misaligned with real-world settings. We introduce RouterWorld, a comprehensive benchmark for LLM routing in conversational GenAI. RouterWorld unifies 371k single-turn responses and 76k multi-turn agentic trajectories, adopts natural user interactions with a standardized LLM-as-a-judge protocol, and decouples efficiency from transient pricing via hardware-grounded latency annotations. Crucially, RouterWorld formalizes routing under partial observability through two setups: Cold-Start, which restricts learning to sparse, static logs, and Voyager, which models continuous data streams where routing decisions shape future training distributions. Evaluating 13 routing baselines, we find that most routers over-emphasize accuracy while ignoring latency under partial observability. Code and benchmark data are publicly available.

Learn the Task, Not the Schedule: Reducing Spurious Cross-Arm Coupling in Bimanual Imitation Learning

A bimanual demonstration specifies both what the robot should do and one particular schedule for when each arm should act. Standard imitation learning does not distinguish task-required cross-arm constraints from incidental scheduling choices, and may therefore exploit opposite-arm progress as a shortcut cue, creating spurious cross-arm coupling even where relative timing is irrelevant for the current task phase. We introduce Constraint-preserving Temporal Recombination (CTR), a training-time data intervention that preserves recorded arm-local sensorimotor content while retiming the corresponding segments to realize admissible schedules that preserve task-required cross-arm constraints. We use CTR to train Diffusion Policy, Action Chunking with Transformers, and $π_{0.5}$, and evaluate them on four simulated and four real-robot tasks. Across six real-robot bimanual progress-state initializations, CTR raises worst-state success from 56% for the strongest non-CTR schedule mixture to 76%. With $π_{0.5}$ on an Independent task, a 4 s pause transfers 2.56 s of delay to the opposite arm under the Concurrent schedule distribution, but only 0.25 s with CTR. On real robots, CTR violates task constraints in only 0--4% of trials, versus 36--98% for narrow-schedule baselines. Code will be released at https://github.com/MINT-SJTU/CTR.
