ScopeMem: Latent Memory That Learns Where to Revise Frozen GUI Agents

Latent memory is gaining ground as a lightweight history supplement for GUI agents. It supplies history to a frozen vision-language model via prefix vectors trained with step-wise cross-entropy (CE). While resulting gains are naturally attributed to encoded history, our controlled experiments show they primarily stem from action revision. The prefix learns to alter the model's default actions. However, this revision is unscoped because step-wise CE pushes outputs toward reference actions at every step without explicitly preserving correct decisions. Consequently, it repairs errors but also breaks 8-13% of initially correct answers. Overall accuracy metrics hide these losses. We therefore propose ScopeMem, a latent-memory method for scoped revision. Its encoder conditions prefix generation on the model's initial answer and is trained to correct errors while preserving correct decisions. On the controlled ablation set, ScopeMem reduces the share of broken correct decisions to 5.6% while repairing more errors than any evaluated CE-trained prefix. On AndroidControl, GUI-Odyssey, and zero-shot AITW, ScopeMem outperforms all evaluated prompting and latent-memory baselines, enabling a frozen Qwen3-VL-8B to match or surpass GUI-specialized agents and Qwen3-VL-32B.
---
Mitigating Reward Hacking in Preference Reward Models with Evolving Penalties

Reinforcement learning for language models has moved from preference reward models to verifiable rewards, which sustain much longer optimization. Preference models are prone to reward hacking: the policy raises their score without improving response quality, or even while degrading it. Yet for open-ended tasks, where quality can neither be verified nor fully written down as rubrics, they remain valuable. We introduce Dynalize, which mitigates reward hacking online, during policy training. Periodically and asynchronously, a language model analyzes training rollouts and writes penalties for undesirable behaviors that the reward model appears to reward; a judge applies them to every rollout. In clinical counseling and creative writing, training on the reward model alone raises its score continuously but ultimately leads to a decline in external benchmarks; Dynalize sustains longer training and results in improved downstream performance. We find that our method suppresses patterns incentivized by preference models that, without penalties, spread to most responses, and even to the model's reasoning, which the reward model does not explicitly score. Furthermore, we show that its gains hold in multi-turn training, and even after we retrain the reward model on the discovered behaviors.
---
SPAR: Structural Pruning and Action Recovery for Vision-Language-Action Models

The limited onboard computing resources of robots make the inference demands of vision-language-action (VLA) models costly to accommodate. Reducing these demands through aggressive compression, however, can degrade manipulation performance beyond what supervised fine-tuning alone recovers. We present SPAR (Structural Pruning and Action Recovery), which simultaneously prunes the hidden and feed-forward intermediate dimensions of the large language model (LLM) backbone through pruning-aware tuning, then applies reinforcement learning (RL) to recover policy performance. Applied to $π_{0.5}$, SPAR reduces the language backbone from 2.34B to 0.09B parameters and the full model from 3.37B to 1.12B, removing approximately 96% and 67% of their parameters, respectively. At this compression level, RL raises average success across the four LIBERO suites from 84.55% to 93.45%, an 8.90-percentage-point gain. The compressed policy achieves a $1.98\times$ full-policy inference speedup over the unpruned model. These results identify substantial intermediate-dimension redundancy and show that post-pruning RL can recover manipulation performance in compact policies.

VeriCUA: Learning Proactive Verification for Reliable GUI Agent Rewards

Reinforcement learning for computer-use agents increasingly relies on proactive reward agents that interact with the post-execution GUI environment to verify what recorded screenshots cannot reveal. The reliability of such verification hinges on two coupled decisions: what to check and when to stop. Existing methods leave these decisions to prompted foundation models. However, neither scaling up the model nor providing few-shot examples reliably improves verification: prompted Judges ranging from 4B to GPT-5.4 all plateau at 68%-70% macro-average agreement. This is because the value of a check depends on the environment rather than the task alone, and becomes apparent only through execution. Learning from execution is itself constrained, as the Actor's terminal state cannot be faithfully replicated during training, allowing only one verification attempt per state. We introduce VeriCUA, a proactive reward agent that learns its verification strategy from these single attempts. A trainable Judge decides what to check and when to stop, while a frozen Probe executes the checks in the live environment. Online reinforcement learning optimizes the Judge for both verdict correctness and efficient Probe use, with returns normalized across terminal states. Trained only on OSWorld and evaluated without further training on MobileWorld and WebArena-Verified, VeriCUA achieves the highest reward agreement across all three benchmarks and lies on the agreement-cost Pareto frontier among proactive verifiers. It surpasses few-shot GPT-5.4 by 5.36 points with 47.6%-59.8% fewer Probe tokens, and even a 1B VeriCUA exceeds every prompted Judge. As the reward for Actor RL, it raises task success by 3.21 points overall and 5.40 points on held-out multi-application tasks. These results identify the verification strategy, not verifier scale, as the key to reliable GUI rewards.

GeoDiv KAN Solver for Time Fractional Navier Stokes Equations on Complex Domains to Realistic CFD Reconstruction

Scientific machine learning provides a promising framework for solving complex problems in science and engineering. Notably, physics informed neural networks (PINNs) have shown considerable potential for solving differential equations and modelling engineering systems. Solving time fractional Navier Stokes equations (TFNSEs) on complex domains is challenging due to temporal nonlocality, nonlinear flow dynamics, geometric complexity, and incompressibility constraints. To address these challenges, we propose a geometry gated, divergence-free Gaussian radial basis function Kolmogorov Arnold network (GeoDiv KAN). The proposed architecture uses geometry dependent feature gating and a streamfunction formulation to preserve incompressibility by construction. GeoDiv KAN is evaluated for the time fractional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations on square, circular, and star-shaped domains. Numerical experiments show that GeoDiv KAN achieves higher predictive accuracy than PINN, PIKAN, RBF KAN, and DF RBFKAN across the considered benchmark problems. To establish practical relevance, GeoDiv KAN is applied to two data driven CFD cases: unsteady flow past a circular cylinder using the \href{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.5039610}{Zenodo dataset} and turbulent RANS flow through a complex U bend using the \href{https://huggingface.co/datasets/JDecke/ubend-cfd}{U-bend dataset}.

ALIGN: Aligning Condition Estimation and Expression in Advantage-Conditioned Behavior Cloning

Human-collected robot manipulation data often mix successful behavior with retries, pauses, recoveries, and failures. Advantage-Conditioned Behavior Cloning (AC-BC) can exploit such mixed-quality data, but only if the conditioning signal is both accurately constructed from offline trajectories and effectively incorporated into the policy's action-vector-field prediction. Existing pipelines can weaken this signal by thresholding estimator-derived advantage scores into noisy conditions, and by routing conditions through prompts that weakly affect the action vector field. We introduce ALIGN, a condition-aligned AC-BC framework for vision-language-action policies. ALIGN directly predicts categorical pairwise conditions in the policy-facing label space and injects them into a DiT action expert through AdaRMS modulation fused with flow time. We evaluate ALIGN on deliberately mixed real-robot datasets for ARX X5 bowl stacking and Franka key hanging, each combining standard demonstrations with retry-heavy trajectories. ALIGN improves condition fidelity, strengthens conditional vector-field separability, and achieves 90.0% and 56.7% closed-loop success, compared with 56.7% and 33.3% for the strongest AC-BC baseline. These results indicate that aligning conditions from estimation to action prediction is an effective way to extract robust manipulation policies from data in which success, recovery and error coexist.

Learning What to Remember: Resource-Efficient Self-Improvement for Lifelong Agents

Self-improving agents aim to continuously improve their behavior by learning from experience after deployment. However, accumulating and reusing more experience does not necessarily lead to better decisions, particularly when generation, interaction, and memory resources are jointly constrained. Under such stringent constraints, we find that several existing lifelong-memory methods provide limited benefits over simple retrieval or even no-memory agents. This reveals a fundamental challenge for resource-efficient self-improvement: agents must learn not only which past experiences are relevant, but which are actually worth reusing to improve future decisions. When memory capacity and opportunities for reasoning and recovery are limited, retrieving an ineffective experience can consume scarce resources and hinder downstream execution. To address this challenge, we propose TokenWorth, a lightweight feedback-driven memory adaptation framework that learns query-dependent memory worth from sparse execution feedback. Without updating the backbone model, TokenWorth uses the learned worth to guide both experience retrieval and bounded memory management, enabling the agent to continually adapt how it reuses accumulated experience. Across three LifelongAgentBench environments and both local and API-based deployments, TokenWorth achieves the highest task success rate while using the fewest output tokens among the evaluated methods under the specified resource budgets. These results suggest that effective self-improvement under resource constraints depends not on accumulating more experience, but on learning what is worth remembering. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/AIGNLAI/TokenWorth.

Rethinking LLM Fine-Tuning via Weight Space Reparameterization: Preserving Safety during Downstream Adaptation

Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on downstream tasks often weakens their previously aligned safety behavior, creating an inherent trade-off between task adaptation and safety preservation. Existing approaches attempt to mitigate this issue by identifying safety-relevant neurons, layers, or update directions in the original parameter space. However, safety-related information is often distributed across many directions in this space, causing downstream updates to overwrite parameters that encode safety behavior. We propose WSR-Tune, a Weight Space Reparameterization-based fine-tuning framework that preserves safety during downstream adaptation. Our approach constructs a safety-conditioned basis and reparameterizes the weight matrices such that safety-relevant information becomes concentrated in a small subset of directions. We then identify safety-critical directions and freeze them in the reparameterized space, while updating the remaining complementary directions for downstream adaptation. By explicitly structuring the parameter space to disentangle safety and task information, our method reduces interference between objectives and enables simultaneous preservation of safety and improvement in downstream performance. Experimental results show that our method achieves a more favorable trade-off between downstream performance and safety retention, demonstrating its effectiveness for reliable LLM fine-tuning.
---
Reliable Estimation of Differential Privacy under Selection Bias

Statistical privacy audits are essential for verifying the theoretical privacy guarantees of differentially private (DP) mechanisms. Auditors typically use Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs) to estimate a lower bound on the true privacy leakage of DP mechanisms at a specified confidence level, which should not exceed the theoretical privacy upper bound guaranteed by DP. However, we found that many auditing implementations in prior work introduce a selection bias when running MIAs, which leads to statistically invalid and overestimated lower bounds. Because most audits are not tight, this bias has thus far gone unnoticed: the estimated lower bounds remained well below the theoretical upper bounds. Nevertheless, accurately evaluating this lower bound is critical, as a flawed statistical assessment could lead to false alarms on correct mechanisms or an incorrect evaluation of the guarantee's tightness. This work employs tools from the statistical literature to correct this bias. We present an overview of the different correction methods and assess their respective advantages. Furthermore, we perform a comprehensive re-analysis of results returned by prior audit implementations and show that without statistical correction, these audits violate the theoretical upper bounds up to four times more often than their claimed confidence level implies. With correction applied, the resulting lower bounds are statistically valid, and violations occur at no more than the nominal rate.
---
Fake News, Real Crashes: Information Injection Attacks on LLM Financial Trading Agents

Large language model (LLM) agents now trade real capital using news as evidence, yet they cannot reliably tell fabricated headlines from real news. Because they read the same feeds under near-identical policies, a single fabricated post can push them into the same trade at the same moment. We present a systematic attack benchmark for information injection against LLM trading agents. Unlike prompt injection, which targets the model's instructions, information injection targets the evidence the agent trusts. The benchmark spans five injection vectors and seven event categories, evaluated over ~10,000 decisions on 15 U.S. stocks. Five findings emerge. (i) Prompt injection achieves 14% attack success rate (ASR), but fabricated news reaches 62% and two 30-word templates reach 100% on all 15 stocks, inverting the usual LLM-security intuition. (ii) For fact-poisoning templates, surface form matters more than content, since removing credibility cues such as a named source or a "BREAKING" tag drops ASR from 100% to 0% while the fake claim remains identical. (iii) Success depends on the agent's state, with a fake indicator panel scoring 91% against an agent about to hold but 12% against one about to buy. (iv) Attacks generalize across model vendors and remain effective when blended, unlabeled, into real news feeds. (v) Most attacks evade standard defenses, since model scaling stops shallow vectors but not fact poisoning, peer debate fails, and only hierarchical aggregation reduces ASR.

Physiology-Aware Graph Attention for Early Ventricular Reentry Prediction

Sudden cardiac death mostly originates from post-infarct ventricular tachycardia. This reentry is sustained by propagation around scar tissue, but its risk remains difficult to predict from infarct imaging alone. While in silico induction studies can reveal these mechanisms, exploring many substrates and pacing locations remains computationally expensive. We propose a graph attention classifier for early prediction of sustained ventricular reentry from simulated cardiac electrophysiology. Each simulation is represented as a graph with early nodal voltage samples and edge attributes encoding geometry, stimulation frequency, tissue labels, amplitude, and diastolic interval. These descriptors are computed only from the first two paced activations, before sustained reentry is observed. On a balanced independent test set of 1000 simulations, the classifier achieves 94.6% accuracy, 95.4% sensitivity, 93.8% specificity, 94.6% F1-score, 0.987 ROC-AUC, and 0.985 PR-AUC. Ablation experiments show that diastolic interval is the most critical descriptor. Qualitative attention inspection suggests that different heads focus on border-zone, stimulation location, and early propagation path. These results support graph attention as a fast early-prediction approach for large-scale in silico ventricular reentry studies.
---
Local data augmentation for imbalanced binary classification

Facing imbalanced binary classification, we aim at maximizing the recall while limiting the proportion of false detections in the minority class. For doing that, the present work studies the effect of several rebalancing strategies, from the uniform subsampling of the observations from the minority class, to a more involved local data augmentation scheme. To be more specific, we establish that only rebalancing class proportions cannot modify the classification performance, whereas local data augmentation is proved to have an impact on the final results. The local augmentation scheme is introduced by means of a two-step procedure. The first step randomly subsamples anchor points among the training observations. These anchor points are used in the second step, for drawing new augmentation data following an ''augmentation kernel'' to be specified. This enables us to decouple the impact of the anchor points subsampling from the one of drawing augmentation data accounting (or not) for the local geometry of the support. The experiments on synthetic data illustrate the practical performance of several strategies for subsampling anchor points combined with either a Gaussian or a diffusion-based augmentation kernel. Compared to SMOTE-like data augmentation procedures, a better performance is achieved when subsampling in the close neighborhood to the classification boundary combined with a Gaussian augmentation kernel. Furthermore in specific settings where the geometric structure of the support matters, the experimental results show that using a diffusion-based augmentation kernel outperforms the Gaussian one.

When Coding Agents Outpace Review: Evaluating PRD-Driven Development in Mature Enterprise Repositories

Coding agents increasingly undertake development tasks in mature enterprise repositories, yet existing benchmarks offer limited insight into whether they fulfill complete Product Requirement Documents (PRDs) and where delivery breaks down. Issue resolution and development from scratch leave incremental PRD fulfillment underexplored, while isolated tests provide incomplete evidence of business and integration requirements. We introduce PACE, a benchmark of 43 enterprise tasks with 750 expert-audited Code Points. It combines requirement reconstruction, criterion construction, and evidence-based evaluation to measure PRD fulfillment and connect delivery gaps to agent behavior. Across 11 model--scaffold configurations, most scores fall between 52.1 and 78.1; GPT-5.6-Sol achieves the highest complete-delivery rate of 14.0\%. Incomplete implementation and missing integration are the principal bottlenecks: producing individual components does not ensure a complete delivery. Evaluation achieves 95.2\% agreement with expert consensus, with lower case-level variation across judging records than repeated executions. Beyond model comparison, the benchmark provides actionable feedback for harness refinement: diagnosis guides targeted improvements, and successive harness releases raise mean performance from 59.4 to 63.0 using the same model and task suite. Code is available at https://github.com/TianqiLiu1/PACE-Bench.

Answer Delivery Under a Shared Token Budget: An Audited Study of Stopping and Finalization

An inference budget is useful only when the system delivers an answer within it. We study a fixed-budget policy combining first-valid-box stopping with a charged finalization cue. On 100 fresh, independently audited GSM8K test questions per model, it improves delivered endpoint accuracy over first-box stopping by 8.0 percentage points for Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507 and 7.0 for DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B. A prelocked repeat on the same questions gives 7.0 and 10.0 points; neither seed shows a gross harm relative to first-box stopping. The point estimates of incremental physical-slot savings are below one percent. A finalization-only ablation separates cue recovery from stopping, and a fixed scalar reader tests extraction as an alternative explanation. The cue is prior art; the contribution is a component and readout analysis with charged injections and verified paths. Results cover two checkpoints, one task, one prompt family, and two fixed seed conditions.

BOSS: Token-Budgeted Online Sampling and Selection for LLM Reasoning

Test-time scaling can improve LLM reasoning, but existing approaches typically adapt generation or apply answer selection after a predetermined generation schedule. We study within-instance allocation: under a hard per-instance prompt-plus-completion token cap, when should inference stop, generate another candidate, or spend residual tokens adjudicating candidates already observed? BOSS is a training-free policy for this three-way decision, with worst-case budget checks before every generation and judge call. Its key contribution is online generation-adjudication allocation under one shared token ledger. Across two Llama-3.1 models and four reasoning benchmarks, BOSS attains the highest accuracy in 22 of 24 model-task-budget cells under the common hard cap. At Llama-3.1-8B and B=4k, it reaches 60.5 macro-average accuracy versus 58.6 for Adaptive-Consistency, 59.1 for TALE-EP, and 58.9 for fixed-schedule League. On identical Stage-I candidate pools, randomized A/B judging with repeated voting improves plurality from 58.4 to 60.3, with the full BOSS selector reaching 60.5. Approximately matched-cost controls further characterize how the benefit of adjudication changes with available inference budget. Together, these results establish generation-adjudication allocation as a distinct design axis for reasoning under strict per-instance caps.

Autonomous intelligence for mining productivity: a multi-agent AI approach to operational value creation

Integrated Remote Operation Centres (IROCs) are emerging as the architectural frontier of Industry 4.0 for capital-intensive sectors such as mining, oil and gas, and energy. Yet two intertwined gaps constrain their evolution towards full autonomy. First, no integrated agentic architecture has been proposed for mining IROCs that orchestrates Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition systems, digital twins, predictive analytics and human-machine interfaces under safety-critical constraints. Second, no evaluation framework jointly addresses the real-time, Health-Safety-Environment, regulatory-auditability and cost-per-decision requirements that govern industrial agentic deployments. This paper makes four contributions. It synthesises industry needs and quantified gains documented in analogous industries that motivate an agentic approach to mining IROCs. It introduces EI2ROC, a three-tier multi-agent architecture combining a Large Language Model orchestrator with specialised monitoring and production-capacityprediction agents communicating through a Model Context Protocol-style tool interface. It provides a critical review of agentic evaluation along six orthogonal dimensions and identifies the industrial blind spot of current benchmarks. Finally, it proposes a multi-dimensional benchmarking framework for industrial agentic AI comprising scenario library, harness, metrics and humancalibration protocol. The paper states explicitly which components are implemented in the current prototype, which belong to the proposed reference design and which remain future work, and reports preliminary cost and latency observations from an operating site. Together, EI2ROC and the benchmarking framework constitute a methodological blueprint transferable across CAPEX intensive industries.

GAIDA: Generative AI for Human-in-the-Loop Document Annotation

Document annotation is a fundamental yet labor-intensive step in building document information extraction systems. While Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and Large Language Models (LLMs) can automate parts of this process, we argue that annotation systems should be judged not by how completely they remove human effort, but by how effectively they redirect it toward review and correction. We propose a modular human-in-the-loop architecture that integrates OCR-based extraction, LLM-based semantic pre-annotation, and human validation in a single editable workflow, and present \textbf{GAIDA}, a research prototype serving as its reference implementation. GAIDA supports interchangeable OCR engines, LLM backends, and prompt configurations, and enables stage-specific measurement of annotation effort. In an initial single-annotator, stage-specific study on the FUNSD scanned-form and WildReceipt retail-receipt benchmarks, OCR assistance reduced region-creation and transcription time by up to \textbf{80.9\%}, and reviewing LLM pre-annotations reduced semantic-labeling time by up to \textbf{73.7\%}, despite imperfect pre-annotations of 78.86\% and 86.98\% Micro F1 on FUNSD and WildReceipt, respectively. Finally, we reuse the same implementation, adapting only OCR and prompt configuration, to build and openly release \textbf{AraReceipt}, a manually annotated dataset of 100 Arabic receipt images labeled with 25 key-information classes. The results indicate that imperfect automated outputs provide practical value when presented as editable suggestions under human control.

PhyPro-FM: A Physics and Prompt-Guided Graph Foundation Model for Contingency Analysis

The continuous evolution of next-generation power systems, characterized by the high penetration of renewable energy resources and dynamic topology variations, poses formidable challenges to traditional N-1/N-2 contingency analysis in terms of computational efficiency and scalability. While deep learning-based methods offer accelerated inference, existing studies predominantly treat contingency analysis as a pure regression problem, often overlooking the physical essence of grid operation and engineering requirements of real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, this paper proposes a physics and prompt-guided graph foundation model (PhyPro-FM) for generalizable and engineering-viable contingency analysis. The proposed architecture integrates graph neural networks with a learnable global query mechanism and, notably, introduces a prompt-guided learning paradigm. By leveraging historical snapshots as few-shot prompts, the model dynamically captures correlations between target and historical operating conditions, significantly enhancing generalization capabilities under unseen topologies. Furthermore, we design a physics-informed training mechanism that prioritizes branch power flow errors over nodal voltage errors, aligning the optimization objective with critical grid security constraints. To ensure engineering applicability, a physics-informed inference mechanism is also developed, incorporating a residual-based correction module and a specialized post-processing strategy for singular branches to circumvent numerical instability. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that PhyPro-FM outperforms state-of-the-art methods in accuracy and generalization, validating its superior physical consistency and robustness for practical engineering applications.

CARRS: A Constraint-Aware, Real-Time Routing System for Emergency Vehicles in Urban Environments

Emergency vehicles such as police vehicles, fire engines, and ambulances require a stable and efficient route optimization to reach life-critical events in the shortest times possible. However, the dynamic nature of urban traffic makes this ideal scenario much more challenging, causing critical life-threatening delays. Traditional civilian navigation systems cannot account for real-time traffic changes alongside vehicle-specific constraints, creating inadequate routing scenarios. This research presents the Constraint-Aware Real-Time Routing System (CARRS), a unified software architecture tailored for emergency coordination. The CARRS architecture is engineered to fuse together classical pathfinding algorithms such as A*, Dijkstra, and Bidirectional A*, with Constraint Satisfaction Problem methodologies. The Design Science Research Methodology is the research framework, and prototyping is the iterative process. The artifact was empirically evaluated using OpenStreetMap data for major metropolitan road networks, combined with simulated dynamic traffic scenarios. Performance metrics focused on route optimality, constraint adherence, rerouting responsiveness, and the potential for future system scalability. Findings revealed the system's capability to generate optimal routes for operationally relevant subgraphs in under 35 seconds, with incremental rerouting responsiveness below 20 seconds. Furthermore, the system achieved more than 90% adherence to vehicle-specific constraints, making the CARRS architecture viable for real-time dynamic routing requirements. This study delivers a routing platform that bridges artificial intelligence-driven optimization with software engineering operational compliance, presenting a practical transformative step toward adaptive emergency vehicle coordination in dynamic urban environments.

VibeTraining-Bench: Data-Trainer-Harness Co-Design for Iterative System Improvement

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires agents to diagnose system bottlenecks and coordinate improvements across training and inference. We refer to the agents' capability to jointly optimize training data, the trainer, and the inference harness as cross-component co-design. We introduce VibeTraining-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability across tasks spanning terminal operation, software engineering, multi-turn tool use, and mathematical reasoning. Within each task, research agents start from an identical initial agent system under a fixed resource budget. Guided by validation feedback, they iteratively refine data, trainers, and inference harnesses, then select and submit a final agent system for evaluation on the test set. To prevent reward hacking, we enforce three safeguards: test data isolation, separate research and grading containers, and network restrictions for research agents. Our evaluation results show that frontier research agents substantially improve target agent systems. For example, on SWE-bench Verified, GPT-6-Astra raises the target agent system's performance from 32.9% to 53.7%, comparable to the human-developed instruct model's 55.4%. However, there is still room to improve their ability to co-design and iteratively refine data, trainer, and harness to achieve cumulative performance gains. We hope that VibeTraining-Bench will serve as a unified platform for advancing RSI, deepening our understanding of cross-component optimization for agent systems. Code and data are available at https://github.com/D2I-ai/VibeTraining-Bench.

A Generic On-Board AI Processing Chain for Earth Observation: From Application Development to Embedded Deployment and Mission-Level Orchestration

Earth observation satellites acquire more data than they can downlink. Extracting information on board with artificial intelligence (AI) turns the satellite into a data-reduction stage that downlinks compact products, such as masks or detections, instead of full scenes; deploying it in a mission, however, involves the whole processing chain and not only the neural network. We present a generic on-board AI processing chain along three axes, with wildfire segmentation as the worked example among ship detection and cloud segmentation. (i) The AI model is tuned (spectral-band selection, input normalization), quantized to 8-bit integers (int8) and ported to an AMD Versal VCK190, with its excellent segmentation performance preserved on the board. (ii) A modular tiler-inference-mosaicer workflow, reused unchanged across applications, brings the tile-level model to scene level, turning a full acquisition into a scene-level mask or detection list. (iii) Each step is packaged as a unikernel and orchestrated on board by ORCHIDE, a cloud-native orchestrator. The chain is characterized end to end, from the standalone execution on the target to its execution under orchestration with parallel workflows on a representative test bench. Beyond AI inference on individual tiles, this approach aims at increasing the maturity of on-board AI deployment by addressing a complete processing chain, from the acquired scene to the mission-level execution.

Large-Scale Biomedical RAG: Corpus Scaling, Document Representation, and Efficiency Trade-offs

We present a controlled, large-scale evaluation of how corpus scale, document representation, and reranking affect the effectiveness and efficiency of biomedical Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. Retrieval collections were progressively expanded while retaining the relevant source documents, enabling consistent comparisons across corpus sizes. We compared abstract, flat full-text, and hierarchical full-text representations across first-stage retrieval, reranking, and end-to-end multiple-choice question answering. Configurations selected on development data were evaluated on five external biomedical benchmarks and a held-out test set, while retrieval latency and index storage were measured to quantify operational cost. Performance patterns observed with small or controlled collections did not consistently persist as collections grew, and the usefulness of reranking depended on the corpus and retrieval setting. RAG improved three of four model configurations across the external benchmarks, with mean gains of up to 9.6 percentage points, and improved all four configurations on the held-out test set by 10.2-30.3 points. Abstract retrieval performed best when reference evidence was abstract-aligned, whereas full-text representations were generally more effective when richer evidence was needed. Hierarchical representation matched or outperformed flat full-text retrieval in most end-to-end comparisons while requiring 67% fewer indexed units and 44-56% less storage. These findings show that corpus scale should be treated as an explicit evaluation variable in biomedical RAG, and that the most appropriate design depends on evidence format, model behavior, and operational constraints.

CIS-Transolver: A Cross-Layer Incremental-State Neural Operator for Fine-Scale Automotive Aerodynamic Flow Prediction

Neural operators offer an efficient data-driven approach to rapid prediction of complex automotive aerodynamic flows. However, spectral bias and other factors limit accurate reconstruction of fine-scale flow variations near complex geometries. Existing studies improve the representation and fitting of fine-scale information mainly through information extraction and training objectives. Further study is needed to determine how extracted local details can be used continually in subsequent operator computations and contribute effectively to the overall flow-field representation. We propose CIS-Transolver, a cross-layer incremental-state neural operator that extends fine-scale modeling from local information extraction to its use across layers. The method extracts fine-scale information through local multiscale analysis and encodes it as geometric moment states that are propagated and updated across layers. By combining information from previous layers with feedback to the value representations in Physics-Attention, the method allows local details to contribute continually to subsequent modeling of global physical correlations. We systematically evaluate CIS-Transolver on three public automotive aerodynamics benchmarks, ShapeNet-Car, AhmedML, and DrivAerNet++, covering surface pressure fields and volumetric pressure and velocity fields. CIS-Transolver achieves the lowest mean relative L2 error among the evaluated methods for all five prediction metrics across the three benchmarks. The relative error reductions average 8.72% across the five metrics compared with the best baseline for each metric, reaching 16.38% for ShapeNet-Car pressure prediction and 15.60% for AhmedML velocity prediction. These results provide a new approach to fine-scale flow prediction for complex geometries.

The Control Layer Behind Rule-Following Agents Trading Across Market Conditions

Trading discipline often fails people. Our study tested whether four AI trading agents followed their strategies and risk rules. Offering traders the evidence and an agent evaluation toolkit to separate luck from following the rules, before giving agents meaningful autonomy or capital. Our study analysed 2,627 closed positions from 12 February to 27 August 2026 across the same asset universe and execution conditions. Most positions were simulated against live prices with a 0.09% round-trip fee; 18% involved real Hyperliquid orders. Each position declared a strategy, stop and target, with a limit of five open positions per agent. The fleet finished net positive after fees. Rule adherence was high across all four agents, including the unprofitable one. Profitability tracked how often agents won relative to their break-even win rates. Institutional-style controls ran by default and contained losses through a 29% market drawdown and recovery, with documented exceptions. The discipline held for all four agents. Agents kept 97.5% of gross loss within declared stops, and typical losses closed at declared levels. Declared risk remained stable after losses and broadly stable across calm and volatile months. Twenty-three of 24 runs respected the position limit; logs identify the exception's size and duration. The same live data produced different results across assigned strategies. The trade-log tests can be rerun from the published trade snapshot; regime tests also require the published Bitcoin prices and regime labels. Section 3.5 specifies the stop-breach tolerance. These trade-level results are not account-level returns or proof of a lasting edge. Our study provides an evaluation toolkit to help traders distinguish rule-following from luck before committing capital. For anyone considering allocating capital to agents to trade, discipline should be the entry test, not the only test.

MCDA: Multi-View Graph Contrastive Learning with Dynamic Topology Augmentation for Functional MRI Analysis

Resting-state fMRI (rs-fMRI) is a vital non-invasive tool for diagnosing brain disorders and uncovering pathological mechanisms. However, existing graph contrastive learning (GCL) methods often underexploit augmentation histories across training epochs and the relationships among different augmented views. In this paper, we introduce a topology-guided Multi-view graph Contrastive learning framework with Dynamic graph topology Augmentation (MCDA) for fMRI analysis. Unlike traditional GCL that uses fixed noise or edge-dropping, MCDA employs a Dynamic Augmentation Update (DAU) module that iteratively refines the augmented topology. Specifically, we incorporate self-aware and cross-sample augmentation strategies to simultaneously capture individual-specific functional signatures and inter-subject heterogeneity. We then design a topology-guided multi-view graph contrastive learning scheme that explores interrelationships not only between the original network and its augmented views, but also across different augmented views to maximize structural information preservation. We characterize the score-level optimization directions and establish a lower bound for the contrastive objective. The framework combines classification and contrastive losses for brain disorder diagnosis and examines discriminative functional connectivity patterns as potential biomarkers. Experiments on two public rs-fMRI datasets comprising 1,236 subjects demonstrate that MCDA outperforms the state-of-the-art methods. The code has been made publicly available at: \url{https://github.com/goodcodingagoodboy/MCDA}.

StrucChunk: Structure-Aware Retrieval for Legal Document RAG

Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can ground LLM outputs in domain-specific knowledge, but legal documents are hard: standard chunking fragments hierarchical argument structure, and generic embeddings struggle with both precise citation matching and semantic understanding. We propose StrucChunk (Structure Aware Chunking) with hybrid dense-sparse retrieval for hierarchically structured legal documents. It treats documents as directed acyclic graphs of provisions, preserves clause boundaries, resolves cross-references before embedding, and combines BM25 with BGE-M3 via Reciprocal Rank Fusion. We evaluate on five corpora, three legal traditions and two languages. StrucChunk reaches 100% boundary precision (vs. 7.7% for recursive splitting on CrPC), preserves 97.6% of documented cross-references (vs. 0%), and matches section coverage with 40.3% fewer chunks on GDPR. On LegalBench-RAG (6,858 query-passage pairs), it achieves Char-Recall@8 of 48.18% vs. 23.59% for the best baseline (+104%), surpassing the published state of the art by +83%. On 501 queries each for CrPC and CPC (Indian law), Recall@5 improves from 0.557 to 0.804 (+44.4%) and from 0.158 to 0.259 (+64.6%); chunking accounts for 88% and 73% of the gain. On 501 GDPR queries, Recall@5 is 0.868 with R@1 improving +59%. On NitiBench-CCL (3,730 Thai financial law queries), HR@5 is 0.856 vs. 0.594 for naive chunking (+44.1%), showing that section-boundary alignment is the dominant factor across languages, and cross-reference augmentation adds +2.3% on cross-reference queries. We release the implementation, 5,256 evaluation queries across five legal corpora, annotation methodology and chunking quality metrics. Code: github.com/SinghSuryaDeep/legal-rag-strucchunk-1

Selective Transfer through Sequence Distillation: From Benchmark Knowledge to Backdoor Behaviour

Knowledge distillation is widely used to transfer capabilities from teacher models to student models, but recent work shows that it can also transfer unintended teacher properties. We study whether different hidden teacher properties survive the same distillation channels. Specifically, we compare two properties: benchmark-associated knowledge and refusal backdoors, and show that distillation treats them very differently. Teachers fine-tuned on benchmark data successfully pass that knowledge to students through all four distillation channels we test, including when students are trained only on teacher-generated number sequences containing no natural language or domain content whatsoever. Refusal backdoors tell the opposite story: backdoored teachers achieve near-perfect triggered refusal, yet sequence-distilled students inherit essentially none of this behaviour. Logit-based distillation transfers it substantially, reaching up to 85.5\% triggered refusal in students that saw zero backdoor examples. Distillation is therefore selective, and the selection criterion is not what data the student sees, but what structure the teacher's property has. The code is available at https://github.com/MJonibek/kd-leakage.

The Scheduler Is a Hidden Retriever: How Asynchrony Shapes Evidence Acquisition in Search Agents

Language-model agents increasingly solve complex tasks through parallel reasoning and asynchronous information gathering. Controlled replay across two model families and three benchmarks shows that delivery timing redirects search decisions made before all results arrive, despite identical initial evidence. Acquisition disagreement reaches up to 24 percentage points above same-order repeat variation. This dependence persists after all initial evidence arrives and through a second search, turning temporary differences in what agents have seen into lasting differences in what they acquire. An acquisition-regret bound with a matching construction shows that invariance at a fixed early arrival count can limit the use of evidence already received. We introduce Confluent Evidence Acquisition (CEA), which instead releases local reasoning when each subquestion's evidence is complete and allocates shared search independently of completion order. For a fixed plan and stable conditional response law, we prove joint invariance of decisions, acquisitions, and answers. With question-only plans across three benchmarks, CEA brings acquisition disagreement to within 1.0 percentage point of repeat variation and achieves accuracy comparable to complete-input controls, improving over early commitment by up to 12.6 percentage points. Replacing CEA's fixed allocation with first-ready allocation under contention adds 21.6 percentage points of acquisition disagreement, while local query-target disagreement remains near repeat variation. Timed comparisons show that CEA reaches decisions earlier and responds faster than the same program waiting for all initial evidence.

Robotic Photography System based on Vision-Language-Action Model

Modern robotic photography requires understanding photographic intent, planning camera motions, and producing aesthetically pleasing images in complex environments. We present RoboPhoto-VLA, a unified Vision-Language-Action framework that jointly models perception, reasoning, and camera control for autonomous robotic photography. RoboPhoto-VLA incorporates PhotoCoT for structured action planning and Aesthetic Direct Preference Optimization (ADPO) to align camera behaviors with human aesthetic preferences. To facilitate training and evaluation, we construct OpenPhotography, a robotic photography dataset with language-guided demonstrations. Extensive real-world experiments demonstrate that RoboPhoto-VLA produces photographs better aligned with human aesthetic preferences while generalizing effectively to unseen subjects, viewpoints, poses, and complex photographic instructions.

Toward Online Robust Zero-Sum Markov Games with Function Approximation

Online robust zero-sum Markov games pose a coupled operator-learning and game-solving problem: from nominal interaction data, the learner must recover a worst-case minimax Bellman operator while maintaining strategic coverage and controlling stage-game approximation error. Existing robust reinforcement-learning theory does not yet provide such an operator-learning perspective beyond specialized multi-agent settings. We develop an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework that combines dual robust Bellman fitting, optimistic minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and exploiter-mix data collection. Our analysis is organized around the robust Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class and yields a regret reduction that separates exploration complexity, robust operator-estimation error, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. Under explicit interpretable assumptions, we obtain conditional theorem interfaces for general and projected function approximation, together with closed results in several structured regimes, including tabular, epochwise cross-fit linear, self-normalized pure-online linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected RKHS truncation. Small-scale numerical experiments illustrate robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return-gap trade-off predicted by the framework.

Physics-anchored residual quantum neural networks for cloud-cover parameterization

Compact quantum neural networks have been explored for cloud-cover parameterization, but their predictive value depends on how physical knowledge is incorporated and which classical models provide the reference. We investigate an offline residual formulation that retains a fitted \XR\ cloud-cover estimate and learns a correction to its logit in transformed target space. Using released ICON/DYAMOND-derived data, we compare ten model variants in six- and eight-input settings, with three matched replicates and 10,000 training samples per replicate. All quantum circuits use local Z readout and noiseless exact expectation values. Under a common 30-epoch protocol, residual prediction reduced physical-space test mean squared error relative to direct QNN prediction by 54.3\% and 38.8\% for six-input XYZ and ZZXY circuits, and by 49.2\% and 38.9\% for their eight-input versions. Residual QNNs also outperformed the selected compact residual multilayer perceptron in every paired replicate. A larger, longer-trained Grundner-Q1 adaptation nevertheless achieved lower error: residual Q1 reached mean MSEs of 0.00778 and 0.00791, compared with 0.01124 and 0.01082 for residual XYZ. The results support residual prediction for compact simulated QNNs within the tested budget and establish a clear boundary set by stronger classical references. They do not establish computational quantum advantage or online climate-model skill.

VHDLBench: Benchmarking and Fine-Tuning Large Language Models for VHDL-Based Chip Design

The rapid evolution of digital circuit complexity demands innovative approaches to hardware design automation. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have influenced software development, their application to Hardware Description Language (HDL) generation remains largely unexplored, particularly for VHDL. This paper presents a comprehensive study for AI-assisted digital circuit design, addressing gaps in current Electronic Design Automation workflows. The work introduces a VHDL dataset of 18,500+ syntax-verified examples from open-source repositories and converted Verilog designs. Five 7B-parameter LLMs were fine-tuned using parameter-efficient QLoRA technique, enabling deployment in resource-constrained environments. To evaluate model efficacy, a rigorous three-level pipeline was established: (1) syntax and synthesis validation, (2) functional simulation with testbenches, and (3) FPGA prototyping on Zynq SoCs. Results show fine-tuned models achieve up to 92% syntax correctness and 73% functional accuracy on common design tasks, with DeepSeek-Coder demonstrating the highest performance gains. Generated designs were successfully deployed on real hardware, confirming not only syntactic but operational viability. This work demonstrates that LLMs, when tailored with domain-specific data and lightweight fine-tuning can contribute meaningfully to the future of generative EDA workflows.

Exploration via Activation-Space Steering for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains a language model by sampling responses, scoring them with an automatic verifier, and updating the policy from the resulting rewards. The policy therefore receives direct learning signals only from trajectories that appear in its rollouts. Yet repeated sampling from the same policy can concentrate on similar reasoning paths, leaving useful alternatives unexplored under a fixed rollout budget. We introduce Activation-Space Exploration (ASE), an online method for diversifying training rollouts. By clustering activation differences between correct and incorrect responses to the same problem, ASE builds a structured bank of latent exploration vectors. Different vectors are then applied to subsequent rollouts to explore alternative reasoning paths. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base trained on DAPO-Math-17k, ASE improves average Mean@8 across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks over GRPO by 4.87 and 1.87 percentage points, respectively, under the same rollout budget.

REFLEX: RNA Ensemble Generation via Flexibility-Calibrated Stochastic Bridge

RNA molecules often function through multiple experimentally observed conformational states. A central challenge is to capture ensemble diversity without sacrificing structural fidelity: samples may collapse toward averaged representative structures or drift into unsupported conformational regions. We introduce REFLEX (RNA Ensemble generation calibrated by FLEXibility), a sequence-conditioned generator for RNA backbone-frame ensembles based on a heteroscedastic stochastic bridge on SE(3). During training, experimental B-factors calibrate residue-wise bridge widths, exposing each observed conformer through local perturbation neighborhoods with residue-specific scales while keeping constrained regions sharper. A learned flexibility condition guides the bridge-field network to recover target conformer-specific frames from these calibrated noisy states. At inference, REFLEX uses only the input sequence, requiring no MSA-subsampling heuristics. On a multi-conformer RNA benchmark, REFLEX achieves the best precision-recall trade-off for ensemble coverage across evaluation thresholds, while recovery-stress ablations indicate gains in target-frame recovery under stronger perturbations.

LOCU: Lowdin-Orthogonalized Constraint Updates for Multi-Constraint Policy Optimization

We investigate multi-constraint policy optimization in constrained Markov decision processes (CMDPs), where interactions among constraint gradients often yield ill-conditioned update directions, causing numerically fragile steps and unintended cancellation or redundant overlap in constraint corrections. To address this, we propose LOCU, which mitigates numerical instability arising from constraint interactions by applying symmetric Lowdin orthogonalization to the natural gradients of the reward and constraints under the Fisher-Rao metric. Combined with Fisher-geometric screening and near-collinear compression, LOCU yields a compact reduced space in which the trust region becomes a Euclidean ball and constraints are handled symmetrically through a low-dimensional active-set solve. For infeasible iterates, LOCU constructs a Pareto-descent direction which preserves the local feasibility of satisfied constraints within the same reduced formulation. Experiments show that LOCU remains stable under high constraint coupling, narrow feasible regions, and infeasible starts, and generalizes across different cost critic architectures.

Beyond Static Leaderboards: Capability-Focused Evaluation with Structural Diversity for Time Series Foundation Models

Static public benchmarks for time series foundation models (TSFMs) typically have fixed and narrow test distributions. Their repeated reuse encourages benchmark-specific tuning, making reported performance reflect adaptation to the fixed test set rather than genuine forecasting generalization. In addition, a single aggregate score cannot fully characterize model capabilities across distinct temporal structures and input relationships. We propose CaFE (Capability-Focused Evaluation), a refreshable evaluation extension grounded in original benchmark data that enriches static benchmarks with structural diversity and enriches single-score black-box evaluation with multi-dimensional, capability-oriented assessment. CaFE first profiles the original benchmark and defines eight Structural Features (SFs) that characterize its temporal structure. For each SF, it resamples the original data according to its structural support, removes edge-of-distribution samples, and augments the retained samples by varying the SF's salience, producing samples with different structural profiles. After filtering out out-of-distribution samples while preserving diversity, CaFE yields a refreshed evaluation set. TSFMs are then evaluated on this set using both conventional forecast-error metrics and intervention-response metrics, giving each model a per-feature capability profile alongside endpoint accuracy. Experiments on GIFT-Eval and FEV-Mini20 show that CaFE yields stable evaluation results and reveals per-feature strengths and weaknesses that a single aggregate score hides. Moreover, on genuine data filtered by structural condition, models selected by CaFE outperform those chosen by the aggregate leaderboard. CaFE therefore makes model selection conditional on the structural response of interest.

Machina Pauliana: A neuro-symbolic approach to the actio pauliana in Dutch insolvency law

Applying a legal provision frequently requires both structured rule-based reasoning and sensitivity to open-textured terms developed in case law. The actio pauliana of Article 42 of the Dutch Bankruptcy Act (Faillissementswet, Fw), which allows an insolvency practitioner to avoid transactions that prejudice creditors, requires both. We present a neuro-symbolic model of Article 42 Fw. Its symbolic core is written in Catala in a literate style that keeps statute and formalisation aligned and was developed through pair programming, with open-textured terms represented as boolean inputs where neural components couple to the symbolic solver. A neural routine structures a case's natural-language facts into boolean and date inputs. Neural subroutines resolve the open-textured terms through case-based reasoning over Dutch civil judgments, retrieved through a pipeline that filters on the requirement being resolved and expands along the procedural chain of a case. Segmenting and labelling those judgments attains high precision and recall against a manually annotated gold standard of 100 judgments. On ten hand-annotated cases the neuro-symbolic model handles most criteria reasonably but struggles with the knowledge requirement. Retrieval depth shows no clear quantitative effect, although retrieved judgments provide explicit grounding in prior case law.

CoRS: Fast Neural Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling via Composite Interval State Reuse

Neural solvers for the Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) typically refresh graph representations and update feasibility after every operation--machine decision. However, only feasibility must remain exact at every step, whereas graph encoding is substantially more expensive and can potentially be refreshed less frequently. We present CoRS, which decouples them: an interval-level graph representation is reused across multiple sequential decisions, while a stepwise feasibility state is updated after every action. This two-timescale design amortizes graph encoding without parallelizing action generation or relaxing scheduling constraints. CoRS further develops a feasibility-preserving composite-interval decoding scheme with dynamic constraint tracking and boundary state synchronization, which reduces representation refresh frequency while maintaining continuous feasible schedule construction. Furthermore, to alleviate representation staleness caused by interval-level state reuse, CoRS introduces enhanced static features as a compensation mechanism. By providing more comprehensive structural information of the scheduling problem, these features improve the model's perception of overall problem characteristics. Extensive experiments on synthetic and multiple public FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that CoRS significantly reduces inference time while maintaining competitive solution quality, achieving favorable speed-quality trade-offs under tight wall-clock budgets and low-latency scenarios.

Towards More Granular Reasoning: Mapping the DSA Transparency Database Taxonomy to Platform Terms and Conditions Using Large Language Models

Article 24(5) of the Digital Services Act (DSA) requires providers of online platforms to submit every content moderation decision they make, with a corresponding Statement of Reasons (SoR), to the DSA Transparency Database (DSA-TDB). Such decisions are classified against seventeen high-level attribute categories that categorize the primary reason for the moderation decision. Because over 99.8% of statements cite a violation of the provider's own terms and conditions rather than a legal provision, the database is overwhelmingly a record of contractual enforcement using catch-all reporting; however, whether its categories actually describe those contractual rules well has not been examined. In this paper, we map the terms and conditions (T&C) of 31 designated online services against the DSA-TDB's high-level category taxonomy using a few-shot GPT-4.1 classifier over 447 policy documents. The analysis concerns how T&C rules are represented in the taxonomy, not how they are enforced. Two findings emerge. First, high-level category applicability varies sharply by service type: unsafe products account for 17.5% of e-commerce assignments and 0.2% of adult-platform assignments, and negative effects on civic discourse reaches 9.9% within the Meta family but is absent from adult platforms, Booking.com, and Wikipedia. Second, the categories have notable overlap among sub-category specifications, which makes assigning a single high-level category to a decision difficult. We argue that catch-all reporting is plausibly in part a consequence of the DSA-TDB's schema and attribute design, and recommend a schema that is differentiated by service type with more mandatory attributes derived inductively from platform governance language.

Fairness or Fallacy? Mapping the Landscape of AI Bias in Disease Detection

Artificial intelligence (AI) has several transformative effects on healthcare, like to facilitate clinicians in disease detection and to manage patient care with enhanced precision. Due to the increasing complexity of medical data and the growing volume of diagnostic images, the substantial computational power needed to process such information creates both opportunities and challenges. To accomplish this objective, numerous methodologies have been proposed to integrate AI into disease detection systems. All these methodologies consider different aspects of clinical diagnosis, considering key parameters such as the type of AI model employed, the nature of input data, and the population demographics being analysed. In this paper, we review different state-of-the-art techniques which have been proposed for various areas of disease detection, specifically focusing on bias identification and mitigation. The proposed solutions are distinguished based on the type of input data they utilize, the architecture of model, and type of bias (algorithmic bias, demographic bias, model bias, and deployment bias). The time frame chosen to filter out relevant studies is between 2015 and 2026. Eventually, we review 23 related articles out of 64,389 analyzed from seven different digital libraries. The obtained results guide us to reach a conclusion with respect to state-of-the-art selected studies. Furthermore, these results reveal significant research gaps that can assist researchers in addressing AI-related bias in medical diagnosis. By prioritizing explainability, data diversity, and fairness in algorithmic design, as well as equitable access for affected patient populations, they can utilize available healthcare resources more effectively.

LLMs Prompted for Legal Context Refuse More: Overrefusal from Small On-Premises LLMs in Criminal Legal Context

Confidentiality requirements increasingly push legal professionals toward small, on-device LLMs that keep case data in-house, used for everyday tasks such as summarization, translation, and reformulation. Following standard prompting guidance, a user naturally states their role (``I am a lawyer...''). We show that this innocuous habit has an unexpected cost: such authority framing can make the model selectively refuse legitimate requests, introducing bias through uneven access to assistance. We evaluate four modern small open-weight LLMs on benign legal-domain prompts to measure this over-refusal.
  Surprisingly, we find that authority-style prefixes (''I am legal counsel working for a national supreme court'', ''I'm a defense lawyer'') systematically increase refusal rates by 2--20x over the no-prefix baseline, while a known role-play jailbreak prefix shows mixed effects, sharply increasing refusals in some models and barely shifting them in others. The finding suggests that small on-prem deployable LLMs are unstable under contextual framings that a real institutional user might naturally introduce, and further investigation is essential to minimize opportunities for bias.

GeoEdit: Adversarial De-Evidencing for Silent Geoprivacy Protection via Image Editing

Recent efforts have demonstrated the strong capabilities of Multimodal Large Reasoning Models (MLRMs) in geographic reasoning. By jointly analyzing subtle visual cues, these models can infer the locations of photos, thus enabling doxing even by non-expert adversaries. Existing defense methods struggle to balance the shareability of photos with effective protection, and often fail to sufficiently disrupt the implicit geo-evidence chains embedded in scene semantics. To this end, we propose GeoEdit, an adversarial de-evidencing framework for silent geoprivacy protection via image editing. Specifically, we apply minimal edits to high-risk evidence anchors in the image, thereby disrupting the underlying spatial reasoning chains while preserving overall scene semantics. We identify these anchors by combining structured knowledge with adversarially discovered geographic cues. To further navigate the large editing action space, we adopt a learnable policy to iteratively refine editing decisions, distilling successful trajectories into transferable natural-language insights for consistent and efficient editing. Evaluated on LifePhotoBench, GeoEdit achieves strong protection against 6 mainstream MLRMs, outperforming visual perturbation baselines by over 0.59 in image fidelity.

Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. Nash-MOPD: Optimizing Task Trade-offs in Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation

Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) aggregates the capabilities of multiple domain-expert teachers into a single student model. However, all domains in MOPD share the student's finite budget of optimization steps and rollouts. Fixed uses constant domain sampling ratios based on corpus sizes; by contrast, D$^3$-MOPD adjusts the ratios using the teacher-student reverse-KL divergence and its recent decrease. However, favoring large recent decreases can direct more training toward domains that already improve quickly, while domains that improve more slowly may receive too few examples. To address inflexible allocation and the risk of giving insufficient training to domains that improve more slowly, we propose Nash-MOPD, an adaptive domain scheduler based on a local minimax game using existing reverse-KL statistics. The equilibrium maximizes the smallest predicted proportional reduction under the local model and gives more prompts to domains that need more examples for comparable improvement. Consequently, Nash-MOPD responds to changing learning progress and supports domains that improve more slowly without extra teacher queries or rollouts. At step 100, Nash-MOPD exceeds both baselines' final mathematics and code checkpoint scores on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and reaches Fixed's on Qwen3-8B-Base. Furthermore, it leads eight of nine reported metrics and is best or tied best on all six mathematics and code accuracy metrics, respectively.

Don't Rewrite the SID Map: Topology Preservation for Generative Retrieval in Dynamic E-Commerce Search

Generative retrieval assigns each item a discrete semantic identifier (SID) and retrieves items by autoregressively generating SID sequences. In dynamic environments such as e-commerce, new items arrive continuously, requiring periodic updates to the SID-to-Item mapping and model to maintain retrieval timeliness. Each update, however, can invalidate search paths already learned by the retriever, and the quantitative relationship between mapping change and retrieval quality has not been systematically characterized. We study this problem with extensive controlled experiments over 67 days of large-scale e-commerce search logs and obtain three core findings. (1) SID semantic drift causes severe retrieval degradation that may be masked by large beam sizes: mapping updates reduce HitRate by 41.78-62.05% at beam size 1 but only 8.33-10.85% at beam size 1000, as redundant candidate generation conceals the true severity of drift. (2) The fraction of changed SIDs is strictly monotonically related to retrieval quality: as the remapped fraction increases from 0% to 72.75%, HitRate decreases monotonically, dropping up to 42.66% at beam size 1. Full remapping remains substantially worse than the preservation policy under warm-start training, indicating that short-budget continued training alone cannot repair topology disruption. (3) The SID topology-preserving principle makes even the simplest warm-start strategy highly effective: fixing old-item SIDs, inserting new-items, and warm-starting from the previous model improves old-item HitRate by 10% and new-item HitRate by 109%, validated across two non-overlapping update cycles. These findings distill into a concise deployment principle: insert new-items, freeze old-items; warm-start when possible, otherwise freeze; never change SIDs the model has already learned. A three-week online A/B test on TmallAPP shows UCTCVR +0.29% and GMV +1.16%.

Reliable Before Profitable: QuantHarness for Governing Financial Strategy Development with LLM Agents

LLM agents can turn natural-language financial research requests into executable and backtested strategies, but a successful run may still implement the wrong rule, use information unavailable at decision time, or obscure how repairs and candidate selection changed the strategy. Because these failures arise throughout development, final code and performance metrics alone are insufficient to establish either fidelity to the request or the validity of the development process. We therefore address the reliability of financial strategy development at the trajectory level: the system must control how intermediate outputs become downstream state and preserve the evidence needed to justify the terminal outcome. We introduce QuantHarness, a two-layer agent harness in which a finance-specific Workflow structures specification, data grounding, construction, testing, and review, while Harness Core enforces stage-specific information, action, and commitment boundaries. We also introduce FinRelBench, a benchmark that evaluates strategy-development reliability through execution success, requirement satisfaction, and runtime evidence. QuantHarness achieves the highest Task Success at 90.72\%, compared with 74.23\%--84.54\% for the comparison systems. Controlled stress tests show that its boundaries contain targeted violations, while structured run records improve evidence retrieval and support decision replay under the evaluated protocol.

From Code to Playable Systems: CEGame-Bench for Long-Horizon Game Creation Agents

A game ships when a player can launch it, finish it, and return to a saved world, none of which a successful build guarantees, yet game-creation agents are mostly tested on one engine or on one-prompt browser games. We present CEGame-Bench, the first long-horizon benchmark for complete-game creation across five production engines: Defold, Godot, Roblox Studio, Unity, and Unreal Engine. Agents build each game from an empty project and a design document in runs averaging up to 295 steps and 65M tokens; the document doubles as a contract, and a hidden evaluator accepts a game only by real-input play. Under four scaffolds, agents earn most integration points but usually under half the gameplay points, and their games reach the ending in only 16% of runs, while references built by engineers with coding agents score near the maximum, which places this artifact-to-play gap in the agents. No two engines rank configurations alike (Kendall's tau 0.14-0.62), and the same game usually scores higher on Unity and Unreal Engine, which check more before play. We release Game Completion Boost, an agent skill for requirement-driven playtesting and repair that raises the execution-based score of three models by 14.1-24.3 points.

How Robust Are Prognostic Radiomic Features? Comparing Selection Techniques on Pooled Glioblastoma Cohorts

Radiomic survival studies in glioblastoma report heterogeneous prognostic features, but how much of this stems from the methodology itself is rarely examined. We analysed the association of 463 radiomic, anatomical and clinical features from pre-treatment MRI with overall survival in 705 IDH-wildtype glioblastoma patients from three public cohorts, using three techniques: a log-rank test after median split, univariate Cox regression (both Benjamini-Hochberg corrected) and an XGBoost regressor interpreted with SHAP. The log-rank test selected 99 features, Cox regression 72 and SHAP its top 20; five were selected by all three: age, a peritumoural T2 texture feature, the number of connected peritumoural mask components and two image-level intensity maxima. The two survival tests shared 51 features, about half of the SHAP top 20 were also selected by either test, and where selections overlapped, effect directions agreed. Many selections were PyRadiomics diagnostic descriptors that are not restricted to tumour voxels. Analytical pipeline choices, including ranking method, pre-filtering and descriptor handling, can substantially influence which features appear prognostic.

Not All Similarity Is Leakage: Distribution-Aware Identity Leakage Assessment in Synthetic Face Datasets

Synthetic data provides the means for more ethical and less biased facial recognition. Nonetheless, the limitations of generative models and the conditioning process they rely on pose a risk of identity leakage, resulting in synthetic samples that are overly similar to those seen in a generator's training set, mitigating privacy benefits. In this work, we show that previously proposed approaches are affected by two key limitations: high classifier dependence and a tendency to identify leakage with respect to real datasets that were not used in training. To address these shortcomings, we propose a novel metric called Scaled Overlap (SO), which adapts a non-parametric data-copying test for facial recognition and extends it to the identity level. By leveraging a multi-model setup that compensates for errors in individual classifiers, it provides insights into the true strength of identity leakage. Our analysis identifies leaking datasets (CemiFace, SFace2, IDNet) with the most acute leakage introduced by generative model conditioning in CemiFace, demonstrates that real datasets do not contribute equally to generator training, and shows that the majority of synthetic images are drawn from multiple identities rather than relying on a single identity. Ultimately, our study provides new insights into the quality of synthetic datasets and gives more reliable techniques to help the community ensure the trustworthiness of synthetic data.

CUA-Lite: An Open Platform for Computer Use Agents

Open resources for computer use agents (CUAs) -- environments, datasets, and models -- remain scattered: without a standard, these resources cannot be integrated and scaled; additionally, evaluation, RL, and SFT cannot share one infrastructure. We present CUA-Lite, an open platform for developing CUAs, built around three standardized abstractions: (1) Unified environment interface, under which we integrate 12 benchmarks across desktop, browser, and mobile, plus lightweight VM-free sandboxes that replicate OSWorld's desktop at much lower cost, providing over 30,000 verifiable tasks. (2) Unified supervised data format, into which we convert the major public CUA datasets, from GUI grounding and understanding to full rollouts, and record our fresh rollouts from frontier CUAs on these verifiable environments. (3) Model harnesses shared across evaluation, RL, and SFT, one per model and implemented for 14 model families. Each harness runs its model in these computer environments, producing the rollouts that evaluation and RL consume, or renders stored supervised data into the model's own training format for SFT. We release reference scores across the integrated benchmarks, and show that RL and SFT through the same harness improve open-weight models. We release our dataset, code, and models, and the platform is open to contributions.

FinSafeGuard: A Quality-Driven Synthetic Dataset for Financial AI Safety

Generating high-quality synthetic datasets at scale remains a fundamental challenge for training specialized AI systems in data-scarce domains. We present a comprehensive methodology that produces 14.3 million quality-filtered synthetic training examples from a structured taxonomy of 632,000 scenario specifications. Our four-stage pipeline ad- dresses critical challenges in synthetic data quality, diversity, and realism through: (1) systematic pre-generation research establishing optimal generation parameters via diminishing returns analysis, (2) conditional auto evolution mechanisms that remediate low-diversity batches during generation, (3) a two-layer semantic deduplication architecture combining lexical and embedding-based methods, and (4) LLM-as-Judge quality evaluation filtering samples based on behavioral correctness and tuple fidelity. Empirical analysis across three domains (Banking, Insurance, Financial Services) demonstrates domain-specific deduplication patterns ranging from 9.9% to 40.6%, with output generation modes exhibiting highest redundancy. Our batch size optimization reveals mode-specific diversity saturation thresholds, enabling principled allocation strategies. The complete pipeline reduces 26.4M raw samples to 14.3M high-confidence examples through merge (20.9% reduction), semantic deduplication (18.7% reduction), and quality-based filtering (15.9% reduction), providing a reproducible framework for domain-specific synthetic data generation with rigorous quality control.

Honeyed Words, Hidden Daggers: Benchmarking and Training LLMs to See Through Mock Politeness

The Chinese idiom "kou mi fu jian" ("honeyed words, hidden daggers") names speech that is polite in form but hostile in function. We present ZhenhuanBench, a 1,592-item benchmark built from expert commentary on Empresses in the Palace, covering speaker intent, emotion-language mismatch, and per-listener uptake. Text-only evaluation covers 34 LLMs. GPT-6 Astra leads the leaderboard at 73.48% overall, while its listener-reaction score is 58.37%. The annotations also supply supervised traces and string-verifiable training rewards. With 338 gold traces, an 8B model outperforms teacher distillation on identical items. Subsequent RL saturates, and chance correction reduces the estimated recoverable performance gap by a factor of 7.5. Our CPR-GRPO loop (Compress-Probe-Replenish) uses this estimate to weight gold supervision, reaching 67.0 on public multiple-choice tasks and 74.5 under the training-matched hidden protocol. Without target-task training, CPR outperforms Base, SFT, and GRPO across six Chinese and English pragmatic and sarcasm evaluations. On three external datasets, training with CPR yields higher mean Macro-F1 than SFT and GRPO.
