HierDTA: Hierarchical Structure-Aware Representation Learning for Drug--Target Affinity Prediction

Drug--target affinity (DTA) prediction plays an important role in computer-aided drug discovery, where reliable computational models can help prioritize compound--protein pairs before experimental validation. A key challenge in DTA prediction is to effectively mine multi-level molecular information from drug structures while accurately modeling the binding-site environment of target proteins. To address these issues, we introduce HierDTA, a hierarchical structure-aware framework for DTA prediction. \textcolor{black}{HierDTA represents each drug as an atom--motif heterogeneous graph and introduces an augmentation module that propagates functional-motif semantics to the corresponding atoms. For proteins, kinase-domain structures are converted into residue interaction graphs. The resulting atom and residue representations are integrated using cross-attention to capture pair-specific relationships, while molecule fingerprints and pretrained ESM-2 embeddings provide complementary global information. Compared with state-of-the-art methods, HierDTA achieves superior performance on the Davis dataset across all metrics and attains the highest \(r_m^2\) of 0.791 on KIBA. It also exhibits strong generalization under cold-start settings, particularly on KIBA, where MSE is reduced by 21.7\% in the unseen-protein scenario. Ablation study and interpretability analysis supported the contributions of the hierarchical augmentation and interaction components.} Case studies further showed that HierDTA provided early enrichment of candidate bioactive compounds. These results indicate that HierDTA is an effective framework for affinity prediction and target-specific virtual screening.

Decision4Rec: A Jev-Inspired Framework of Decision Models on Knowledge and Active Graphs for Multi-Agent Recommender Systems

Decision models, such as the commercial Jev, are language models that return one probability for each option of a question instead of generating text. We present Decision4Rec, an open and modular framework that makes decision models useful for recommendation. A knowledge graph writes the logs of each recommendation scenario as short statements on a defined scale, such as "sold: top 10%". A pool of agents, proposes candidates, and a residual ranker keeps the best 30. A decision model, trained once on real choices of three scenarios, reads them through one interface of typed questions, and a fusion step combines its answers with the score of the ranker. Any model with this interface can be used, and we run the commercial Jev, the released open Kev and Kev models trained by us on recommendation requests. The framework with a trained 4B decision model is 25 to 36% above the best trained baseline in NDCG@10 in three sparse Amazon catalogues, one of them never used to train the decision model, and almost tie with baselines on the dense Steam dataset. Most of the gain comes from the pool, and with a trained recommender it comes from what the agents know about its candidates rather than from new candidates. The 27B decision model adds to the pool 5 to 6% above the 0.8B model in three of four domains. A trained 0.8B open model beats the released Jev in eleven of fifteen settings and is never worse. On MovieLens-1M, a dense domain never seen in training, the gain is 5 to 7%. Code, configurations and trained models are available at https://github.com/marcopoli/decision4rec and https://huggingface.co/m-polignano.

AuDQ: Auditable Data Quality through Agentic Rule Discovery

High-stakes tabular pipelines rely on scattered, static quality checks that are hard to evolve and audit against policy intent. Regulated domains particularly suffer as the LLM-era automation raises expectations for traceability and accountability. We propose a vision for an ontology-grounded, repository-native data quality layer in which rules are first-class artifacts: versioned, provenance-linked to governance texts, and re-playable through immutable execution traces. As a first step toward validating this vision, we present a concrete implementation of its agentic rule-discovery core with two rule acquisition modes (Full-Agentic and Guided) and a human-in-the-loop (HITL) review interface. We define an evaluation framework and run experiments addressing whether our fully autonomous, divide-and-conquer agentic rule discovery produces rules of the same quality and diversity as those specified by a human expert, reporting preliminary results on two real world industry datasets in the finance and retail domains.

TamedFlow: Trajectory-Aware Distillation for Teacher-Assisted Diffusion Sampling

Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) deliver high-fidelity image and video generation, but inference requires many sequential model evaluations. Two acceleration strategies are increasingly important: model compression makes each evaluation cheaper, while adaptive inference avoids selected teacher computation through reuse, forecasting, or speculative execution. Their combination, however, is brittle: directly applying the evaluated adaptive methods to a compressed student can degrade quality, an observed failure mode we call the \textbf{Combination Gap}. We link this gap to a mismatch between pointwise distillation and short-horizon execution: a student trained at teacher states need not remain reliable when its own predictions are chained. We propose \textbf{TamedFlow}, which trains a compressed DiT for this deployed role through cross-architecture feature alignment, a near-identity Velocity Alignment Head, and randomized rollout supervision. At inference, a lightweight Learnable Trajectory Estimator routes completed drafts: accepted drafts bypass the teacher, while rejected drafts roll back and receive one teacher rescue step. Distilling DiT-XL/2 into DiT-B/2, TamedFlow reaches FID $2.58$ at $2.25\times$ measured speedup over DDIM-50 on ImageNet $256\times256$. Ablations support trajectory supervision, and routing controls favor LTE over random decisions at nearly matched latency. Evaluations on ImageNet $512\times512$, MS-COCO, and Latte/FaceForensics extend the empirical scope.

Multimodal Decoupling for Diversified Recommendation through Semantic and Structural Shaping

Recommender systems often suffer from over-concentrated results when optimized for accuracy, limiting recommendation diversity. While diversity has become an important objective, existing methods still face fundamental limitations from both semantic and structural perspectives. On the one hand, multimodal recommender systems provide rich semantic signals, yet are often treated as auxiliary to ID embeddings, leading to ineffective semantic utilization for diversity. On the other hand, the accuracy--diversity trade-off persists during training, where accuracy gains often come at the cost of diversity. We show that this phenomenon is fundamentally governed by both the structural properties of learned representations and the dynamics of optimization. To address these challenges, we propose MOSS, a unified framework for multimodal decoupling through semantic and structural shaping. Specifically, we first introduce an MLLM-based Decoupled Feature Extraction module to disentangle multimodal content into informative representations. To enhance semantic utilization, we design a Category-aware Embedding Selection module that extracts compact, category-aware semantic features. To address structural limitations, we propose two complementary modules: Alternating Dual-Graph Propagation, which alleviates representation collapse by integrating collaborative and multimodal propagation, and Entropy-based Diversity Regularization, which mitigates optimization bias by promoting balanced category exposure. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that MOSS consistently achieves a superior accuracy--diversity trade-off over strong baselines.

SPEM: Structure-Aware Procedural Memory for Efficient Skill Use in LLM Agents

LLM-based agents increasingly rely on reusable skills. However, scaling skill libraries makes efficient and accurate skill use difficult. Full skill loading incurs high token and runtime overhead. In contrast, flat semantic retrieval treats skills as independent text entries, ignoring dependencies, execution order, state transitions, and data flow constraints. We propose SPEM, a lightweight structure-aware procedural memory framework for efficient skill use in LLM-based agents. SPEM maintains Atomic Skill Memory for individual skills and Skill-DAG Memory for reusable compositions derived from successful executions. For a new task, SPEM reuses high-confidence Skill-DAG templates when available. Otherwise, it retrieves atomic skills and invokes Skill-GNN for state- and structure-aware skill selection. Successful executions are written back as new Skill-DAGs, allowing procedural memory to accumulate over time. Experiments on ALFWorld and SkillsBench show that SPEM achieves the best overall effectiveness--efficiency trade-off, reaching 87.1% ALFWorld success and 38.38% SkillsBench reward while reducing token and runtime costs against strong baselines.

FrameGRPO: Incentivizing Video Reasoning via Multi-Frame Rollouts and Temporal Credit

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, particularly Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has improved video reasoning in multimodal large language models. However, standard video GRPO varies only linguistic trajectories while conditioning every rollout in a group on the same sampled frames, which can produce homogeneous rewards and weaken group-relative learning when the shared visual evidence is insufficient or distracting. We propose \textbf{FrameGRPO}, a simple and effective reinforcement-learning framework that requires neither auxiliary networks nor changes to the model architecture. Specifically, FrameGRPO combines two lightweight components: (1) \textbf{Multi-Frame Rollouts}, which use only uniform temporal subsampling of a single maximal-frame decode to construct aligned views that diversify visual evidence and reasoning trajectories; and (2) \textbf{Gated Temporal Efficiency Credit}, which selectively reinforces a correct lower-ratio trajectory when all rollouts in a fixed full-ratio reference subset fail, without imposing an unconditional preference for fewer frames. Extensive experiments across diverse video understanding and reasoning benchmarks demonstrate robust overall improvements over standard GRPO, highlighting temporal-view diversification as an effective mechanism for exploration and credit assignment in video MLLMs.

Understanding When Reflective Prompt and Skill Optimization Helps on Locally Served Reasoning Models: A Two-Model Study of GEPA and SkillOpt

Reflective text-space optimizers such as GEPA and SkillOpt have reported large gains on frontier and mid-size models. We test replication across two reasoning-native target-serving configurations: DeepSeek V4 Flash through DwarfStar and Qwen3.8-27B through LM Studio. For each target, we evaluate the same held-out tasks and four conditions - minimal baseline, GEPA prompt, SkillOpt skill, and manually engineered prompt - on HotpotQA (n=500 per condition) and MATH-500 (n=250). In the primary Qwen v2 rerun under the same frozen protocol, GEPA selects the HotpotQA seed and exactly reproduces baseline behavior, whereas SkillOpt's validation gain reverses into held-out degradation. On MATH-500, GEPA raises exact match from 0.828 to 0.904 while reducing latency but increasing token consumption, so lower response time does not imply lower token cost. DeepSeek likewise shows its strongest GEPA effect on MATH-500 (0.852 to 0.908). Across both targets, GEPA's strongest gains occur on MATH-500, while neither optimizer improves the HotpotQA baseline. Optimization is therefore task- and method-dependent, and validation gains may reverse on held-out evaluation. An earlier Qwen run is retained as serving-sensitivity evidence. These results suggest that textual headroom for a model-task pair and the observed effect of a text-space optimizer are more informative when decomposed into content/reasoning, answer-realization/evaluator alignment, and serving-completion/reliability. The cross-benchmark pattern further suggests that optimization may benefit from low-ambiguity, actionable evaluation signals.

Accelerated Drift of Scientific Prose in the LLM Era: Evidence from an Interrupted Time-Series Analysis

Every scientific text is embedded in the writing norms of its time: it reflects earlier publications while also shaping the texts that follow. The growing use of LLM-based writing tools introduces a new factor in this evolving system, but the linguistic changes coinciding with their emergence remain insufficiently understood. We investigate whether the trajectories of scientific-preprint writing changed in rate or direction after conversational LLMs became publicly accessible, using the release of ChatGPT in November 2022 as the observational breakpoint. We compare pre- and post-2022 trajectories across vocabulary, punctuation, readability, and syntax in abstracts from arXiv's artificial intelligence, statistics, and quantitative biology categories and from medRxiv. Using interrupted time-series regression, we find substantial post-2022 changes across multiple feature families and research domains, including increases in average word length, reduced formula-based readability, and declines in maximum dependency-tree depth and sentence-level depth variation. Individual features follow different dynamics: some lexical markers accelerate persistently, while others rise and then recede. Our findings show that isolated marker frequencies capture only part of the shift: changes in the rate, direction, and structure of linguistic trajectories provide complementary evidence of broader post-2022 change in scientific-preprint writing. More broadly, these findings motivate monitoring scientific writing as a multidimensional, evolving system rather than through a fixed set of LLM-associated markers.

TimesJev: Thinking Fast on Time Series with System 1 Sufficiency

Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong potential for time series reasoning, while models that reason over time series are usually with long chains of thought. However, this process often consumes thousands of tokens, directly leading to substantial latency during inference. In contrast, System 1 models such as Jev make decisions instantly and can naturally be an alternative, but raw time series are not compatible with the embedding space of these models, thus hindering the reasoning performance, especially when input series contain thousands of numeric points. In this paper, we make a very early attempt to propose a time series System 1 model, namely TimesJev. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. Finally, experiments on 13 datasets demonstrate that TimesJev outperforms reasoning models by over 62% with 62.9% less inference time than their fine-tuned versions on average.

Procedural Memory for Language Agents: A Neurosymbolic Perspective on Explicit Goal Structure

Large language models remain unreliable on long-horizon tasks requiring procedural reasoning. We argue that a key cause is the absence of explicit procedural memory: accessible, modifiable knowledge of how tasks are accomplished, distinct from semantic and episodic memory. Goal-Oriented Graphs offer such a representation, encoding goals and prerequisite dependencies as inspectable symbolic structure. We examine their construction, the challenges they pose, and principles for integrating them with neural reasoning.

Map-Distribution Mixture-of-Experts for Multi-Agent Pathfinding

Designing capable neural policies for multi-agent pathfinding (MAPF), which map local observations to action distributions, represents an important challenge in multi-agent learning, with practical relevance to scalable collective automation problems such as warehouse logistics. In contrast to prior studies focusing primarily on individual policy design, this study explores a meta-level Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approach to learning-based MAPF. Specifically, given a collection of compact yet high-performing experts specialised for different map distributions, our architecture learns a router that selectively fuses their outputs according to each agent's local situation. We instantiate and validate this MoE approach with a commonly used graph neural network-based policy as well as a newly developed recursive action-token refinement model. Combined with supervised and reinforcement learning fine-tuning of the experts, our experiments show that MoE generally improves upon the pretrained policy and outperforms existing leading methods. At the same time, our findings reveal that appropriately trained, parameter-matched monolithic models still outperform MoE, highlighting a nuanced role for map-distribution MoE for MAPF.

NIOCIS: Typed-Decision Next-Hop Routing with Node-Conditioned Option Spaces for Tool-Use Agents

Tool-use agents pay per decision: at each hop a language model re-reads the state and thinks for seconds, though its actions are finite and decisions recur. We present NIOCIS, a routing layer that folds execution logs into a loop-collapsed action graph (forward transitions plus recovery back-edges) answering one typed question: which declared option at a state, plus stop, does the fleet take next? A small model reads a calibrated distribution over them in one pass, emitting no text. On 1,615 production security-operations episodes we measure what the layer replaces, returns and is bounded by. Replaces: the typed encoder matches the logged next hop on 32.2% of the 3,875 held-out decisions (34.9% within the declared options) in 10 ms of encoder time (19.5 ms for the typed-choice form), against 2.2 s per LLM hop; two feature readouts reach 32.8% and 32.3%, an ordering the paired test does not resolve, so the typed layer is chosen for cost, not a separated lead. Tuning: jpt-4b reaches 9.9% zero-shot and 31.9% fine-tuned, a gap an exact paired test does not resolve (p=0.68), at 244 ms. Returns little: the realised hop falls outside the declared options for 7.9% of decisions and outside the model's top twenty for 9.3%; the most confident 15% holds 91.9% accuracy. Bounded: a cross-trace leave-one-out test puts the released state's ceiling at 25.6--44.9% as the state key grows more specific, i.e. repeated states disagree more often than they agree; the option-space ablation attributes the gain to per-node candidate restriction (+3.5 points over a flat catalog) rather than graph traversal (zero within its interval); a gated self-evolution loop lifts every arm above its anchor yet ends below full supervision; a confidence-gated cascade loses agreement when the escalation arm is the zero-shot stand-in; and agreement past a third of the fleet needs a state carrying the plan and the observation.

Auditor-Assisted Summary-Channel Verification for Hosted LLM Identity Substitution

Hosted LLM services increasingly expose model identity as a product claim, but external users and auditors often cannot inspect which model configuration served a request. We study an auditor-assisted regime in which a trusted auditor enrolls claimed identities and prepares verification material before deployment, while audit-time verification uses only the claimed identity and released output, without runtime access to model internals, inference logs, routing decisions, or provider metadata. We propose SUMMARK, a summary-channel watermarking framework that uses user-visible reasoning summaries as a keyed audit surface. During setup, SUMMARK constructs identity-specific vocabulary carriers, trains lightweight summary adapters, and calibrates per-identity thresholds. At deployment time, a keyed statistical test assesses whether the released summary is consistent with the claimed identity and, when needed, attributes the most likely enrolled identity. Across model families, scales, and task domains, SUMMARK reliably detects cross-family and cross-scale substitutions at low false-positive rates while preserving task-facing answer quality. We further study FP16->INT8/INT4 precision-consistency diagnostics and robustness under bounded post-processing, rewriting stress tests, and text-only spoofing, revealing both strong performance and clear failure boundaries. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/trinitr0toluene/Summark.

ClueFlow: Learning Long-Horizon Search through Attributed Research-State Transitions

Long-horizon search requires agents to acquire relevant evidence and integrate it into an evolving research state. Existing methods maintain research states to guide exploration or assign turn-level progress signals, yet grounding these signals in explicit, attributable state changes remains underexplored. We introduce ClueFlow, which learns long-horizon search from attributed research-state transitions. ClueFlow constructs Source-State-Final attribution chains that connect evidence-acquiring browser actions, Evidence and Candidate updates, and the final answer supported by those updates. During search, ClueFlow records typed state changes and potentially supporting browser actions, then applies gold-conditioned semantic validation and source tracing after execution. We use this attribution to guide both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. During SFT, attribution selects which decisions receive supervision with their original interaction contexts. During Turn-PPO, the same attribution augments outcome-based turn advantages with credit assigned along the Source-State-Final chains. Transition-level validation also recovers useful supervision from gold-consistent state changes in trajectories with incorrect final answers. We evaluate ClueFlow across two Qwen backbones and four search benchmarks spanning live-web and fixed-corpus settings, observing consistent gains from SFT and further improvements from PPO. With Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, ClueFlow achieves 60.0% accuracy on BrowseComp and 72.0% on BrowseComp-Plus. In controlled BrowseComp-Plus experiments, attribution-guided SFT matches all-turn exact-context SFT using about 30% of the training tokens, while attribution-guided PPO outperforms outcome-only PPO by 4.1 pp. These results demonstrate that state-transition attribution enables efficient supervision and effective credit assignment for long-horizon search agents.

GraphEvoResearcher: Graph-Aware Training-Free Self-Reflective Reasoning for Cost-Bounded Multi-Hop Question Answering

Multi-hop question answering (QA) requires models to chain intermediate conclusions while remaining cost-aware. Existing training-free self-reflective protocols (generate -> critique -> revise) improve accuracy but operate on a flat token stream: revisions cannot identify which prior step is unreliable, and early stopping cannot exploit structural convergence. We introduce GraphEvoResearcher, a training-free protocol that lifts EvoResearcher's self-reflective meta-reward onto an explicit reasoning graph - nodes are intermediate conclusions/evidence and edges encode support, contradict, and depends_on. A graph-aware meta-reward combines correctness, efficiency, reflection, diversity, and four structural signals (connectivity, acyclicity, branch coverage, path convergence) to decide whether to revise, backtrack, or stop. A graph-convergence early-stop terminates when evidence branches converge on a single conclusion. On a real LLM (DeepSeek), over GSM8K, MATH, and BBH (n=50 each), GraphEvoResearcher reaches 98%, 92%, and 82% respectively, the highest of all baselines, with the largest gains on the hardest set (MATH: +24 pp over single-shot, +12 pp over flat reflection), while keeping construction cost bounded at ~2 steps / 4.5-5 nodes. Our results indicate the structural signal of a reasoning graph is most valuable exactly where single-step reasoning is unreliable.

On the Benefits of Uncertainty Estimation for Species Classification: A Case Study on Optimizing Expert re-Annotation

Automated identification of diatoms from microscopic images is a cornerstone of large-scale freshwater biomonitoring, yet the high diversity of species and strong class imbalance pose significant challenges for reliable classification. In this study, we develop a ResNet-18 baseline for a 759-class diatom dataset and investigate how calibrated confidence estimates support selective classification to reduce expert workload without sacrificing taxonomic integrity. While the initial model exhibits overconfidence with an Expected Calibration Error (ECE) of 0.117, post-hoc temperature scaling (T = 2.12) dramatically improves reliability, lowering the ECE to 0.055.By leveraging these calibrated probabilities within a triage framework, we demonstrate that a Selective Macro-F1 score of 99.01% can be maintained at 66.2% coverage (rejection threshold $τ$ = 0.934). Qualitative analysis reveals that the system effectively functions as an intelligent filter, consistently deferring rare taxa-such as AAMB and AASI-and ambiguous morphologies for expert review. Our findings, supported by the torch-uncertainty framework, highlight the potential of simple calibration techniques to make automated diatom identification practical and robust, prioritizing the preservation of rare species metrics essential for ecological assessment. To support reproducibility, our code, environment specifications, and calibration results are available anonymously at https://anonymous.4open. science/r/greenprwksp-B53D/README.

Correctness-Dependent Asymmetric Target Distributions for Self-Distillation Policy Optimization

Self-distillation policy optimization provides dense token-level supervision for LLM post-training through feedback-conditioned self-teachers. However, directly matching the self-teacher can disrupt successful behaviors on correct rollouts while providing insufficient correction on incorrect ones. We propose Asymmetric Policy Optimization via Self-Distillation (APSD), which constructs correctness-dependent targets by mixing student and self-teacher distributions: interpolation preserves successful behaviors on correct rollouts, while extrapolation beyond the self-teacher strengthens correction on incorrect ones. We analyze APSD through divergence decomposition, reverse-KL barycentric geometry, and gradient analysis, highlighting the importance of gradient flow through student-dependent targets. Experiments under reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards and rich feedback show that APSD achieves strong overall performance against reinforcement-learning and self-distillation baselines and matches SDPO performance with 2.6x fewer training steps on average across Qwen3 model scales.

GLOBE: Geometry-Led Online Behavioral Early Exit via Unit-Sphere Trajectories

Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can improve problem solving, but models often keep generating after the correct answer is already reachable; the extra steps raise serving costs and may replace a correct intermediate result with an incorrect one. We propose GLOBE (Geometry-Led Online Behavioral Early Exit), an online, training-free algorithm that terminates the model's CoT process early. GLOBE embeds each completed reasoning segment as a point on a unit sphere and reads the shape of the resulting semantic trajectory. GLOBE first applies a trial phase that generates short trial answers in a separate branch, stopping the main stream on an accepted trial after checking confidence against a threshold. If generation continues, a trajectory phase stops the stream once this path repeatedly reverses direction with little net progress. On five benchmarks including mathematical reasoning, tool calling, and agent memory, GLOBE cuts output tokens by 49.7% and active end-to-end wall time (E2E) by 51.0% across two models, and achieves a five-task average of 80.58%, comparable to Full CoT.

Reconstruction and Discrete-Event Simulation Assessment of an Analytical

This article presents an analytical-DES benchmark for intra-aisle robotic picking in automated warehousing. The investigated cycle combines horizontal shuttle travel, tote handling and item picking at storage locations. Simulation results are compared with the analytical formulation of Lerher et al. under the same rack geometry, shuttle-motion, handling and picking conditions, and the same definitions of cycle time and derived item throughput. Reproducing the published analytical tables requires the distance interpretation dES = 1.5 m and dTB = 0 m. The analytical-DES benchmark comprises 180 scenarios formed from three rack lengths, two paired motion profiles and n = 1 to 30 visited picking locations, with ten simulation replications per scenario. The six n = 1 cases are reported separately because no travel-between component is present. Across the remaining 174 scenarios, the mean absolute relative cycle-time discrepancy is 0.8655%, the median is 0.6758%, and the maximum is 7.0370%. The mean absolute error is 2.411 s and the RMSE is 2.744 s. The simulated scenario-mean cycle time is below the analytical value in all 174 principal scenarios. The results therefore show close numerical correspondence under the tested conditions, but not exact agreement, formal statistical equivalence, or validation against an operating warehouse.

Behavioral Probes for Information Flow in LLM Swarms

Most existing approaches to LLM swarm optimization are utility-driven, focusing on final task utility while leaving the processing, integration, and reliance of information within individual interactions largely implicit. To address this, we study LLM swarm interactions from an information-flow-based view and introduce behavioral probes to quantify information flow patterns. Specifically, we repurpose External Context Score (ECS) and Parametric Knowledge Score (PKS)---originally designed for hallucination analysis---to measure a node's external-context dependence and parametric-knowledge dependence, respectively. Across controlled and realistic swarm settings, we observe consistent patterns: ECS increases with input relevance and distinguishes useful multi-source contributions, while PKS decreases as informative context accumulates. Building on this heuristic interpretation, we demonstrate how these probes can complement utility-driven structure optimization in two controlled applications. Probe-guided node pruning reduces redundant communication and token consumption, and probe-guided reasoning-path search accelerates convergence compared to utility-only baselines. Our empirical results suggest that behavioral probes can make the internal information flow of LLM swarms observable and serve as effective auxiliary signals for structure optimization.

ABACUS: Program-Guided Multistep Reasoning over Frozen Judgment Models

Judgment models answer semantic questions by scoring a finite set of candidate outcomes. Can reusable programs turn these local decisions into powerful multistep reasoning? We introduce \method, a framework that jointly constructs judgment schemas and composition programs for frozen judgment models. A generative teacher designs the procedure offline; the judgment model resolves local semantic questions online, and the saved program composes its decisions into an answer. We study three allocations of design effort between humans and the teacher, focusing on construction through a shared interface. We instantiate the framework with Kev. On three selected BIG-Bench Hard tasks, nine independently constructed programs solve all 450 executions of 150 development inputs. A separate deployment study freezes three programs after development-set reference-agreement refinement and evaluates them on 600 held-out questions. These programs achieve near-perfect 99.67\% macro accuracy, versus 64.00\% for direct Kev and 68.80\% for final-answer-only Qwen3.5-4B. They attain comparable observed accuracy to Qwen's unrestricted response policy (99.07\%) with an \textbf{8.43-fold online speedup}: 0.255 versus 2.150 seconds per question. These results show how reusable programs can substantially expand the reasoning capability of a compact judgment model while keeping its parameters fixed.

PaceVLA: Adaptive Temporal Action Quantization in Vision-Language-Action Models

Fast and reliable manipulation is crucial for practical robot deployment. Although Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently shown remarkable progress as generalist robot policies, their execution often remains temporally inefficient. A key source of this inefficiency is that policies learn fine-grained temporal patterns from teleoperated demonstrations, requiring many low-level action steps. Temporal action quantization offers a simple way to accelerate execution by aggregating consecutive low-level actions into temporally coarser actions. However, applying action quantization without considering when fine-grained control is required introduces a speed-accuracy trade-off: accelerated actions may efficiently reduce redundant steps in some phases, while discarding critical motion details in precision-sensitive interactions. To address this, we propose PaceVLA, a unified framework for adaptive temporal execution in VLAs that integrates two coupled components: (1) mode-specific action decoders built on a shared action expert, supporting multiple temporal granularities and prediction horizons, and (2) a quantizability-guided mode selection scheme which decides whether coarse execution is appropriate, and a context-conditioned router that selects an execution mode at each inference step. Experiments across three multi-task simulation benchmarks and six real-world tasks show that PaceVLA improves execution efficiency while maintaining task success rate across multiple embodiments, including long-horizon and dexterous manipulation.
---
EmbodiedWorld-200K: Toward Embodied World Agents via Embodied-Planning Dataset and Interactive World Models

Building embodied agents that can perceive, reason, and act across diverse 3D worlds is a central research direction toward artificial general intelligence. Yet most existing work remains confined to specific tasks in a single scene and transfers poorly to new environments, leaving general embodied agents for open-world settings under-explored. We refer to such agents as Embodied World Agents (EWAs), whose defining property is the ability to understand diverse visual environments and execute general actions in open-world conditions. To advance EWA research, we study open-world embodied navigation-planning and release EmbodiedWorld-200K, a large-scale dataset of over 200K samples spanning diverse scenes. Each example is equipped with multi-level annotations, ranging from low-level camera-motion trajectories to high-level semantic instructions. The dataset also provides unified evaluation splits and task-specific metrics, supporting offline pretraining and fair evaluation of EWAs. Beyond offline supervision, a defining characteristic of an EWA is its interaction with the environment; training solely on offline data is therefore insufficient, especially for generalizing to scenes not covered in the training distribution. Building dedicated real interactive environments, however, is often prohibitively expensive. To address this, we propose a novel training scheme that employs a video world model as a low-cost environment proxy and performs interactive reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training of the EWA within it. Empirically, pretraining on EmbodiedWorld-200K substantially improves the open-world embodied planning ability of the EWA, and further RL post-training inside a world model yields clear gains on out-of-distribution scenes. The dataset, baseline checkpoints, and training and evaluation code are available at https://xiaokunfeng.github.io/EmbodiedWorld-200K/.

Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents

Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In particular, agents often become trapped in high-confidence yet unproductive behaviors, repeatedly executing invalid actions or entering action loops that hinder task completion. We observe that these frequently violate environment preconditions, revealing a mismatch between the agent's representation of the internal state and the dynamics of the underlying environment. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.

CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows

Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Existing tool-agent benchmarks largely score final business states, accepting trajectories that reach correct outcomes through non-compliant processes.
  We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks.
  Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.

AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments

Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.

OrderGPT: Generation Order as a Learnable Modeling Choice

Generative modeling of discrete data has two popular paradigms: autoregressive models such as GPT, which factorize a sequence in a left-to-right (L2R) order, and masked diffusion models, which are essentially any-order autoregressive modeling. Rather than competing methods, these are the two extreme points of a single axis -- the generation order. For structured discrete domains without a natural traversal, both extremes may be suboptimal, yet the intermediate choice -- a single well-adapted order -- remains largely unexplored. We introduce OrderGPT, which builds on Any-Order GPT to learn a task-level generation order without canonical-order supervision. Our key observation is that predicting variables from randomly sampled reveal prefixes produces recurring attention patterns in the backbone that provide a model-dependent signal of predictive dependencies. We aggregate this signal into a reciprocal dependency graph and use a spectral teacher to derive continuous generation priorities. A lightweight OrderMLP integrated into the GPT backbone learns to predict these priorities from attention, incorporating order learning into the architecture within a single training run. Training gradually shifts toward the learned order, which is then frozen for subsequent autoregressive training and generation. The same mechanism adapts across modalities: adaptively it recovers near-L2R dependency structure in language, learns anatomically organized factorizations on human pose, and discovers a factorization on tabular data that places statistically and semantically related fields nearby, while improving downstream modeling metrics across all domains. The code is available at https://github.com/Chenhe-yang/OrderGPT/.

Claim Laundering: When Agent Decision Models Trust Records Over Reality

Agent decision models select actions in a single forward pass from inputs that combine environmental observations with the agent's own record of previous actions. When an action fails silently, these sources can conflict: the record reports success, but the environment shows that the step did not take effect. We ask which source determines the next decision. Across Android screen traces, ALFWorld, and WebShop, we construct paired states that independently vary the observation, the logged action, and an explicit success claim. In all 12 configurations of four decision models and three domains, records have a larger average effect on commitment decisions than observations; after claimed-but-failed steps, the models still stop, advance, or purchase in 53-100% of cases. Yet the relevant evidence is often represented internally: linear probes on intermediate hidden states distinguish successful from failed steps on held-out apps, tasks, and goals (AUROC 0.77-1.00), and activation patching reveals increasing influence from records in later layers. We use this signal in EVIGATE, an intermediate-layer veto that requires no additional forward pass and adds 0.04-2.0% serving latency. Under injected silent failures, EVIGATE improves task success by 18.7 points in live ALFWorld agents, 5.0 in WebShop, and 5.1 in a phone simulator, and in WebShop and the simulator it also outperforms random vetoes at the same rate. Its effectiveness depends on representative calibration states: gates calibrated on offline counterfactual states do not transfer reliably to live agent trajectories.

Policy Search in Code Space for Multi-Agent Pathfinding

Developing coordination policies for complex multi-agent systems relies on either labor-intensive algorithm design or opaque task-specific reinforcement learning. With recent advances in autonomous coding agents, we show that policy search directly in code space offers a compelling third path that combines automated optimization with explicit interpretability. We investigate a human-directed workflow in which coding agents iteratively propose, implement, evaluate, diagnose, and eventually refine algorithms guided by simulation feedback and explicit correctness verification. We conducted two case studies and found that this workflow robustly outperformed any manually engineered solutions. On a challenging map in the 2024 League of Robot Runners competition, a two-day search discovered a policy that exceeds the stronger locally reproduced competition entry by 35.0% on the densest instance and generalizes to unseen map instances. In simulated real-world warehouses with irregular lanes, continuous robot geometry, and asynchronous motion, a week-long search discovered an entirely new class of multi-agent pathfinding algorithm that outperforms a calibrated proprietary solution in throughput by 58.9%. Under stated assumptions, we further prove that the new algorithm is collision-free and completes, in finite time, every accepted goal that is not withdrawn or replaced. Together, the studies show that policy search in code space powered by state-of-the-art coding agents offers a highly competitive path to building real engineering solutions that are both effective and analytically tractable.

CoASFL: Coupled Consistency Optimization for Asynchronous Split Federated Learning

Split Federated Learning (SFL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training large-scale deep models on resource-constrained edge devices. Although asynchronous SFL alleviates the straggler bottleneck caused by system heterogeneity, it inevitably introduces two coupled dimensions of inconsistency: horizontal inconsistency across clients and vertical inconsistency between clients and the server. These inconsistencies, further exacerbated by statistical heterogeneity, result in biased activation representations, model divergence, and severe client drift. To address these challenges, we propose CoASFL, an inconsistency-aware asynchronous SFL framework that incorporates a dynamic activation merging mechanism and a consistency-aware model aggregation mechanism. Specifically, CoASFL constructs more consistent activation sequences from heterogeneous clients to alleviate horizontal inconsistency, and dynamically adjusts aggregation weights according to client participation frequencies and progress gaps to mitigate vertical inconsistency. By jointly coordinating activation-level and model-level consistency, CoASFL effectively suppresses inconsistency propagation during asynchronous training. Extensive experiments on an 80-device heterogeneous edge testbed demonstrate that CoASFL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art SFL approaches, accelerating training by 1.61$\times$ to 3.74$\times$ and improving final model accuracy by up to 3.26\% under heterogeneous scenarios.

Global probabilistic forecasting of air pollution

Fine particulate matter (PM2.5) refers to particulate matter with an aerodynamic diameter of 2.5 micrometre or less. It comes from human activities, such as traffic and industry, and from wildfires, desert dust and gases emitted by vegetation. Short-term exposure to it accounts for about a million deaths a year. Operational forecasts run on grids tens of kilometres wide, which hide the pollution breathed by nearly two hundred million people a day. Machine learning has transformed weather forecasting, but data-driven forecasts of air pollution still blur extremes and lose skill as the air travels. Here we introduce Foehn, a generative model that learns from global kilometre-scale estimates of PM2.5 and computes where the air comes from. Over a year of retrospective forecasts, it outperforms the operational forecast at three stations in four and forecasts at the scale of this hidden exposure. Its ensembles are calibrated, and their mean detects local pollution extremes four days ahead as well as the operational forecast does one day ahead. Such forecasts could warn the millions whom coarse maps miss, once kilometre-scale data are available in real time.

The KV-Surgeon: Compensation-Aware Offline KV-Cache Compaction

Offline compaction of the key--value cache (KV-cache) reduces the cost of repeatedly querying a fixed context by constructing a smaller cache once and reusing it subsequently. The central challenge of compaction is deciding which cache entries to delete and which to retain. Existing methods score each candidate for deletion on its own, with the rest of the cache being held fixed. However, softmax renormalization of the attention operation couples these decisions in a nonlinear manner: removing one entry changes the attention weights of every remaining one. In this work, we introduce KV-Surgeon, which uses calibration data to take interactions into account in a tractable way. We derive a quadratic surrogate that admits closed-form expressions for jointly making the optimal greedy removal decision as well as compensation within the remaining entries. On QuALITY, LongHealth, and Long Code Arena, and at every evaluated compaction ratio, KV-Surgeon substantially advances the cost--accuracy Pareto frontier of dense and mixture-of-experts models from 4B to 30B parameters. At $50\times$ compaction, KV-Surgeon improves question-answering accuracy by up to four points over attention matching (Zweiger et al., 2026), the state of the art, at comparable cost. Lastly, we introduce an even faster variant, which also exceeds the state of the art while requiring only two fifths of its cost.

Keep the Lineages Alive: Diversity-Preserving SMC for Diffusion LLM Decoding

Inference-time sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) decoding can improve the reasoning accuracy of masked diffusion language models (diffusion LLMs) without post-training by resampling a population of partial generations according to a reward. Its effectiveness therefore depends on the resampling step, which must concentrate computation on promising candidates while retaining enough diversity to explore alternative reasoning paths. The reward can be internal, the decoder's own confidence on the committed prefix, or external, a process reward model scoring that prefix. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Across mathematical-reasoning and code-generation benchmarks on two base models, LP-SMC raises oracle coverage, the fraction of problems with at least one correct particle, in every setting, by 1.8 to 25.6 points, and raises end-to-end accuracy under a common reward-model selector in eleven of twelve settings, by up to 7.9 points on LLaDA-1.5 and 18.3 on Dream-7B. The coverage gain persists under the external reward model, which suggests that diversity-preserving resampling and the guidance signal contribute separately to inference-time search.

EvoSkill v2: Fully Agentic Skill Evolution from Benchmark Adaptation to a Stateful Evolver

EvoSkill v2 is a three-agent system that edits the instructions (skills) an agent is given, leaving its model and harness intact. The user starts a run by describing a benchmark and supplying a grader, which a benchmark adaptor turns into a benchmark-specific plugin. A solver attempts the tasks, and an evolver reads the solver's training rollouts and rewrites the skills at each epoch. We implement the evolver as one stateful coding agent with a bash tool for the whole run, unlike the two most common designs among the skill optimizers we surveyed: a sequence of single model calls, as in SkillOpt, and an agent that starts afresh each round, as in SkillBoost. We also measure how much the solver's rollouts vary from one attempt to the next, give the evolver several attempts per training task, and accept every epoch's skills instead of selecting among them on noisy scores. With fine-grained ground truth, EvoSkill v2's skills raised the held-out pass rate above the stronger of SkillOpt and SkillBoost by 7.7 percentage points on SpreadsheetBench v2 and by 5.4 on $τ^3$-bench banking. In both benchmarks and both feedback settings, EvoSkill v2's evolver cost less per percentage point of held-out pass rate gained than either baseline's optimizer. EvoSkill v2 also enables a less capable model to improve its own skills, demonstrated by a gain of up to 21.5 percentage points in the $τ^3$-bench banking experiment where Claude Haiku 5.5 powers both solver and evolver.

TESS: LLM-guided Translation, Exploration, Simulation, and Search for Embodied Task Planning

In this work we focus on the problem of embodied task planning, which requires an agent to accomplish a complex task by separating it into atomic actions. Recent studies have developed LLM-based methods to address this problem. However, the executability and correctness of the generated plans remain suboptimal in complex environments and tasks. On the other hand, classical search algorithms have the potential to solve the planning problems perfectly, yet they require fully observable environments, known state transitions and goal state, which are often unattainable in general task domains. Therefore, we propose to implement an LLM-guided search algorithm that makes the search process possible and efficient by incorporating LLMs' commonsense knowledge. Our method leverages LLMs to (1) translate natural language instructions into formatted goal conditions, (2) facilitate efficient exploration in partially observable environments, (3) implement a rule-based world simulator, and (4) prune the space of candidate actions to improve search efficiency. Experimental results on VirtualHome and ALFWorld demonstrate its superior performance.

UtilityBench: Measuring Language Model Utility When Errors Have Different Costs

As language models are increasingly deployed in real-world scenarios with important outcomes, the impact of their mistakes often extends far beyond what correctness can capture. In domains like medicine, a single error can lead to serious consequences and put patient safety at risk. However, mainstream evaluation protocols still treat all errors as equal, overlooking the spectrum of real-world costs. To address this, we propose a utility-based evaluation paradigm that explicitly incorporates the varying consequences of errors. Guided by this principle, we develop UtilityBench, a large-scale benchmark of 15,000 instances with verifiable answers, quantifying error severity using Best-Worst Scaling to elicit meaningful, data-driven cost estimates. These quantified utilities shape when a model should answer or abstain, guiding it to make decisions that align with the true importance of each scenario. As another merit, we introduce UtilityAUC, a metric that measures the area under the utility curve determined by a model's response ordering, thereby enabling utility preference alignment beyond error frequency. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that UtilityAUC can distinguish between models where standard accuracy and AURC fall short. Empirically, evaluating 20 state-of-the-art models, we find that greater accuracy or more advanced architectures do not reliably translate to higher UtilityAUC, highlighting a gap between problem-solving ability and responsible decision-making in language model evaluation.

Secure Agentic Coding through Counterexample-Grounded Feedback

As coding models and agent harnesses become more capable, reliable security verification is becoming a central bottleneck. Improving secure code generation requires a signal whose reported progress remains aligned with actual program behavior under optimization; otherwise, an optimizer can improve the signal's verdict without removing the underlying vulnerability. Existing model-based approaches require models to either infer program behavior from source code or specify correct outcomes for adversarial tests, placing substantial reasoning demands on the security signal. We introduce \falcon{}, a counterexample-grounded verification framework that separates attack exploration from behavioral verification and connects them through execution traces of the program under test. In test-time repair, \falcon{} yields substantially stronger held-out functional-security gains than alternative security signals across secure code generation benchmarks, while preserving or improving functionality. As an RL reward, \falcon{} also outperforms static-analysis and learned rewards on held-out functional-security, even when all policies successfully optimize their respective training rewards. Combining \falcon{} with either proxy reward further improves held-out performance, suggesting that execution-grounded feedback can mitigate reward overoptimization while preserving useful guidance from existing security signals.

3D-MoE: Complementarity-Guided Hardware-Software Co-Design for MoE Decode on 3D-Stacked NMP

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) have become a mainstream way to scale Large Language Models (LLMs), because each token activates only a small subset of experts, reducing inference cost. However, MoE decode remains highly memory-bound and exhibits strongly skewed, time-varying expert loads. 3D-stacked DRAM near-memory processing (NMP) provides the bandwidth that decode demands, yet its tiled organization makes load balancing expensive: expert parallelism (EP) preserves locality but suffers from hotspot-induced imbalance, while more distributed strategies weaken locality and increase NoC traffic.
  To address this locality-balance conflict, we observe that domain-specialized token routing creates offsetting expert-load fluctuations across decode steps, a property we call expert complementarity. Using this insight, we propose 3D-MoE, a hardware--software co-designed system for MoE decode on 3D-stacked NMP. 3D-MoE uses offline, one-time complementarity-guided grouping and locality-aware mapping to establish coarse-grained inter-group balance, handles residual intra-group hotspots through a lightweight Collaborative Memory-Network (CMN) that streams each hot expert's weight tiles once from its host DRAM channel to helper cores over a reconfigured local broadcast path, and uses cost-aware online remapping to remain robust to workload drift. Across MoE models, 3D-MoE achieves an average 4.63$\times$ MoE-layer speedup over EP and improves end-to-end throughput by 1.58$\times$--1.86$\times$ over the strongest NMP baselines, with 2.11\% additional logic-die area.

When Should AI Chime In? Benchmarking Timely Contribution of Proactive Agents in Multi-Party Chat

Proactive agents can support group collaboration by surfacing useful information without waiting to be asked, but even relevant contributions can be disruptive if poorly timed. In this work, we isolate the timing question: given a fixed candidate contribution such as a clarification or relevant fact, when should an AI agent chime in? We introduce PITCH (Proactive Intervention Timing in CHat), to our knowledge the first benchmark with large-scale human judgments of proactive agent timing collected from participants in the same unscripted multi-party conversations they took part in. To collect high-quality judgments, we design a novel interface in which 2-4 people chat synchronously, author candidate AI contribution messages, place their own and others' contributions into the chat history, and then deliberate as a group about the best placement for each message. PITCH contains 384 candidate contributions and 1,091 judgments, each marking an ideal turn, an acceptable window, or that a contribution should not appear, from 89 participants engaged in 35 conversations. Evaluating 15 models across 28 configurations, we find that while models can place contributions well when given the entire conversation history, they systematically chime in too early when asked to decide live as the conversation unfolds: best-turn accuracy falls from 57.8% to 19.0%, with nearly half of placements occurring before every participant's acceptable window. In comparison, lay humans deciding live are more accurate (23.7% vs 19.0%), and fewer of their placements are premature (39.7% vs 46.4%). Model reasoning traces show that 93% of intervention justifications cite relevance, while almost none consider waiting. By providing a human-grounded benchmark, our work reveals gaps in agents' ability to integrate into group settings and points towards proactive agents that can reason explicitly about appropriate timing.

Motor compound fault diagnosis using an attention-based cross-talk architecture

As physical AI increasingly relies on sensing to understand physical systems, reliable condition monitoring of industrial machinery has become more important. Motors are ubiquitous in industrial systems, and increasing system complexity raises the likelihood of simultaneous faults. Accordingly, compound fault diagnosis that identifies multiple fault components and their severities is increasingly required. Because different fault components jointly affect the same vibration signal, their severity classification can be formulated as related tasks, making multi-task learning (MTL) suitable for exploiting shared information. However, conventional shared-trunk architectures force tasks to share a common representation, potentially causing negative transfer because fault components are characterized by different time-frequency regions. To address this issue, this study proposes a Residual Neural Dimension Reductor with Time-Frequency Channel Attention (RNDR-TFCA), an attention-enhanced cross-talk architecture for multi-output compound fault diagnosis. RNDR-TFCA preserves task-specific feature extractors while selectively exchanging information across tasks. Time-frequency attention emphasizes informative intra-task regions, while channel attention enhances useful inter-task information after nonlinear feature fusion. Experiments on a rotor-bearing system with inner-race fault, outer-race fault, misalignment, and unbalance showed that RNDR-TFCA achieved a macro F1 score of 0.898, outperforming single-task, multi-class, shared-trunk, and cross-talk baselines. Ablation experiments confirmed the benefit of jointly applying both attention mechanisms. RNDR-TFCA required 713 K parameters and 170.42 MMACs, only slightly higher than RNDR. Channel-expanded baselines still underperformed RNDR-TFCA, indicating that the gain was not simply attributable to increased model capacity.

Manifold-Constrained Residual Adaptation with Frame-Adaptive Layer Aggregation for Speaker Verification

Large self-supervised speech models provide powerful representations for speaker verification, but full fine-tuning is expensive. Most conventional adapters insert small trainable modules to modify the output of each layer, while keeping the original residual connections unchanged. Building on our preliminary study of manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections in speaker encoders, we explore parameter-efficient adaptation of both residual paths and feature selection across layers. We first propose a Manifold-Constrained Residual Adapter (MCRA), which splits the hidden dimensions of each Transformer residual state into N groups and uses a small Sinkhorn-normalized matrix to exchange information among them. A learnable gate controls the balance between the original and mixed residual paths. The gate adds only one scalar parameter at each residual connection and initially favors the original path, allowing gradual adaptation. We further propose Frame-Adaptive Layer Aggregation (FALA), an attention-based aggregation method that uses a single learned query shared across all utterances to compute layer weights separately for each speech frame. Because the layer representations depend on the input, the resulting weights vary across both utterances and frames, unlike the fixed weights of a conventional layer-weighted sum. Compared with a frozen WavLM baseline using a fixed layer-weighted sum, our method achieves relative EER reductions of 22.4% on VoxCeleb1-O and 2.9% on CN-Celeb, with trainable adaptation parameters equivalent to only 0.0009% and 0.0016% of the WavLM-Large parameters, respectively.

CODA: Cohort- and Drift-aware Foundation Model for Multimodal Clinical Reasoning

Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have demonstrated strong performance across diverse clinical tasks. However, existing approaches predominantly operate at the individual sample level and fail to model the interplay between population-level cohort patterns and patient-specific variation that underlie real-world clinical reasoning. In practice, clinicians routinely reason through patient cohorts sharing similar demographic, physiological, or pathological characteristics, while accounting for patient-specific deviations from the group prototype. Motivated by this gap, we propose CODA, a cohort- and drift-aware foundation model for multimodal clinical reasoning. CODA explicitly integrates cohort structure into the modeling pipeline through two complementary mechanisms: cohort-aware population stratification and drift-aware individual adaptation. In particular, we leverage reinforcement learning to discover clinically meaningful patient cohorts, and further model each patient's deviation from cohort prototypes as a structured drift signal encoding fine-grained individual heterogeneity. These cohort- and drift-aware representations are incorporated into the foundation model to enable reasoning that is simultaneously informed by population-level patterns and sensitive to individual-level variation. Extensive experiments on real-world electronic health record (EHR) benchmarks demonstrate that CODA achieves consistent effectiveness and strong generalizability across diverse clinical tasks, including closed-QA, open-QA, and report generation.

Benefit-Targeted Routing and Bounded Revision for LLM-Based Evidence Screening in Manufacturing Supplier Discovery

An evidence-review system must allocate additional model judgments and control their use. We study these software decisions on 6,164 frozen manufacturing source-task pairs. Benefit-targeted routing learns recovered agreement minus newly introduced disagreement. On the original 300-case test, 60 consultations yield 202/231 agreements with two human references versus 189/218 for a logistic-risk router. An additional 300 cases were independently annotated twice using equal frozen evidence. Without retraining the historical policies, the corresponding counts are 164/190 versus 163/193; the differences do not resolve an advantage. Strict scalar-Ridge controls share preprocessing, regularization and fitting, and remain inconclusive. Quotation and requested-state replacement gates retain rejected cases and budget costs; their joint gate rejects one original and two additional consultations without changing primary outcomes. Evidence ablations, fusion and bounded updates supply further controls. A completed 200-pair identity audit yields 97.45 percent weighted merge precision after eight author adjudications. This audits a provisional map, while prospective company-disjoint performance remains untested.

Minimum-Cost Robust Evidence Certificates for Horn Reasoning under Correlated Source Failures

A small justification for a rule-based conclusion may disappear when a source is withdrawn, and multiple derivations need not provide independent evidence. We study minimum-cost certificates that preserve a Horn conclusion under every withdrawal of at most k source domains. Evidence records remain distinct from asserted facts, supporting replicated attestations and overlapping failure dependencies. Transversals of minimal-support footprints characterize robustness and explain why disjoint-proof requirements can reject valid certificates. An exact procedure alternates weighted evidence selection with a source-failure oracle. A rule-closed counterinterpretation yields a globally valid selection cut without enumerating supports or failure scenarios. We prove correctness, finite termination, and cost optimality, establish coNP-completeness of unrestricted verification and NP-completeness of synthesis for every fixed budget, and identify a tractable reachability restriction. Experiments on controlled benchmarks, reachability graphs, and ontology-derived instances demonstrate that our counterinterpretation-guided synthesis achieves up to 92% optimum coverage within practical resource bounds, outperforming scenario expansion and general abduction while confirming the necessity of transversal-based reasoning over proof-packing heuristics.

Adaptive Decoding Methods for Token-Efficient Reinforcement Learning

Rollout decoding affects both which trajectories are used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and how many completion tokens are spent collecting them. We study whether candidate-token support should remain fixed or widen during training in the context of rollout sampling efficiency. Our main schedule moves from top-K=10 to K=20 and then to full support as the attempted-token budget increases. In a 4B code-RLVR setting, the schedule has a higher interpolated 150M-token validation value than its matched fixed-K=10 continuation in all three runs, with a mean gain of $1.80{\pm}0.42$ points. The schedule also performs comparably to a separately trained fixed-K=20 baseline, the strongest fixed-support setting in our sweep. Follow-up experiments suggest that late widening to full support is the most promising variant, although the evidence remains noisy, and mixed-reward-group diagnostics do not clearly explain the gain. An exploratory single-run test replay yields identical point estimates for the schedule and fixed $K=10$. Overall, our results show that rollout support is an important control for sampling efficiency in RLVR and motivate further study of adaptive support schedules.

Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners?

Large language model agents are often deployed in environments where new knowledge, tools, and workflows keep arriving. Such an agent is therefore expected to adapt continually, learning new tasks as they arrive without forgetting earlier ones. This resembles the continual learning problem, but at a pace that conventional gradient updates cannot sustain. Nonparametric self-evolving agents are a natural alternative: they adapt by rewriting their own harness, such as prompts, memory, and skills, from task feedback while the model weights stay frozen. We study whether such agents are effective continual learners across four drifting task streams that shift in topic, workflow, or domain (built from BIRD, BFCL, and ScienceWorld). Evaluating three representative methods spanning experience retrieval and self-modification (ReasoningBank, GEPA, and Meta-Harness), we find that although self-evolution improves aggregate performance on most streams, higher plasticity consistently comes at the cost of stability. Specifically, self-modifying methods apply global edits to a shared prompt or workspace, interfering with unseen tasks and overwriting earlier knowledge as the stream shifts. Conversely, similarity-based memory retrieval localizes updates across distant domains, but still retrieves foreign memories on unseen tasks and suffers from retrieval collision when similar queries require conflicting workflows. These findings show that current self-evolving agents remain incomplete continual learners.

MASIF: Implicit Failures for Process Observability in Multi-Agent Systems

Multi-agent systems (MAS) can complete a task despite local execution errors that are corrected, bypassed, or carried forward without individually determining its outcome. We call these implicit failures (IFs) and study them as signals for process observability beyond final task outcome. Across 1,828 traces from nine MAS-benchmark configurations, IFs occur in 54.3% of successful and 75.2% of failed traces. We develop taxonomies of error types and subsequent absorption mechanisms, supported by human and cross-model annotation audits. Counterfactual replay further probes how repairing an IF changes the final outcome. To examine the practical value of IFs, we evaluate IF-based best-of-N trace selection against existing process reward models, and introduce a novel RL algorithm, IF-GRPO, that uses IF costs to refine preferences among successful trajectories in GRPO. Experiments show that IF-GRPO exhibits some gains in accuracy and process metrics. Importantly, it exposes trade-offs between task performance, IF count and cost, and token-normalized IF density. Overall, our results show that IFs provide complementary process observability into how MAS reach their outcomes, including error recovery, propagation, and IF-based process differences that final task success alone does not expose.
---
Rubrics Beyond Evaluation: Exploiting Rubric Structure in Reinforcement Learning

Rubrics are widely used to evaluate open-ended question answering by breaking overall quality into explicit criteria. These explicit criteria make rubrics a rich source of learning guidance, yet their potential for guiding the training process has been largely overlooked. Existing methods use rubrics mainly as direct feedback signals, without explicitly modeling the capabilities behind their criteria. This leaves their capability structure largely unused in guiding the training process. We introduce Rubric-Structured Reinforcement Learning (RSRL), an RL framework that unlocks the full potential of rubrics by exploiting their structure throughout the training process, rather than treating them simply as direct reward signals. At its core, RSRL builds on three key designs: (1) Rubric-to-Capability Structuring first recovers the capability structure hidden in rubric criteria, (2) Self-Sufficient Capability Learning then uses this structure to bootstrap underdeveloped capabilities into effective learning, (3) Capability-Residual Credit Routing finally preserves capability-specific gains during optimization. Experiments show that RSRL consistently improves both overall performance and underdeveloped capabilities, boosting downstream scientific question-answering performance by up to 7.43 over the initial policy and 7.60 over non-rubric structured RL baselines, showing that reinforcement learning can substantially benefit from the capability structure encoded in rubrics.

Measurement Validity and Representation Dynamics of Confidence Signals in Large Language Models

Whether a language model "knows what it knows" matters for trustworthy deployment and for deciding when it should abstain. Prior work usually measures this through one channel: verbalized confidence, a linear probe on hidden states, or agreement among sampled answers (semantic entropy), and often treats these as interchangeable. We compare all three in a pre-registered audit of five Qwen2.5 models (0.5B-7B, including a base/instruct pair at 7B) on factual retrieval (PopQA, SimpleQA) and multi-step reasoning (GSM8K, MATH). First, probes trained on prefill states to predict whether sampled answers will disagree become informative earlier for retrieval (about 7% of layer depth on average) than for reasoning (about 28%). The pre-registered five-layer test supported this only marginally; a finer 21-layer follow-up, designed after those results, supports it more clearly. In most retrieval cells, however, a prompt-only TF-IDF classifier matches the probe, so the early signal may reflect surface features of the question. Second, verbalized confidence is unstable: slightly different prompt templates yield scores that barely correlate. Third, Expected Calibration Error is misleading: verbal confidence looks well calibrated because it clusters near the base rate while discriminating poorly, whereas sampling agreement has worse ECE but about twice the resolution. Of five pre-registered hypotheses, two were falsified, one was partly supported, one was not supported, and one could not be tested because the base model's verbal confidence was degenerate.

SWIFT: Self-guided Weak-to-strong dIFfusion models via Target extrapolation

Training high-quality diffusion models is computationally expensive, and inference-time guidance often adds further sampling cost. In this work, we introduce Swift, a simple yet effective self-guided training method that uses a frozen weak denoiser to improve a stronger model without adding inference overhead. For each noisy input, Swift extrapolates the clean regression target away from the weak denoiser's prediction, amplifying components that remain underfit. The weak guide can be an early training checkpoint, requires no backpropagation during main-model training, and is discarded afterward. The resulting model retains the original sampler and supports both conditional and unconditional generation without label dropout or an unconditional branch. We explain this mechanism through a spectral analysis of finite-time population gradient flow in an RKHS. With an early-stopped weak guide, we prove that a nonempty range of extrapolation strengths yields strictly lower posterior-mean error than matched standard training, despite shifting the regression target away from the posterior mean. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-256 demonstrate improvements across model scales and representations. In one ImageNet-256 configuration, Swift surpasses the baseline's best FID using roughly half the total training compute, including guide preparation and training overhead. On RAEv2, it achieves FID 1.15 without inference-time guidance and 0.99 with guidance. These results establish target extrapolation as an effective approach to weak-to-strong diffusion training with efficient single-model inference.
