A Cue's Hidden Price: Auditing Seller Influence on AI Shoppers

Large language models are increasingly asked to shop on a user's behalf, which lets seller-controlled content such as badges, endorsements, and framing steer what they recommend. That poses the question at the core of generative engine optimization (GEO): not whether seller content shifts an agent's choice, but how much. The usual gauge of that influence, how often a cue wins the choice, has a blind spot: once a cue is strong enough to be picked almost every time, the choice rate can no longer show how much stronger one cue is than another. We introduce price-equivalent (PE) auditing, which removes this ceiling by pricing the cue in money. In a counterbalanced, position-controlled forced choice, we raise the cued product's price until it is chosen only half the time, and report that premium as the cue's price-equivalent: a formally defined, policy-conditioned parity threshold that makes very different cues comparable. Priced this way, cues that look equally strong at equal price differ by a wide margin, so choice-rate audits substantially under-resolve seller influence. We run the audit over 113,472 trials, seven models across families, and two product categories. To our knowledge, we also run the first price-equivalent audit of a decision-native model, Jev, which outputs a choice probability directly instead of generating text; strikingly, its profile is unlike any text model we tested, resisting most cues yet remaining susceptible to the strongest social-proof claims. We release the stimuli, logs, and code as a benchmark that reports each model's cue-price profile rather than a single score, giving developers and auditors a common, reproducible way to measure how content moves AI shopping agents.

Not Every Rollout Needs Distillation: Correctness-Filtered On-Policy Self-Distillation

On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) supervises student-generated trajectories with a reference-conditioned version of the same base model. We ask whether every trajectory supplies distinct supervision that warrants its distillation cost. Gradient measurements on Qwen3-1.7B at initialization reveal that correct and incorrect responses have similar individual gradient magnitudes, but incorrect responses have approximately three times the coherent gradient share. Their estimated population-mean directions also largely overlap. A signal--noise model fitted at a small group size tracks alignment at larger group sizes without refitting. Motivated by these measurements, we introduce correctness-filtered OPSD (C-OPSD), which generates one response per problem and distills only a subset of incorrect responses. Removing unselected rows before both distillation forwards avoids their teacher/student forward and student backward work. Across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B on AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25, C-OPSD achieves on-par performance with full OPSD. In a measured micro-batch-size-four implementation, executed rows decrease by approximately 68%, and with retained rows packed into full micro-batches, training takes about 40% less time while rollout generation remains unchanged. Together, the measurements and training results connect outcome-conditioned supervision redundancy to more compute-efficient distillation.

OV-PEAR: Observation-Verified Pose-Scale Estimation and Reconstruction for Unknown Industrial Manipulation Objects

Fine industrial robotic manipulation requires 6D pose, scale, and an operation-ready 3D model. Existing industrial pose estimation relies on CAD models, object-specific training data, or stable workcells, limiting use with unseen objects; zero-shot methods suffer from single-view occlusion, generative bias, and scale uncertainty. We present OV-PEAR, an observation-verified framework for 3D reconstruction and pose-scale estimation of unknown industrial objects. Its three-stage object-centric pipeline: (1) segments RGB-D instances and generates object-level 3D hypotheses; (2) anchors them to the robot frame via appearance-aware view retrieval, explicit scale search, and visibility-sensitive registration; (3) back-projects multi-view RGB-D evidence into the object-canonical frame to verify voxel occupancy, suppress hallucinations, and produce reusable trusted object models. On BOP-Industrial, OV-PEAR achieves 0.657 average AR and improves pose and voxel-level geometric consistency in real scenes; real-robot connector assembly reaches 99.67% grasp and 84.00% assembly success. The model library and pose-scale log enable short-term object-level state maintenance at 0.02 ms/frame.

Where RL Breaks: Localizing and Repairing Failures via Targeted Continued Pretraining

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is widely used to improve reasoning in language models, yet growing evidence suggests that it mostly sharpens what the base model can already do. This raises a practical question: when RL fails, what is the base model missing? We look inside the RL policy's own rollouts and find that failures are localized: whether a rollout fails is strongly predicted by a small set of characteristic n-grams, ranging from reflection phrases such as "let's recheck" to specific operations such as "the inverse of A". These failures persist throughout RL training, although they are not beyond the model's reach: other sampled answers to the same question are often correct. Building on this observation, we use the failure $n$-grams to retrieve text from the pretraining corpus and continue pretraining the base model on it before RL. The resulting corpus has only 35M tokens, 3--4 orders of magnitude smaller than typical mid-training. We evaluate this approach on OLMo2 and Qwen3 models trained with RL on (1) GSM8K; (2) a mixture of math, commonsense, and knowledge questions; and (3) general math and science reasoning. Despite its size, the corpus yields consistent gains: our approach outperforms continued pretraining on the same amount of text sampled from the pretraining corpus across 10 out of 11 settings, including by $2.0$ points on GSM8K when inserted before mid-training. The retrieved text also transfers across model families: text retrieved for OLMo2-1B's failures improves Qwen3-4B on the mixture by $1.9$ points over the same baseline. Together, our results suggest that where RL fails is a cheap and effective signal for selecting pretraining data.

Diffusion Language Models Change Speed Across Tasks and Languages

Diffusion language models can write several tokens in one forward pass, and they decide during generation how many passes to spend. Two outputs of the same token length can therefore vary a lot in cost. To understand the underlying factors of this cost variation, we analyze two diffusion models across 10 tasks. We find that the number of forward passes per token depends heavily on the task. Text copied or lightly edited from the input is cheap, while open-ended writing is costly. In DiffusionGemma, generating a token for story writing requires more than three times as many forward passes as for grammar correction. Two output properties explain much of this gap: how much of it is new relative to the input, and how rare its words are within the task. The same holds within a single response when thinking is involved. The final answer is cheap when it repeats the model's reasoning trace and costly when it adds something new. In multilingual generation, we find that highly tokenized languages also require more passes per token, adding to the cost they already pay with more tokens. Finally, cost tracks correctness: on the same math problem, wrong answers take more passes per token than correct ones, and weighting candidate answers by decoding speed beats majority voting by up to 4.2 points at no extra cost. Taken together, our results show that diffusion's computational advantage depends on how much of the output is already determined, while its decoding effort offers a practical signal for making inference more efficient and reliable.

FrontierRefactor: Benchmarking Multi-Scale Performance-Oriented Codebase Refactoring

Autonomous codebase refactoring requires coding agents to restructure working software while strictly preserving externally observable behavior and prioritizing execution performance and compute efficiency over stylistic preference. Existing benchmarks: refactoring benchmarks assess functional correctness while overlooking runtime regressions, whereas code performance benchmarks target isolated micro-kernels that ignore codebase-scale complexity. We observe that refactoring scale is governed by the scope of the observable contract, the caller-visible interface fixing behavioral invariants while freeing private implementations for optimization. Building on this insight, we introduce FrontierRefactor, a benchmark of 60 qualified engineering tasks spanning four scales, from computational kernels to full repositories, across in-place optimization and cross-language rewriting. Evaluating frontier coding agents through a reference-free cascaded protocol across 1,080 execution runs reveals that correctness and speedup are largely decoupled: 41.2% of submissions satisfy functional equivalence yet fail to deliver verified performance improvements, with 19.3% suffering performance regressions and 21.9% trapped in measurement noise. Refactoring paradigms reveal orthogonal engineering bottlenecks: while cross-language rewrites gain speedups from native compilation after passing functional checks, agents struggle with contract fidelity, with pass rates dropping from 65.0% on modules to 32.6% at package scale; conversely, in-place optimization reliably preserves behavioral contracts at 86.5% but rarely achieves acceleration without physical performance profiling. Trajectory analysis shows that agents fall victim to uncalibrated micro-benchmarking illusions, an operational blind spot that multi-turn execution-timing feedback directly resolves, raising strict success from 16.0% to 40.0%.

When Retrieval Partially Conflicts with Skills: Understanding Procedural Hallucination in Language Models

Language models increasingly use skills that provide reusable instructions and procedures for task execution. After a relevant skill has been selected, the model may need to apply its governing procedure together with retrieved task context. When the retrieved context is useful but contains cues that violate the skill, the model must retain compatible information while rejecting the conflicting cues. We study failures in which the model adopts such cues and produces a retrieval-supported but skill-invalid answer, which we call procedural hallucination. We introduce SourceClash, a matched benchmark with 1,271 bundles and 6,355 prompts across ten procedural domains. Each bundle fixes the task, skill specification, and answer space while varying the relation between the skill and retrieval. Across nine models, valid-answer rates average 25.7% under conflicting retrieval, compared with 78.2% and 72.8% under two supportive retrieval settings. Mechanistic analysis of open-weight models shows that the answer-preference difference typically reaches its maximum shortly after generation begins. Residual replacement moves the model toward valid answers, while attention decomposition attributes most of the repair to value vectors. Intervention at the identified value pathway also improves final answer validity. These results characterize how partly conflicting retrieval changes skill-guided generation.

Optimizing Marketing Budgets for Social Influence: A Generalized Fractional Incentives Framework

Marketing managers routinely allocate limited budgets across individuals in social networks whose responsiveness and influence vary substantially. We study the problem of Budgeted Generalized Fractional Influence Maximization (BGFIM), which captures the allocation of continuous levels of marketing spend across users in the presence of heterogeneous response and network spillovers. In this framework, adoption probabilities are governed by user-specific response functions that capture both baseline propensity and marginal responsiveness, while incentive costs vary across users. We propose a scalable solution approach based on a discrete greedy procedure with provable approximation guarantees, enabling tractable optimization under realistic budget constraints. Our formulation generalizes prior influence maximization models by allowing heterogeneous response and cost structures, providing a more flexible representation of targeted marketing interventions. Using experiments on multiple large-scale networks, we show that continuous allocation strategies can outperform standard heuristics and improve influence outcomes under heterogeneous conditions, and we illustrate how the framework can support profit-oriented decision making by identifying budget levels that balance marginal returns and costs. Overall, our results highlight the value of fine-grained budget allocation in networked marketing environments and offer a scalable approach for data-driven marketing resource allocation.

When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine

Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. Our work provides an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness under underspecification.

Dynamic Dual-Feedback Conformal Inference for Time Series Forecasting

Quantifying uncertainty in time series forecasting is particularly demanding because sequential data exhibit temporal dependence and are prone to distributional changes. Conformal inference has emerged as a powerful uncertainty quantification approach through the construction of prediction sets. Recent advances have introduced online conformal methods that adaptively adjust prediction thresholds through feedback mechanisms. However, the existing feedback mechanism typically relies solely on miscoverage indicators (actual feedback), whether the true label falls within the interval at each time step, while overlooking the empirical prediction threshold (estimated feedback) that is derived from the oracle conformal method. In this paper, we propose Dynamic Dual-feedback Conformal Inference (DDCI), which incorporates a dual-feedback mechanism consisting of actual feedback and estimated feedback. The former drives the primary adjustment of the intervals based on true observations, while the latter dampens excessive expansions or contractions by leveraging empirical thresholds from conformal inference during updates. By balancing these two signals, DDCI achieves more stable and narrower prediction intervals in sequential settings while maintaining near-nominal coverage in practice.

When Stable and Preferred Semantics Agree: A Path-Based Approach

We investigate structural conditions under which preferred and stable semantics agree in abstract argumentation frameworks. Our approach is based on suitably chosen sets of paths in the attack graph and binary relations induced by them. We establish a general path-based criterion for semantic agreement and instantiate it with several classes of paths, obtaining sufficient conditions expressed in terms of well-foundedness of the corresponding relations.
  The resulting conditions strictly generalize two principal structural criteria for preferred and stable agreement in the literature: Dung's limited controversiality condition and the SCC-symmetry condition. In particular, Dung's result is recovered as a special case by taking the relevant path set to be the set of even paths. We further introduce two infinite sequences of path sets, each yielding a family of agreement conditions. For the first sequence, a Collapse Theorem reduces the infinitely many associated well-foundedness conditions to three essentially distinct ones, whereas the second sequence gives rise to genuinely distinct conditions with no analogous collapse. Our results apply to both finite and infinite argumentation frameworks, admit simplified formulations in the finite case, and also yield sufficient conditions for the existence of stable extensions.

AirMPA: A Meteorology-to-Pollution Adapter for Global Air Quality Forecasting

Accurate real-time forecasting of atmospheric pollutants is essential for reducing exposure risks and improving public health. Traditional numerical forecasting systems suffer from incomplete parameterizations, uncertain inputs, and high computational cost. Existing deep learning approaches improve efficiency, but often entangle meteorological and pollutant variables within unified forecasting frameworks, limiting flexibility and pollutant-specific representation learning. To address these challenges, we propose AirMPA, a decoupled autoregressive framework for meteorology-to-pollution forecasting. AirMPA predicts 13 atmospheric pollutants at 0.4° spatial resolution and 12-h temporal resolution using meteorological fields, emission priors, and historical pollutant concentrations. The model is built on an improved Swin-Transformer architecture with zonal release attention and dual-branch fusion, enabling more effective modeling of global transport continuity and heterogeneous pollutant dynamics. During training, AirMPA uses the fifth-generation atmospheric reanalysis from the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts (ERA5) as meteorological forcing, while during inference it directly ingests forecast fields from external meteorological models, enabling stable autoregressive prediction up to 5 days ahead. Experiments show that AirMPA consistently outperforms Aurora and surpasses the Copernicus Atmosphere Monitoring Service (CAMS) in approximately 90% of pollutant-lead-time evaluation cases over the 5-day forecast horizon, while outperforming CAMS on all variables beyond 48 h. These results demonstrate the potential of decoupled meteorology-to-pollution forecasting for global air quality prediction.

SAR: Learning Global Item-Space Matching and Geometry-Aware Placement Ranking for Buffered Online 3D Bin Packing

Online 3D bin packing with a bounded buffer requires irreversible item-selection and placement decisions under limited knowledge of future arrivals. Feasibility alone is insufficient, as feasible actions can produce different residual-space structures and subsequent packing opportunities. A central architectural question is how to allocate spatial reasoning between item selection and placement evaluation. We propose Spatial Affordance Retrieval (SAR), a novel learning-based framework that coordinates global item-space matching with geometry-aware placement ranking. A voxel-token spatial encoder represents 3D container occupancy, enabling a picker to match buffered items to the residual space without requiring item-specific placement proposals as inputs. After item selection, a loader ranks feasible rotation-position candidates using candidate geometry, local geometric features (LGF), and inter-candidate comparison, without directly accessing global spatial tokens. The feasible candidate set connects the two stages, which are jointly optimized under a shared long-horizon packing return. On the Random, JD, and SF datasets, the best SAR variants improve utilization over the strongest evaluated online baselines by 0.96-4.20 percentage points under the no-buffer setting (K=1). With a buffer (K=5), the best utilization rates across SAR variants reach 93.55%, 91.04%, and 90.41%, respectively. Ablations support the complementary roles of global item-space matching and geometry-aware placement evaluation, counterfactual analysis suggests that learned item selection favors greater future packing potential, and experiments demonstrate compatibility with multiple candidate generators.

Can LLM User-Proxies Stand In for Humans in Reliance Measurement? Replicating Rel-A.I. with Simulated Users

Can language-model user proxies reproduce human reliance on an AI answer across contexts? Published simulated-user studies do not establish validity for this partial-answer decision, and Rel-A.I.'s released human record omits trial-level choices. We audit its 90 printed questions and 15 domain-by-marker reliance rates using sampled decisions and counterbalanced A/B probabilities from open models through 72B parameters. A matched crossover varies the printed agent specialty within each question, separating question content, label averages, and their interaction. Mistral Small's strong-marker math-minus-law label marginal is -0.4 percentage points, yet matching the agent label to the question domain raises reliance by 13.3 points on average across the five domains. The best A/B proxy has 22.5-point absolute-rate error and 8.3-point domain-effect error against the human table. A two-sample independent-binomial human replication has 3.5-point expected domain-effect error under the stated working model. The interaction is model dependent, and inference layout changes identical-prompt probabilities. This single-study audit does not establish proxy validity for human effect sizes or general directional screening.

OpenSaC: A Python Library for Search as Code in Deep Research Agents

Deep research agents investigate complex questions through iterative search, source examination, and evidence synthesis. A conventional workflow alternates reasoning with individual search and reading actions. Search as Code introduces a new paradigm: agents write programs that compose search, source reading, reranking, model calls, and local processing, returning selected observations for further reasoning. To support this workflow in existing coding agents, we present OpenSaC, an open-source Python library with a companion Search as Code skill. Configurable capability interfaces let agents combine these operations while retaining evidence for inspection and reuse. Compared with standard Codex using its built-in web search, the OpenSaC configuration uses less execution time and fewer model tokens on BrowseComp and WideSearch, with higher accuracy on BrowseComp and slightly lower row F1 on WideSearch. We aim to lower the barrier to adopting Search as Code and provide an open foundation for community development and transparent research on deep research agents.

Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills

Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.

From Trace to Fix: Residual Task State in Long-Horizon Agents

Recovering from failures in long-horizon, stateful language agents is challenging because the action introducing a defect, the symptom exposing it, and the persistent state requiring repair frequently diverge. Existing methods largely focus on retrospective failure attribution, leaving sequential diagnosis and state repair poorly integrated under constrained budgets. We introduce TRACE-FIX, a unified recovery framework centered on versioned residual task state. Trace transforms execution logs into a structured dependency-linked ledger, prioritizing unresolved task requirements rather than salient surface errors. Fix formulates recovery as budgeted planning over admissible actions, incorporating outcome forecasting and explicit repair-reserve constraints to prevent premature budget exhaustion. To rigorously decouple localization accuracy from downstream repair capability, we present LongH-Debug, evaluating root-cause attribution, verified-fault recovery, and end-to-end closed-loop execution independently. Our empirical analysis demonstrates that structured residual tracking substantially improves causal localization over raw-trace baselines, while budget-aware sequential planning converts verified fault signals into more reliable task recovery without destructive trial-and-error. Code is available at https://github.com/toffeecat/tracedp.

EvalRx: Agentic Diagnosis and Verified Repair of Foundation Models

Model evaluation reveals where models fail, but rarely why or whether a proposed fix genuinely works. We introduce EvalRx, an open-source agentic framework for statistically grounded model diagnosis and repair. EvalRx discovers systematic failure modes, explores candidate mechanisms, formulates falsifiable hypotheses, and verifies them by executing frozen, pre-specified tests before using supported diagnoses to guide tiered repair. Candidate repairs are validated against the unchanged model and promoted only when they improve the target behavior without unacceptable regression. Across language, vision-language, and audio-language settings spanning Gemini, Qwen, Gemma, and Nemotron, EvalRx improves performance in all 12 evaluated model family-modality combinations, with gains ranging from +1.4 to +10.9 percentage points (+5.8 points on average). These results show that model evaluation can serve not only to measure failures, but to support an auditable process for diagnosing and repairing them.

TRACE: Tracking Multimodal Clinical Trajectories for Inpatient Risk Prediction

Accurate inpatient risk prediction requires modeling heterogeneous clinical evidence that accumulates irregularly over time. This challenge spans in-hospital mortality, prolonged length of stay, and readmission, each with task-specific observation windows. Existing multimodal approaches combine structured health record data, chest x-rays, and clinical text, but primarily focus on modality fusion or missing-modality compensation, with limited attention to temporal trajectories over an admission. We propose TRACE (TRAjectory modeling of multimodal Clinical Events), a daily-scale framework organizing clinical events into day-level trajectories, separating day-invariant global context from day-specific local events drawn from EHR data, chest x-rays, radiology reports, and discharge summaries. The trajectory is encoded using temporal attention, allowing unavailable modalities to emit no tokens. A phenotype-guided contrastive objective further organizes admissions under heterogeneous disease distributions. Across cohorts from MIMIC-IV, eICU, and MIMIC-III comprising nearly 180,000 total stays, we evaluate TRACE on mortality, length of stay, and readmission prediction. TRACE achieves the highest AUROC across all nine dataset-task settings and the highest ACC in eight, outperforming baselines. Ablation studies validate our attention masks and contrastive learning, while trajectory-level attribution localizes risk-associated hospital days and modalities.

ActTime: Action-Conditioned World Modeling for Time Series

Time-series models increasingly condition on known future inputs. In controlled systems, some of these inputs are actions that can be changed, compared, and optimized before execution. A model must therefore do more than predict the realized future: it must predict how outcomes change under different actions from the same observed history and support reliable comparison of candidate plans. We introduce ActTime, an action-conditioned world model for time series. Over the observed history, ActTime updates variable-specific latent states through an action-conditioned transition and corrects them using observations. It then rolls the same transition forward under candidate actions to generate future trajectories. Training uses only factual trajectories, without paired alternative-action outcomes or planning supervision. We construct a simulator-grounded evaluation suite across building energy, traffic, greenhouse climate, and inventory management, covering factual prediction, intervention-effect prediction, offline decision making, and closed-loop planning. ActTime ranks first in 12 of 16 domain-task settings. Across eight methods, the mean pairwise model-ranking reversal rate is 17.8% between factual prediction and intervention fidelity, and rises to 28.4% between intervention fidelity and decision ranking. These results show that factual accuracy, intervention fidelity, and decision quality are related but not interchangeable.

Computation, Not Memory: Chain-of-Thought and State Tracking in LLM Agents

Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little to rule-following, although it helps one model complete the task. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. Without reasoning, linear probes recover the state from the model's activations about as well as a text classifier recovers it from the transcript. However, decoding accuracy does not predict which interventions change the agent's actions. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step. The state that can be decoded without reasoning is a limited guide to how the agent acts.

Remember Less, Adapt Longer: Bounded-State In-Context Reinforcement Learning with Recurrent Summary Memory

In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) enables agents to adapt to new tasks without parameter updates, relying on memory to retain what they learn from experience. For agents operating continually, existing approaches using memory trade off bounded cost against access to past experience. Approaches using growing memories, such as full-history attention and accumulating summaries, incur storage and per-decision costs that increase with task length. Fixed-size memories bound storage but restrict access: sliding windows discard older steps, while recurrent networks compress even recent interactions into a hidden state. We introduce Recurrent Summary Memory (RSM), a Transformer policy that attends exactly to a short working buffer of recent interactions and carries a fixed-capacity summary of earlier experience. When the buffer fills, RSM rewrites the summary from its previous contents and the buffer, then clears the buffer, so deployment memory and per-decision cost stay $O(1)$ in task length. On long-running partially observable tasks, frozen RSM policies sustain their adapted performance for eight times the training horizon with about 1% of full-history memory, ending ahead of every evaluated baseline while growing memories deteriorate. When the task configuration changes mid-run, RSM re-adapts better than growing and recurrent memories. Clearing the summary or blocking the policy's access to it removes its benefit, while swapping in another task's summary steers behaviour towards that task. Together, these results show that our fixed-capacity summary can sustain in-context adaptation far beyond the training horizon.

Cognitively Decoupled Meta-Induction for Out-of-Distribution Generalization

In-context learning (ICL) tackles downstream tasks conditioned on demonstrations. This paradigm relies heavily on neighbor-based matching between queries and demonstrations, limiting out-of-distribution (OOD) performance. For better generalization, rule-based inductive reasoning derives task-level regularities, which we term task-level inductive representations (TIR), before directly answering. However, existing methods lack generalizable inductive ability across diverse tasks. Inspired by meta-learning, we propose Cognitive-Decoupling meta-Induction (CoDI) for TIR-based inductive reasoning on general tasks. CoDI comprises a Cognitive-Decoupling Framework for query-blind TIR induction, and a Dual-Loop Meta-Inductive Learning method for cross-task inducer optimization. Empirically, CoDI achieves substantial performance gains on five ID and OOD benchmarks, and preserves OOD gains without explicit induction at inference. Code is available at https://github.com/Yuqian-Wang2277/codi-meta-induction.

A Calibrated Instrument for Detecting Disposition Drift in Language Models

Before language models are released, they are tested for harmful traits such as willingness to carry out dangerous requests, deception, power seeking, and sycophancy. The safe behavior these tests certify may degrade as models are modified, by topical fine-tuning, new training data, or changes to settings such as the depth of their reasoning. Most work reports such drift as single summary rates scored by another AI model, without the error analysis needed to interpret them. We adapt a calibrated measurement methodology recently developed for assessing how inference optimizations affect the quality of model outputs. Applying it to several misalignment dimensions in two open-weight models, we first establish the instrument's precision, showing that shifts of a few percentage points can be reliably detected. We then induce drift deliberately by fine-tuning on correct and on subtly harmful medical and financial advice; harmful advice induces a far greater shift than correct advice. Most strikingly, a model that reasons before answering can conceal the shift almost entirely in its answers, even though a direct reading of its internal state shows that its disposition has moved. With reasoning switched off, the same model shifts steadily as the share of harmful advice in its training grows. These results demonstrate the importance of testing safety with reasoning turned both on and off.

TimelineAgent: An LLM-based Framework for Query-Centric Event Timeline Extraction Across Multiple Documents

Query-centric cross-document event timeline extraction aims to construct event timelines from multiple documents under diverse query focuses. The task is very challenging due to the heterogeneous event modeling and reasoning requirements arising from both the intrinsic complexity of cross-document timeline construction and the diversity of query focuses. In this paper, we propose TimelineAgent, an LLM-based multi-agent framework featuring a multi-stage reasoning workflow, where heterogeneous event features are organized into reusable skills and orchestrated through a goal-oriented protocol. To facilitate evaluation, we construct a benchmark covering diverse query focuses. Experimental results show that TimelineAgent consistently outperforms existing methods, offering a practical framework for complex real-world timeline construction.

No Matter Who You Are: Addressivity and Sycophancy in Large Language Models

Sycophancy, where an LLM becomes overly agreeable, deferential, or flattering toward the user, has been identified as a major failure mode. While post-training has been implicated as a cause, how it internally gives rise to sycophantic behavior remains insufficiently understood. Post-training transforms the model's output from text completion into utterances directed toward an addressee, thereby giving the output \textit{addressivity}. We hypothesize that the accompanying pressure to follow the addressee's instructions and align with their preferences links this addressivity to sycophancy. To test this, we use Sparse Autoencoders to extract addressivity directions from pre-trained and post-trained models and examine their relationship with sycophancy. Steering models with addressivity directions can amplify or suppress sycophancy, and this effect is observed only in post-trained models. Furthermore, comparing the internal mechanisms of both model types confirms that the association between addressivity and sycophancy is strengthened through post-training. Finally, through a controlled post-training experiment, we find that models are sycophantic not toward a specific addressee but toward any recognized addressee, suggesting that the presence of an addressee, rather than the addressee's identity, is what elicits sycophancy.

Anatomy of Off-Policy Policy Gradient: Importance Sampling, KL Regularization, and Baselines

Reinforcement learning has become a central tool for large language model (LLM) post-training, where policy gradient methods are routinely deployed off-policy, even though vanilla policy gradient assumes on-policy sampling. We study when such off-policy deployment is justified, and what roles its core ingredients, including importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines, play in handling the resulting distribution shift. We show that for a general class of off-policy policy gradient updates, consistency, in the sense that the optimal policy remains a stationary point, requires one of two structures at the optimal policy: trajectory-level importance weighting, which suffers from the well-known curse of horizon, or KL regularization with a baseline equal to the optimal soft value, instantiated by the grouped mean. Focusing on the KL route, we identify it with the group-relative policy gradient (GRPG) update, establish an equivalence to a trajectory-level Bellman residual minimization objective, and obtain a finite-sample regret bound under all-policy coverage. This also gives a theoretical account of a variant of the empirically successful GRPO objective. We further uncover a hidden role of baselines in the off-policy setting: a constant shift in the baseline implements pessimism, the standard remedy in offline RL for partial data coverage. We instantiate this via the asymmetric REINFORCE objective, and connect it to the game-theoretic offline RL framework, showing a regret bound with single-policy coverage. Together, these results offer a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM post-training and connect it to classical ideas in offline RL.

Corrected Block Sparse Spectral Optimization: Exact Models, Finite Identification, and Local Dynamics

The block generalized-power method pairs threshold-based support selection with inexpensive Stiefel updates, but the standard block formulation conflates two nonequivalent objectives and leaves inactive components, threshold ties, and post-selection refitting ill-specified. We rebuild the model from the primal. Eliminating each loading column from the normalized loading-ball problem gives a sum of soft-thresholded Euclidean norms (A-SUM); the squared-threshold objective in common use arises from a quadratic lift (A-SQ). We prove both identities, exhibit a two-component instance whose maximizers disagree, and specify explicit conventions for zero-response columns, ties, rank deficiency, and mask-before-refit. For A-SQ we separate the historical full-polar iteration from a safeguarded Riemannian ascent and prove, for the latter, whole-sequence convergence via a sufficient-ascent lemma, a relative-error lemma, and the Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz property, which applies because the soft-threshold objective and the Stiefel constraint are semi-algebraic. A strict threshold margin then forces finite identification of the support and sign pattern. The post-identification map is ordinary power iteration on the active covariance for a rank-one hard threshold, and the Frechet derivative of a coupled polar map for component-specific supports; a radius-based safe-screening rule follows from the same local analysis. Planted-spike experiments match the predicted identification time and local rates. On modern-AI panels (GPT-2 residual stream, ViT CLS stream, Pitprops) the thresholded block-sparse components match dense PCA on reconstruction and improve on it in monosemanticity and class specialisation. On three native-label scRNA datasets (Zheng17 FACS-sorted PBMCs, 68k donor-A with FACS-correlated labels, Tabula Muris) A-SQ beats dense PCA on cluster-recovery AMI by +0.023 to +0.044 at matched sparsity.

Fine-Grained Units, Flat Decisions: Hierarchical Micro-Expert Pruning for Mixture-of-Experts Models

Micro-expert pruning reduces the capacity of mixture-of-experts (MoE) models by removing coupled channels within routed experts. However, finer pruning granularity alone does not guarantee the preservation of downstream capabilities, as similar language-modeling perplexity can accompany substantially different task performance. We revisit micro-expert mask construction as two distinct decisions: allocating retained capacity across experts and selecting channels within that capacity. We propose UC-RAMP, a two-stage post-training pruning framework that explicitly separates these decisions. CAPE uses cross-domain calibration evidence to redistribute expert widths under an exact layer-wise budget, while RAMP refines channel composition at fixed widths through one-out, one-in exchanges guided by a channel-interaction surrogate. Across three MoE models at 25% and 50% routed-expert channel pruning, UC-RAMP achieves the highest ten-task mean accuracy among evaluated pruning methods in five of six settings. At 50% pruning, it outperforms the strongest evaluated baseline by 8.19, 3.96, and 6.14 percentage points across the three models, respectively. In a single-A100 deployment at 25% pruning, UC-RAMP reduces measured time to first token by 19.7--23.9% and time per output token by 20.2--22.3% relative to the unpruned model. The code is available at https://github.com/xzktx003/UC_RAMP.

PFedQFT: Personalized Federated Quantum-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Medical Large Language Models

Medical data remain fragmented across institutional silos because of privacy, governance, and ownership constraints. Federated learning enables medical institutions to adapt large language models (LLMs) collaboratively without transferring raw records. However, independently parameterized low-rank adapters incur repeated parameter and communication costs across Transformer projections, while uniform aggregation may limit adaptation to heterogeneous institutional data. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.

Causal Humility: Governing the Authority of Fairness Claims Under Causal Uncertainty

AI systems increasingly shape consequential decisions in employment, lending, healthcare, and public services, making fairness an important problem for auditing and governance. Fairness assessment often requires more than identifying disparities in outcomes; it also requires understanding how those disparities arise. Causal approaches to algorithmic fairness address this explanatory problem by representing pathways through which protected attributes and other factors may influence outcomes. Yet causal reasoning in sociotechnical systems remains uncertain: relevant confounders may be unobserved, causal directions contested, and conclusions sensitive to modeling assumptions and representational choices.
  We introduce causal humility as a governance framework for managing the relationship between epistemic uncertainty and causal authority. Drawing on causal fairness research, epistemology, political philosophy, and science and technology studies, causal humility has four requirements: making consequential assumptions and omissions visible; preserving contestability across defensible causal accounts; recognizing indeterminacy as a legitimate analytical outcome; and maintaining proportionality between evidentiary support and institutional authority or action.
  We operationalize these requirements through Causal Cards, a documentation artifact for recording the causal basis, assumptions, omissions, model dependence, and governance implications of fairness claims. Using the UCI Adult dataset as a worked epistemic demonstration, we show how different causal discovery procedures applied to the same observational data render fairness-relevant pathways visible, absent, or unresolved. Causal humility treats such disagreement as information about the limits of what can responsibly be claimed and the authority those claims should carry.

Whose Credential Is It? How Owner Attribution in Agent MemoryShapes Cross-User Account Misuse

Shared long-term memory lets an LLM agent serving one user inadvertently retrieve another user's credentials, but it is unclear when such exposure leads to actual harm. We introduce a three-stage audit that separates three events: a foreign credential reaches the prompt (exposure), the agent acts with it (behavior), and the other user's account changes (state). In AppWorld, a store that mixes 763 past logins from all users retrieves another user's credential for 84.7 percent of required apps, yet agents rarely act on it. Misuse appears in a stress test where memory lacks the user's own credential and presents a single foreign one as the user's record: seven agents then use it in 80 to 94 percent of runs and change the victim's account in up to 43 percent, sometimes passing the official check. Naming the owner before or inside each credential line prevents most of this: with all else fixed, misuse falls from 92 to 1 of 305 paired runs, also with name-free usernames, though owner-bound notes cost 3 to 5 successful tasks per model on clean memory. A wrongly written owner largely restores misuse; trailing owner tags and added safety instructions help only some models. Agent memory should consistently record whose credential it stores, and evaluations should measure behavior, not only exposure.

From Black Box to Controller: Inference-Time Steering of LLM via Feature Amplification

Inference-time steering is crucial for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in real-world applications, where they must flexibly adjust behavior preferences. For instance, a model should dynamically adjust its caution level based on the risk of the scenario. This requirement introduces a challenge: modulating the intensity of LLM preferences. Prompt engineering can require task-specific prompt design, while fine-tuning requires additional optimization and data. These requirements can make repeated behavioral adjustments costly. To address this, we introduce NeuroSteer, a plug-and-play framework that enables flexible, quantitative adjustment of LLM preferences at inference time. NeuroSteer employs a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) as its controller. Comparing neuron activation frequencies between positive and negative examples isolates "feature neurons" that correspond to target preferences such as politeness and positive sentiment. During inference, the activations of selected SAE features are amplified in the latent representations to steer the output distribution. Users can adjust the strength of this steering on a per-scenario or per-user basis, granting the LLM fine-grained, quantifiable trait modulation. Our experiments show that NeuroSteer can alter sentiment, toxicity, stance, and politeness at inference time. It achieves higher target-direction steering scores than the compared baselines in several settings, with task- and strength-dependent trade-offs in generation quality.

CVaR-RMD: CVaR Selection Pressure for Robust Metaheuristic Discovery

Large language models have recently been used to discover heuristics through iterative program search, offering a promising approach to automated algorithm design. However, candidates selected by average performance may degrade across instance families, random seeds, and problem scales because mean-based objectives can obscure rare but severe failures. We propose CVaR-RMD, a risk-sensitive evolutionary framework that uses an LLM to mutate executable heuristic programs and ranks candidates by empirical conditional value-at-risk over heterogeneous evaluation conditions. Across three combinatorial optimization domains, CVaR-based selection improves upper-tail performance and reduces failure rates while maintaining competitive mean performance. These gains persist on larger unseen instances and independently curated within-domain benchmarks

ToolOPD: Credit-Aware On-Policy Distillation for Tool-Use Agents

On-policy distillation (OPD) enhances the capabilities of small language agents by providing dense, token-level supervision from a teacher model on student-generated trajectories. However, standard OPD implicitly assumes that credit is uniformly distributed across long-horizon trajectories, assigning the same strength of teacher supervision to different actions. Through credit-distribution analysis and outcome-reversal counterfactual experiments, we find that trajectory credit is concentrated on a small number of critical actions. Moreover, even when the trajectory is held fixed, reversing only the final outcome substantially changes the credit assigned to multiple interaction turns. These findings show that the uniform-credit assumption fails to identify critical decisions and ultimately degrades the model's reasoning accuracy. To address these challenges, we propose ToolOPD, an outcome-aware on-policy distillation framework designed for long-horizon tool use. ToolOPD first summarizes each completed trajectory and its outcome into a reusable hindsight skill. It then measures how this skill changes the teacher's probability of each student action token and converts the change into outcome-aware credit. The resulting credit preserves supervision that supports successful decisions or discourages failed ones, while suppressing irrelevant and conflicting signals. ToolOPD uses these credits to reweight the action-level distillation loss and jointly optimizes it with reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments across multiple long-horizon tool interaction benchmarks demonstrate that ToolOPD significantly outperforms existing reinforcement learning and self-distillation baselines.

Progress-Guided Preference Generation for Aligning Generative Control Policies

Generative policies have achieved strong performance in imitation learning tasks, but their success is often constrained by the limited availability of expert demonstrations. Although preference-based fine-tuning can leverage suboptimal rollouts to improve performance, existing approaches heavily rely on expensive human annotations. In this work, we introduce Progress-Guided Preference Generation (PGPG), an unsupervised preference alignment framework that eliminates the need for human-in-the-loop supervision. Our method leverages ordered coverage alignment to measure sequential progress relative to a small set of expert demonstrations, enabling the automatic extraction of pseudo-preference labels from autonomously collected rollouts. Furthermore, we introduce key enhancements to improve the reliability of these progress-guided preferences, enabling effective fine-tuning of pretrained generative policies without requiring manually designed reward functions. Extensive experiments across a suite of challenging long-horizon manipulation tasks demonstrate that PGPG outperforms standard supervised fine-tuning baselines. Moreover, our conceptually simple framework is broadly applicable across multiple classes of generative policies, demonstrating strong versatility when integrated with both diffusion and flow-matching policies.

MT-CC: Multi-Group Temperature Scaling for Asymmetric Calibration Behavior in Class-Incremental Learning

As deep neural networks are required to continuously adapt to evolving data, class-incremental learning (CIL) has become an active research area. However, most existing works primarily focus on improving accuracy while overlooking confidence calibration, which measures the trustworthiness of the model's predictions. Although temperature scaling (TS) is effective for static calibration, it has rarely been explored in continual settings where data distributions evolve over time. In this paper, we introduce Multi-Group Temperature Scaling for Continual Calibration (MT-CC), a novel post-hoc calibration framework that addresses the asymmetric calibration behavior in CIL, where the rate of confidence changes does not align with the rate of accuracy degradation across tasks, leading to different levels of miscalibration. Our key contribution is to design multiple temperature parameters through a lightweight grouping network that separates samples by confidence statistics and task-aware signals in CIL. Furthermore, a Groupwise Difference-between-Confidence-and-Accuracy (GDCA) regularization is incorporated to promote intra-group consistency. Experiments demonstrate that MT-CC significantly improves calibration performance while maintaining accuracy, offering a principled framework for reliable continual learning.

When Safety Fails Collectively: Robust Alignment Against Harmful Fine-Tuning via Joint Weight Perturbations

Harmful fine-tuning can rapidly erode the safety alignment of large language models, creating substantial risks when aligned models are adapted to downstream tasks. Existing alignment-stage defenses use harmful examples and simulated harmful perturbations during safety alignment to prepare models for later harmful fine-tuning, yet the collective effects of harmful weight perturbations remain underexplored. We identify a collective failure pattern: disjoint parts of a harmful perturbation have limited effects in isolation, but their joint application increases the model's harmfulness beyond the sum of their individual effects. Guided by this observation, we propose Joint Weight Perturbations (JWP), an alignment-stage defense that masks different parts of a harmful perturbation estimated from the current model and trains the model to respond safely in the resulting states. This exposes the model to different combinations of harmful perturbations and reduces its sensitivity to their accumulation during subsequent harmful fine-tuning. Experiments across downstream tasks and model families show that JWP offers stronger resistance to harmful fine-tuning than prior defenses while largely preserving task performance.

BitFlat: Error-Localized Bitstream Design for Robust Diffusion Image Compression

Zero-shot diffusion image compression based on reverse channel coding achieves high perceptual quality at low bitrates without retraining, yet its bitstream remains vulnerable to bit flips. Prior work has shown that a joint combinatorial representation can amplify a single bit flip into substitutions of multiple atoms and that indexing each atom independently confines this error propagation. However, independent indexing reduces the number of atoms that fit within the bit budget and gives the decoder no indication of which atoms are corrupted. To address these issues, we propose BitFlat, a training-free error-localizing bitstream framework with two components. Blockwise Locality Alignment (BLA) decomposes the globally coupled atom selection at each diffusion step into independent selection units, each describing one signed atom from a dedicated codebook with a fixed-width field, so that any bit error affects only the unit in which it occurs. Reliability-Aware Diffusion Control (RADC) uses the bits saved by BLA to impose structured valid-codeword constraints within each field and converts the decoding state of each field into a binary reliability gate, which turns detected corrupted atoms into erasures at known positions and excludes them from noise synthesis. BitFlat requires neither target residuals nor additional network forward passes and leaves the diffusion model unchanged. Experiments on Kodak, DIV2K, and CLIC2020 show that BitFlat outperforms conventional, learned, and diffusion-based codecs at bit error rates from $10^{-4}$ to $10^{-2}$ and, even with $26.7\%$ fewer bits, surpasses Robust Turbo-DDCM.

Cross-Pollination Retrieval: Finding Transferable Ideas Across Fields in Scientific Literature

Many scientific advances come from cross-pollination, carrying an idea from one field over to a problem in another. Yet retrieving papers across fields remains underexplored, with existing benchmarks confined to a narrow range of fields. We study cross-pollination retrieval, the problem of identifying transferable ideas across research fields, and introduce CPR-Dataset, a large-scale dataset constructed by mining cross-topic citations. Its evaluation split CPR-eval has 1,300 queries in 26 fields, and its training split CPR-train has 10,573 pairs. On CPR-eval, the best of eleven existing retrievers reaches a Recall@10 of only 0.26, whereas an LLM reranker reaches 0.81. Standard contrastive fine-tuning on CPR-train raises Recall@10 to as high as 0.70. The gain carries over to domains held out from training and to analogy and reasoning-intensive retrieval. On AnaloBench, it raises nDCG@10 by up to 33% over the untrained retriever, whereas training on three existing retrieval datasets lowers it. On BRIGHT, it raises nDCG@10 by up to 70%, more than twice the gain from those datasets. CPR-Dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/CPR-Dataset, and the code at https://github.com/SeokwonJung-Jay/Cross-Pollination-Retrieval.

Neither Over- nor Under-Constrained: Evaluating LLM Personalization under a Constraint Qualification Audit

Accurate evaluation of LLM personalization is hindered by current evaluation paradigms that over- or under-constrain the response space, yielding frequent false rejections and false acceptances. To reduce these false rates, we introduce ConQual-Audit, an audit-based framework that evaluates LLM personalization through a three-stage process: (1) extracting semantic constraints from user profiles and task-grounded reference; (2) qualifying constraints by pruning unnecessary constraints and recovering missing constraints; and (3) verifying candidate responses against the audited constraints via natural language inference. Across PrefEval and LaMP, ConQual-Audit outperforms nine baselines, attaining top AUCs (0.88 and 0.90) and the lowest false rate (0.21) on the leanest constraint sets at an amortizable audit cost. Crucially, this constraint audit is portable: integrating it into ExPerT and PREF helps revise smaller, higher-quality constraint sets to reduce their evaluation false rates.

LIRQuant: An Efficient Locally Isolated Rotation Quantization Method for Large Language Models

Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) enables efficient deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs), yet activation outliers remain a key challenge for low-bit quantization. Existing global transformation methods overlook heterogeneous channel sensitivity to quantization noise, leading to suboptimal accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. To address this, we propose \textbf{Locally Isolated Rotation Quantization (LIRQuant)}, a sensitivity-aware framework that selectively applies transformations. LIRQuant identifies sensitive channels using a noise-weighted sensitivity metric, applying expressive learnable rotations only to these channels and lightweight fixed rotations to the remaining ones. This decoupled design reduces optimization complexity without sacrificing outlier smoothing capability. Extensive experiments across the LLaMA-2/3, Mistral, Qwen3, and Mixtral families demonstrate that LIRQuant achieves state-of-the-art accuracy under the challenging W4A4KV4 setting. Meanwhile, LIRQuant calibrates LLaMA-3-8B in only 25 minutes with 20.3 GB peak GPU memory, achieving the fastest calibration and the lowest memory footprint among recent learnable-rotation methods, and its fused inference kernels deliver up to $2.3\times$ prefill speedup and $3.5\times$ memory saving over FP16.
